      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">lorence came back for her promptly at seven with an</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">oil lamp in her hand to light the way. They went</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">down the stairs to a dining room weighed down by a</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">monstrous chandelier, much like the one in the hall- way entrance, which remained unlit. There was a table big enough for a dozen people, with the appropriate tablecloth of white damask. Candelabra had been set on it. The long, white, tapered candles reminded Noemi of church.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The walls were lined with china cabinets crammed with lace, porcelain, and most of all with silver. Cups and plates bearing the proud initial of their owners—the triumphant, stylized D of the Doyles—serving trays and empty vases, which might have once gleamed under the glow of the candles and now looked tarnished and dull.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Florence pointed to a chair, and Noemi sat down. Francis was already seated across from her and Florence took her place at his side. A gray-haired maid walked in and placed bowls filled with a watery soup in front of them. Florence and Fran- cis began to eat.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Will no one else be joining us?” she asked.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Your cousin is asleep. Uncle Howard and Cousin Virgil may come down, perhaps later,” Florence said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi arranged a napkin on her lap. She had soup, but only a little. She was not used to eating at this hour. Nights were no time for heavy meals; at home they had pastries and coffee with milk. She wondered how she'd fare with a differ- ent schedule. A l’anglaise, like their French teacher used to say. La panure a l’anglaise, repeat after me. Would they have four o'clock tea, or was it five o’clock?</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The plates were taken away in silence, and in silence there came the main dish, chicken in an unappealing creamy white sauce with mushrooms. The wine they’d poured her was very dark and sweet. She didn’t like it.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_34" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 34 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164003_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi pushed the mushrooms around her plate with her fork while trying to see what lay in the gloomy cabinets across from her.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“It’s mostly silver objects in here, isn’t it?” she said. “Did all of these come from your mine?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Francis nodded. “Yes, back in the day.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Why did it close?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“There were strikes and then—” Francis began to say, but his mother immediately raised her head and stared at Noemi.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“We do not talk during dinner.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Not even to say ‘pass the salt’?” Noemi asked lightly, twirl- ing her fork.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I can see you think yourself terribly amusing. We do not talk during dinner. That is the way it is. We appreciate the si- lence in this house.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Come, Florence, surely we can make a bit of conversation. For the sake of our guest,” said a man in a dark suit as he walked into the room, leaning on Virgil.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Old would have been an inaccurate word to describe him. He was ancient, his face gouged with wrinkles, a few sparse hairs stubbornly attached to his skull. He was very pale too, like an underground creature. A slug, perhaps. His veins con- trasted with his pallor, thin, spidery lines of purple and blue.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi watched him shuffle toward the head of the table and sit down. Virgil sat too, by his father’s right, his chair at such an angle that he remained half enveloped in shadows.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The maid didn’t bring a plate for the old man, only a glass of dark wine. Maybe he’d already eaten and had ventured downstairs for her sake.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Sir, I’m Noemi Taboada. It’s nice to meet you,” she said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“And I am Howard Doyle, Virgil’s father. Although you’ve guessed that already.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The old man wore an old-fashioned cravat, his neck hidden under a mound of fabric, a circular silver pin upon it as a dec- oration, a large amber ring on his index finger. He fixed his eyes on her. The rest of him was bleached of color, but the eyes were of a startling blue, unimpeded by cataracts and un- dimmed by age. The eyes burned coldly in that ancient face and commanded her attention, vivisecting the young woman with his gaze.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You are much darker than your cousin, Miss Taboada,” Howard said after he had completed his examination of her.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Pardon me?” she asked, thinking she’d heard him wrong.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He pointed at her. “Both your coloration and your hair. They are much darker than Catalina’s. I imagine they reflect</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_35" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 35 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164008_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">your Indian heritage rather than the French. You do have some Indian in you, no? Like most of the mestizos here do.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Catalina’s mother was from France. My father is from Veracruz and my mother from Oaxaca. We are Mazatec on her side. What is your point?” she asked flatly.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The old man smiled. A closed smile, no teeth. She could pic- ture his teeth, yellowed and broken.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Virgil had motioned to the maid, and a glass of wine was placed before him. The others had resumed their silent eating. This was to be, then, a conversation between two parties.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Merely an observation. Now tell me, Miss Taboada, do you believe as Mr. Vasconcelos does that it is the obligation, no, the destiny, of the people of Mexico to forge a new race that en- compasses all races? A ‘cosmic’ race? A bronze race? This des- pite the research of Davenport and Steggerda?” ~~" “You mean their work in Jamaica?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Splendid, Catalina was correct. You do have an interest in anthropology.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Yes,” she said. She did not wish to share more than that single word.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“What are your thoughts on the intermingling of superior and inferior types?” he asked, ignoring her discomfort.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi felt the eyes of all the family members on her. Her presence was a novelty and an alteration to their patterns. An organism introduced into a sterile environment. They waited to hear what she revealed and to analyze her words. Well, let them see that she could keep her cool.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She had experience dealing with irritating men. They did not fluster her. She had learned, by navigating cocktail parties and meals at restaurants, that showing any kind of reaction to their crude remarks emboldened them.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I once read a paper by Gamio in which he said that harsh natural selection has allowed the indigenous people of this continent to survive, and Europeans would benefit from inter- mingling with them,” she said, touching her fork and feeling the cold metal under her fingertips. “It turns the whole super- ior and inferior idea around, doesn’t it?” she asked, the ques- tion sounding innocent and yet a little bit mordant.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The elder Doyle seemed pleased with this answer, his face growing animated. “Do not be upset with me, Miss Taboada. I do not mean to insult you. Your countryman, Vasconcelos, he speaks of the mysteries of ‘aesthetic taste’ which will help shape this bronze race, and I think you are a good example of that sort.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Of what sort?”</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_36" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 36 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164013_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He smiled again, this time his teeth visible, the lips drawn. The teeth were not yellow as she’d imagined, but porcelain- white and whole. But the gums, which she could see clearly, were a noxious shade of purple.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Of a new beauty, Miss Taboada. Mr. Vasconcelos makes it very clear that the unattractive will not procreate. Beauty at- tracts beauty and begets beauty. It is a means of selection. You see, lam offering you a compliment.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“That is a very strange compliment,” she managed to say, swallowing her disgust.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You should take it, Miss Taboada. I don’t hand them out lightly. Now, I am tired. I will retire, but do not doubt this has been an invigorating conversation. Francis, help me up.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The younger man assisted the waxwork and they left the room. Florence drank from her wine, the slim stem carefully lifted and pressed against her lips. The oppressive silence had settled upon them again. Noemi thought that if she paid at- tention, she would be able to hear everyone’s hearts beating.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She wondered how Catalina could bear living in this place. Catalina had always been so sweet, always the nurturer watch- ing over the younger ones, a smile on her lips. Did they really make her sit at this table in utter silence, the curtains drawn, the candles offering their meager light? Did that old man try to engage her in obnoxious conversations? Had Catalina ever been reduced to tears? At their dining room table in Mexico City her father liked to tell riddles and offer prizes to the child who piped up with the correct answer.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The maid came by to take away the dishes. Virgil, who had not properly acknowledged Noemi, finally looked at her, their eyes meeting. “I imagine you have questions for me.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Yes,” she said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Let’s go to the sitting room.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He grabbed one of the silver candelabra on the table and walked her down a hallway and into a large chamber with an equally enormous fireplace and a black walnut mantel carved with the shapes of flowers. Above the fireplace hung a still life of fruits, roses, and delicate vines. A couple of kerosene lamps atop twin ebony tables provided further illumination.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Two matching faded green velour settees were arranged at one end of the room, and next to them there were three chairs covered with antimacassars. White vases collected dust, indi- cating that this space had once been used to receive visitors and supply merriment.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Virgil opened the doors of a sideboard with silver hinges and a marble serving surface. He took out a decanter with</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_37" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 37 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164017_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">a curious stopper shaped like a flower and filled two glasses, handing her one. Then he sat on one of the stately, stiff, gold brocade armchairs set by the fireplace. She followed suit.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Since this room was well illuminated, she was presented with a better picture of the man. They had met during Cata- lina’s wedding, but it had all been very quick and a year had passed. She had not been able to recall what he looked like. He was fair-haired, blue-eyed like his father, and his coolly sculpted face was burnished with imperiousness. His double- breasted lounge suit was sleek, charcoal gray with a herring- bone pattern, very proper, though he’d eschewed a tie, and the top button of his shirt was undone as if he were trying to imi- tate a casualness it was impossible for him to possess.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She was not sure how she should address him. Boys her age were easy to flatter. But he was older than she was. She must be more serious, temper her natural flirtatiousness lest he think her silly. He had the stamp of authority here, but she also had authority. She was an envoy.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The Kublai Khan sent messengers across his realm who carried a stone with his seal, and whoever mistreated a mes- senger would be put to death. Catalina had told her this story, narrating fables and history for Noemi.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Let Virgil understand, then, that Noemi had an invisible stone in her pocket.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“It was good of you to come on such short notice,” Virgil said, though his tone was flat. Courtesy, but no warmth.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I had to.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Did you really?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“My father was concerned,” she said. There was her stone, even as his own badge was all around him, in this house and its things. Noemi was a Taboada, sent by Leocadio Taboada himself.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“As I tried to tell him, there is no need for alarm.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Catalina said she had tuberculosis. But I don’t think that quite explains her letter.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Did you see the letter? What did it say exactly?” he asked, leaning forward. His tone was still flat, but he looked alert.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I did not consign it to memory. Enough that he asked me to visit you.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“T see.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He turned his glass between his hands, the fire making it glint and sparkle. He leaned back against the chair. He was handsome. Like a sculpture. His face, rather than skin and bone, might have been a death mask.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_38" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 38 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164022_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Catalina was not well. She ran a very high fever. She sent that letter in the midst of her sickness.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Who is treating her?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Pardon me?” he replied.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Someone must be treating her. Florence, is she your cousin?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Yes.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Well, your cousin Florence gives her medicine. There must be a doctor.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He stood up and grabbed a fireplace poker, stirring the burning logs. A spark flew through the air and landed on atile dirty with age, acrack running down its middle.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“There is a doctor. His name is Arthur Cummins. He has been our physician for many years. We completely trust Dr. Cummins.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Doesn’t he think her behavior has been unusual, even with tuberculosis?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Virgil smirked. “Unusual. You have medical knowledge?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“No. But my father did not send me here because he thought everything was as usual.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“No, your father wrote about psychiatrists at the first pos- sible opportunity. It’s the thing he writes about, over and over again,” Virgil said scornfully. It irritated her to hear him speak- ing in such a way about her father, as though he were terrible and unfair.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I will speak to Catalina’s doctor,” Noemi replied, perhaps more forcefully than she should have, for at once he returned the poker to its stand with a quick and harsh movement of his arm.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Demanding, are we?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“IT wouldn’t say demanding, exactly. Concerned, more like it,” she replied, taking care to smile, to show him this was really a small matter that might be easily resolved, and it must have worked, for he nodded.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Arthur comes by every week. He'll stop by Thursday to see Catalina and my father.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Your father is also ill?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“My father is old. He has the aches that time bestows on all men. If you can wait until then, you may speak to Arthur.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“T have no intention of leaving yet.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Tell me, how long do you expect to remain with us?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Not too long, I hope. Enough to figure out if Catalina needs me. I’m sure I could find lodging in town if I’m too much of a nuisance.”</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_39" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 39 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164026_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“It’s a very small town. There’s no hotel, not even a guest- house. No, you can remain here. I’m not trying to run you out. I wish you'd come for another reason, I suppose.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She had not thought there would be a hotel, although she would have been glad to discover one. The house was dreary, and so was everyone in it. She could believe a woman could sicken quickly in a place like this.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She sipped her wine. It was the same dark vintage she’d had in the dining room, sweet and strong.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Is your room satisfactory?” Virgil asked, his tone warm- ing, turning a bit more cordial. She was, perhaps, not his enemy.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“It’s fine. Having no electricity is odd, but I don’t think any- one has died from a lack of light bulbs yet.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Catalina thinks the candlelight is romantic.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi supposed she would. It was the kind of thing she could imagine impressing her cousin: an old house atop a hill, with mist and moonlight, like an etching out of a Gothic novel. Wuthering Heights and Jane Eyre, those were Catalina’s sort of books. Moors and spiderwebs. Castles too, and wicked stepmothers who force princesses to eat poisoned apples, dark fairies cursing maidens and wizards who turn handsome lords into beasts. Noemi preferred to jump from party to party on a weekend and drive a convertible.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">So maybe, in the end, this house suited Catalina fine. Could it be it had been a bit of a fever? Noemi held her glass between her hands, running her thumb down its side.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Let me pour you another glass,” Virgil said, playing the role of the attentive host.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">It could grow on you, this drink. Already it had lulled her into a half sleep, and she blinked when he spoke. His hand brushed hers as he made a gesture to refill her glass, but she shook her head. She knew her limits, traced them firmly.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“No, thanks,” she said, setting the glass aside and rising from the chair, which had proven more comfortable than she might have guessed.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I shall insist.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She shook her head prettily, defusing the denial with that tried and proven trick. “Heavens, no. I will decline and wrap myself in a blanket and go to bed.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">His face was still remote, yet now seemed infused with more vitality as he surveyed her very carefully. There was a spark in his eye. He’d found an item of interest; one of her gestures or words struck him as novel. She thought it was her</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_40" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 40 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164031_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">refusal that amused him. He was, likely, not used to being re- fused. But then, many men were the same.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I can walk you to your room,” he offered, smooth and gallant.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">They went up the stairs, him holding an oil lamp hand- painted with patterns of vines, which made the light emanat- ing from it turn emerald and infused the walls with a strange hue: it painted the velvet curtains green. In one or other of her stories Catalina had told her the Kublai Khan executed his en- emies by smothering them with velvet pillows so there would be no blood. She thought this house, with all its fabrics and rugs and tassels, could smother a whole army.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_41" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 41 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164036_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">reakfast was brought to her on a tray. Thank good-</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">ness she did not have to sit down to eat with the</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">whole family that morning, although who knew</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">what dinner might bring. The chance for solitude made the porridge, toast, and jam she had been served a bit more appetizing. The drink available was tea, which she dis- liked. She was a coffee drinker, preferred it black, and this tea had a definite, faint, fruity scent to it.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">After a shower, Noemi applied lipstick and lined her eyes with a little black pencil. She knew her large, dark eyes and her generous lips were her greatest assets, and she used them to excellent effect. She took her time going through her clothes and picked a purple acetate taffeta dress with a full, pleated skirt. It was too fine to be worn as a day dress—she had rung in 1950 in a similar outfit eight months before—but then she tended toward opulence. Besides, she wanted to defy the gloom around her. She decided that this way her exploration of the house would be more entertaining.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">There certainly was a lot of gloom. Daylight did not im- prove High Place. When she walked the ground floor and opened a couple of creaky doors she was inevitably greeted by the ghostly sight of furniture covered with white sheets and draperies shut tight. Wherever the odd ray of sun slipped into a room, one could see dust motes dancing in the air. In the hallways, for every electrified sconce with a bulb there were three that were bare. It was obvious most of the house was not in use.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She had assumed the Doyles would have a piano, even if it was out of tune, but there was none, and neither could she find a radio or even an old gramophone. And how she loved music. Anything from Lara to Ravel. Dancing too. What a pity that she’d be left without music.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She wandered into a library. A narrow wooden frieze with a repeating pattern of acanthus leaves, divided by pilasters,</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_42" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 42 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164041_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">encircled the room, which was lined with tall, built-in book- cases stuffed with leather-bound volumes. She reached out for a book at random and opened it to see it had been ravaged by mold and was perfumed with the sweet scent of rot. She clapped the book shut and returned it to its place.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The shelves also contained issues of old magazines, includ- ing Eugenics: A Journal of Race Betterment and the American Journal of Eugenics.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">How appropriate, she thought, remembering Howard Doyle’s inane questions. She wondered if he kept a pair of cali- pers to measure his guests’ skulls.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">There was a terrestrial globe with countries’ names out of date in a lonesome corner and a marble bust of Shakespeare by a window. A large, circular rug had been placed in the middle of the room, and when she looked down at it she realized it showed the image of a black serpent biting its tail against a crimson background, with tiny flowers and vines all around it.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">This was probably one of the best maintained rooms in the house—certainly one of the most used, judging by the lack of dust—and still it seemed a tad frayed, its curtains faded into an ugly green, more than a few books blighted by mildew.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">A door at the other end of the library connected with a large office. Inside it, the heads of three stags had been mounted on a wall. An empty rifle cabinet with cut-glass doors was set in a corner. Somebody had hunted and given it up. Atop a desk of black walnut she found more journals of eu- genics research. A page was marked in one of them. She read it.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The idea that the half-breed mestizo of Mexico inherits the worst traits of their progenitors is incorrect. If the stamp of an inferior race afflicts them, it is due to a lack of proper social models. Their impulsive temperament requires early restraint. Nevertheless, the mestizo possess many inherent splendid attributes, including a robustness of body...</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She no longer wondered if Howard Doyle had a pair of cali- pers; now she wondered how many he kept. Maybe they were in one of the tall cabinets behind her, along with the family’s pedigree chart. There was a trash can next to the desk, and Noemi slid the journal she had been reading into the can.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi went in search of the kitchen, having been in- formed of its location by Florence the previous day. The kit- chen was ill lit, its windows narrow, the paint on its walls peel- ing. Two people sat on along bench, a wrinkled woman and a man who, though noticeably younger, still sported gray in his</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_43" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 43 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164045_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">hair. He was fifty-something, surely, and she probably closer to seventy. They were using a round brush to clean the dirt off mushrooms. When Noemi walked in, they raised their heads but did not greet her.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Good morning,” she said. “We weren’t introduced properly yesterday. I’m Noemi.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Both of them stared at her mutely. A door opened and a woman, also gray-haired, walked into the kitchen carrying a bucket. She recognized her as the maid who had served them during dinner, and she was of an age with the man. The maid did not speak to Noemi either, nodding instead, and then the couple who were seated on the bench nodded too before pla- cing their attention back on their work. Did everyone follow this policy of silence in High Place?</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“ym”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“We're working,” the man said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The three servants then looked down, their wan faces in- different to the presence of the colorful socialite. Perhaps Vir- gil or Florence had informed them Noemi was someone of no importance and that they should not trouble themselves with her.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi bit her lip and stepped outside the house using the back door the maid had opened. There was mist, like the previ- ous day, and a chill to the air. Now she regretted not wearing a more comfortable outfit, a dress with pockets where she could carry her cigarettes and her lighter. Noemi adjusted the red rebozo around her shoulders.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Did you have a good breakfast?” Francis asked, and she turned around to look at him. He’d also come out through the kitchen door, wrapped in a snug sweater.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Yes, it was fine. How’s your day going?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Tt’s all right.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“What is that?” she asked, pointing at a nearby wooden structure, made hazy by the mist.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“That’s the shed where we keep the generator and the fuel. Behind it is the coach house. Do you want to take a look at it? Maybe also go to the cemetery?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Sure.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The coach house seemed like a place that might have a hearse and two black horses inside, but instead there were two cars. One was the luxurious older vehicle that Francis had driven; the other was a newer but much more modest-looking car. A path snaked around the coach house, and they followed it through the trees and the mist until they reached a pair of</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_44" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 44 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164050_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">iron gates decorated with the motif of a serpent eating its tail like the one she’d seen in the library.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">They walked down a shady path, the trees so close together only a smattering of light made it through the branches. She could picture this same graveyard once upon a time in a tidier state, with carefully tended shrubs and flower beds, but now it was a realm of weeds and tall grasses, the vegetation threatening to swallow the place whole. The tombstones were blanketed with moss, and mushrooms sprouted by the graves. It was a picture of melancholy. Even the trees seemed lugubri- ous, though Noemi could not say why. Trees were trees.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">It was the sum of it, she thought, and not the individual parts that made the English cemetery so sad. Neglect was one thing, but neglect and the shadows cast by the trees and the weeds clustered by the tombstones, the chill in the air, served to turn what would have been an ordinary collection of vege- tation and tombstones into a fiercely displeasing picture.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She felt sorry for every single person buried there, just as she felt sorry for everyone living at High Place. Noemi bent down to look at a headstone, then another, and frowned.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Why are all these from 1888?” she asked.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“The nearby mine was managed by Spaniards until Mex- ico’s independence and left alone for many decades because nobody believed much silver could be extracted. But my great uncle Howard thought differently,’ Francis explained. “He brought modern English machines and a large English crew to do the work. He was successful, but a couple of years after re- opening the mine there was an epidemic. It killed most of the English workers, and they were buried here.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“And then? What did he do after? Did he send for more workers from England?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Ah...no, no need...he always had Mexican workers too, a large contingent of them...but they’re not all buried here. I be- lieve they’re in El Triunfo. Uncle Howard would know better.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">A rather exclusive spot, then, though Noemi supposed it was for the best. The families of local crew members prob- ably wanted to visit their loved ones, to leave flowers on their graves, which would have been impossible in this place, iso- lated from the town.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">They walked onward until Noemi paused before a marble statue of a woman standing on a pedestal, flower wreaths in her hair. She flanked the doorway to a mausoleum with a pedi- mented doorway, her right hand pointing at its entrance. The name Doyle was carved in capital letters above this doorway along witha phrase in Latin: Et Verbum caro factum est.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_45" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 45 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164055_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Who's this?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“The statue is supposed to be the likeness of my great aunt Agnes, who died during the epidemic. And here, the Doyles are all buried here: my great aunt, my grandfather and grand- mother, my cousins,” he said, trailing off, dipping into an un- comfortable silence.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The silence, not only of the cemetery but of the whole house, unnerved Noemi. She was used to the rumble of the tram and the automobiles, the sound of canaries chirping in the inner courtyard by the gleeful fountain, the barking of the dogs and the melodies pouring from the radio as the cook hummed by the stove.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“It’s so quiet here,” she said and shook her head. “I don’t like it.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“What do you like?” he asked, curious.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Mesoamerican artifacts, zapote ice cream, Pedro Infante’s movies, music, dancing, and driving,” she said, counting a fin- ger as she listed each item. She also liked to banter, but she was certain he could figure that out on his own.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I’m afraid I can’t be much help with that. What kind of car do you drive?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“The prettiest Buick you’ve ever seen. A convertible, of course.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Of course?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“It’s more fun driving without the hood on. It makes your hair look movie-star perfect. Also, it gives you ideas, you think better,” she said, running a hand through her wavy hair jok- ingly. Noemi’s father said she cared too much about her looks and parties to take school seriously, as if a woman could not do twothingsatonce. "what kind of ideas?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Ideas for my thesis, when I get to it,” she said. “Ideas about what to do on the weekend, anything really. I do my best thinking when I’m in motion.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Francis had been looking at her, but now he lowered his eyes. “You're very different from your cousin,” he told her.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Are you also going to tell me I lean toward a ‘darker’ type, both my hair and coloration?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“No,” he said. “I didn’t mean physically.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Then?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I think you're charming.” A panicked look contorted his face. “Not that your cousin lacks charm. You are charming ina special way,” he said quickly.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">If you’d seen Catalina before, she thought. If he’d seen her in the city with a pretty velvet dress, going from one side of the</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_46" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 46 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164059_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">room to the other, that gentle smile on her lips and her eyes full of stars. But here, in that musty room, with those eyes dimmed and whatever sickness had taken hold of her body... but then, perhaps it wasn’t that bad. Perhaps before the illness Catalina still smiled her sweet smile and took her husband by the hand, guiding him outside to count the stars.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You say that because you haven’t met my mother,” Noemi replied lightly, not wishing to voice her thoughts on Catalina. “She is the most charming woman on Earth. In her presence I feel rather tacky and unremarkable.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He nodded. “I know what that is like. Virgil is the family’s heir, the shining promise of the Doyles.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You envy him?” she asked.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Francis was very thin; his face was that of a plaster saint haunted by his impending martyrdom. The dark circles under his eyes, almost like bruises against that pale skin, made her suspect a hidden ailment. Virgil Doyle on the other hand had been carved from marble: he exuded strength where Francis irradiated weakness, and Virgil’s features—the eyebrows, the cheekbones, the full mouth—were bolder, entirely more at- tractive.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She could not judge Francis ill if he wished for that same vitality.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I don’t envy him his ease with words or his looks or his position, I envy his ability to go places. The farthest I’ve ever been is El Triunfo. That’s it. He’s traveled a bit. Not for long, he’s always quick to return, but it’s a respite.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">There was no bitterness in Francis’s words, only a tired sort of resignation as he continued speaking. “When my father was still alive he’d take me to town and I’d stare at the train station. I’d try to sneak in to look at the sign with the depart- ure times.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi adjusted her rebozo, trying to find warmth in its folds, but the cemetery was terribly damp and chilly; she could almost swear the temperature had dropped a couple of degrees the more they’d pressed into it. She shivered, and he noticed.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“T’m so stupid,” Francis said, removing his sweater. “Here, have this.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“It’s fine. Really, I couldn’t let you freeze for my sake. Maybe if we start walking back I'll be better.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Well, fine, but please, wear it. I swear I won't be cold.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She put on the sweater and wrapped the rebozo around her head. She thought he might pick up the pace since she was</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_47" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 47 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164104_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">now walking in his sweater, but he didn’t rush back home. He was probably used to the mist, the shady chill of the trees.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Yesterday you asked about the silver items in the house. You were right, they came from our mine,” he told her.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“It’s been closed for a long time, hasn’t it?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Catalina had said something about that; it was why Noemi’s father had not been keen on the match. Virgil seemed to him a stranger, maybe a fortune hunter. Noemi suspected he’d let Catalina marry him because he felt guilty about driv- ing away her previous suitor: Catalina had loved him truly.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“It happened during the Revolution. That’s when a host of things happened, one thing led to another and operations ceased. The year Virgil was born, 1915, that was the absolute end of it. The mines were flooded.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Then he is thirty-five,” she said. “And you are much younger.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Ten years younger,” Francis said with a nod. “A bit of an age gap, but he was the one friend I had growing up.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“But you must have gone to school eventually.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“We were schooled at High Place.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi tried to think of the house filled with the noise of children’s laughter, children playing hide and seek, children with a spinning top or a ball between their hands. But she couldn’t. The house would have not allowed such a thing. The house would have demanded they spring from it fully grown.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Can I ask you a question?” she said, when they were rounding the coach house and High Place was visible, the cur- tain of mist having parted. “Why the insistence on silence at the dining table?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“My great uncle Howard, he’s very old, very delicate, and very sensitive to noises. And the sound travels easily in the house.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Is his room upstairs? He can’t possibly hear people talking in the dining room.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Noises carry,” Francis said, his face serious, his eyes fixed on the old house. “Anyway, it’s his house and he sets the rules.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“And you never bend them.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He glanced down at her, looking a little perplexed, as if it had not occurred to him until now that this was a possibility. She was certain he’d never drunk too much, stayed out far too late, nor blurted the wrong opinion in his family’s company.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“No,” he said, once again with that resigned note in his voice.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">When they walked into the kitchen, she took off the sweater and handed it back to him. There was one maid now,</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_48" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 48 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164109_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">the slightly younger maid, sitting by the stove. She did not look at them, too occupied with her chores to spare them a sin- gle glance.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“No, you should keep it,” Francis said, ever polite. “It’s ra- ther warm.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I can’t be stealing your clothes.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“T have other sweaters,” he said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Thanks.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He smiled at her. Florence walked into the room, again decked in a dark navy dress, her face severe, glancing at Fran- cis and then at Noemi, as if they were small children and she was trying to determine whether they had scarfed down a for- bidden box of sweets. “If yow’ll come with me for your lunch,” she said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">This time it was the three of them at the table; the old man did not materialize and neither did Virgil. The lunch was con- ducted quickly, and after the dishes were cleared Noemi went back to her room. They brought up a tray with her dinner, so she supposed the dining room had been just for the first night and the lunch was also an anomaly. With her tray they also brought her an oil lamp, which she set by the bedside. She tried to read the copy of Witchcraft, Oracles, and Magic Among the Azande, which she’d brought with her, but kept getting dis- tracted. Noises did carry, she thought, as she focused on the creaking of floorboards.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">In a corner of her room there was a bit of mold upon the wallpaper that caught her eye. She thought of those green wallpapers so beloved by the Victorians that contained ar- senic. The so-called Paris and Scheele greens. And wasn’t there something in a book she’d read once about how microscopic fungi could act upon the dyes in the paper and form arsine gas, sickening the people in the room?</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She was certain she’d heard about how these most civilized Victorians had been killing themselves in this way, the fungi chomping on the paste in the wall, causing unseen chemical reactions. She couldn’t remember the name of the fungus that had been the culprit—Latin names danced at the tip of her tongue, brevicaule—but she thought she had the facts right. Her grandfather had been a chemist and her father’s business was the production of pigments and dyes, so she knew to mix zinc sulfide and barium sulfate if you wanted to make litho- pone and a myriad of other bits of information.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Well, the wallpaper was not green. Not even close to green; it was a muted pink, the color of faded roses, with ugly yellow medallions running across it. Medallions or circles; when you</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_49" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 49 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164113_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">looked at it closely you might think they were wreaths. She might have preferred the green wallpaper. This was hideous, and when she closed her eyes, the yellow circles danced be- hind her eyelids, flickers of color against black.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_50" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 50 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164118_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">atalina sat by the window again that morning. She</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">seemed remote, like the last time Noemi had seen</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">her. Noemi thought of a drawing of Ophelia that</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">used to hang in their house. Ophelia dragged by the current, glimpsed through a wall of reeds. This was Catalina that morning. Yet it was good to see her, to sit together and update her cousin on the people and things in Mexico City. She detailed an exhibit she had been to three weeks prior, knowing Catalina would be interested in such things, and then imitated a couple of friends of theirs with such accuracy a smile formed on her cousin’s lips, and Catalina laughed.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You are so good when you do impressions. Tell me, are you still bent on those theater classes?” Catalina asked.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“No. I have been thinking about anthropology. A master’s degree. Doesn’t that sound interesting?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Always with a new idea, Noemi. Always a new pursuit.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She’d heard such a refrain often. She supposed that her family was right to view her university studies skeptically, seeing as she’d changed her mind already thrice about where her interests lay, but she knew rather fiercely that she wanted to do something special with her life. She hadn’t found what exactly that would be, although anthropology appeared to her more promising than previous explorations.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Anyway, when Catalina spoke, Noemi didn’t mind, because her words never sounded like her parents’ reproaches. Cata- lina was a creature of sighs and phrases as delicate as lace. Catalina was a dreamer and therefore believed in Noemi’s dreams.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“And you, what have you been up to? Don’t think I haven’t noticed you hardly write. Have you been pretending you live on a windswept moor, like in Wuthering Heights?” Noemi asked. Catalina had worn out the pages of that book.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“No. It’s the house. The house takes most of my time,” Cata- lina said, extending a hand and touching the velvet draperies.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_51" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 51 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164123_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Were you planning on renovating it? I wouldn’t blame you if you razed it and built it anew. It’s rather ghastly, isn’t it? And chilly too.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Damp. There’s a dampness to it.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I was too busy freezing to death last night to mind the dampness.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“The darkness and the damp. It’s always damp and dark and so very cold.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">As Catalina spoke, the smile on her lips died. Her eyes, which had been distant, suddenly fell on Noemi with the sharpness of a blade. She clutched Noemi’s hands and leaned forward, speaking low.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I need you to do a favor for me, but you can’t tell anyone about it. You must promise you won't tell. Promise?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I promise.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“There’s a woman in town. Her name is Marta Duval. She made a batch of medicine for me, but I’ve run out of it. You must go to her and get more. Do you understand?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Yes, of course. What kind of medicine is it>”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“It doesn’t matter. What matters is that you do it. Will you? Please say you will and tell no one about it.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Yes, if you want me to.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Catalina nodded. She was clutching Noemi’s hands so tightly that her nails were digging into the soft flesh of her wrists.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Catalina, I’ll speak to—”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Shush. They can hear you,” Catalina said and went quiet, her eyes bright as polished stones.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Who can hear me?” Noemi asked slowly, as her cousin’s eyes fixed on her, unblinking.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Catalina slowly leaned closer to her, whispering in her ear. “Tt’s in the walls,” she said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“What is?” Noemi asked, and the question was a reflex, for she found it hard to think what to ask with her cousin’s blank eyes upon her, eyes that did not seem to see; it was like staring into a sleepwalker’s face.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“The walls speak to me. They tell me secrets. Don’t listen to them, press your hands against your ears, Noemi. There are ghosts. They’re real. You'll see them eventually.” ~ Abruptly Catalina released her cousin and stood up, grip- ping the curtain with her right hand and staring out the win- dow. Noemi wanted to ask her to explain herself, but Florence walked in then.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Dr. Cummins has arrived. He needs to examine Catalina and will meet you in the sitting room later,” the woman said.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_52" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 52 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164128_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I don’t mind staying,” Noemi replied.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“But he’ll mind,” Florence told her with a definite finality. Noemi could have pressed the point, but she elected to leave rather than get into an argument. She knew when to back down, and she could sense that insisting now would result ina hostile refusal. They might even send her packing if she made a fuss. She was a guest, but she knew herself to be an incon- venient one.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The sitting room, in the daytime, once she peeled the cur- tains aside, seemed much less welcoming than at night. For one it was chilly, the fire that had warmed the room turned to ashes, and with daylight streaming through the windows every imperfection was laid bare more strikingly. The faded velour settees appeared a sickly green, almost bilious, and there were many cracks running down the enamel tiles decor- ating the fireplace. A little oil painting, showing a mushroom from different angles, had been attacked, ironically, by mold: tiny black spots marred its colors and defaced the image. Her cousin was right about the dampness.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi rubbed her wrists, looking at the place where Cata- lina had dug her nails against her skin, and waited for the doc- tor to come downstairs. He took his time, and when he walked into the sitting room, he was not alone. Virgil accompanied him. She sat on one of the green settees, and the doctor took the other one, setting his black leather bag at his side. Virgil re- mained standing.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Tam Arthur Cummins,” the doctor said. “You must be Miss Noemi Taboada.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The doctor dressed in clothes of a good cut, but which were a decade or two out of fashion. It felt like everyone who visited High Place had been stuck in time, but then she imagined in such a small town there would be little need to update one’s wardrobe. Virgil’s clothing, however, seemed fashionable. Ei- ther he had bought himself a new wardrobe the last time he’d been in Mexico City or he considered himself exceptional and his clothes worthy of more expense. Perhaps it was his wife’s money that allowed a certain lavishness.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Yes. Thank you for taking the time to speak to me,” Noemi said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“It’s my pleasure. Now, Virgil says you have a few questions for me.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I do. They tell me my cousin has tuberculosis.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Before she could continue, the doctor was nodding and speaking. “She does. It’s nothing to be concerned about. She’s been receiving streptomycin to help her get over it, but the</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_53" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 53 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164132_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">‘rest’ cure still holds true. Plenty of sleep, plenty of relaxation, and a good diet are the true solution to this malady.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The doctor took off his glasses and took out a handkerchief, proceeding to clean the lenses as he spoke. “An ice bag on the head or an alcohol rub, that’s really what all this is about. It will pass. Soon she’ll be right as rain. Now, if you'll excuse me</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The doctor stuffed the glasses in the breast pocket of his jacket, no doubt intending to leave the conversation at that, but it was Noemi’s turn to interrupt him.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“No, I won't excuse you yet. Catalina is very odd. When I was a little girl, Iremember my aunt Brigida had tuberculosis and she did not act like Catalina at all.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Every patient is different.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“She wrote a very uncharacteristic letter to my father, and she seems unlike herself,” Noemi said, trying to put her im- pressions into words. “She has changed.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Tuberculosis doesn’t change a person, it merely intensi- fies the traits the patient already possesses.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Well, then, there’s definitely something wrong with Cata- lina, because she’s never possessed this listlessness. She has such an odd look about her.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The doctor took out his glasses and put them on again. He must not have liked what he saw and frowned.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You did not let me finish,” the doctor muttered, sounding snappish. His eyes were hard. She pressed her lips together. “Your cousin is a very anxious girl, quite melancholic, and the illness has intensified this.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Catalina is not anxious.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You deny her depressive tendencies?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi recalled what her father had said in Mexico City. He'd called Catalina melodramatic. But melodramatic and anxious were not the same thing at all, and Catalina had defin- itely never heard voices in Mexico City, and she hadn’t had that bizarre expression on her face.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“What depressive tendencies?” Noemi asked.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“When her mother died, she became withdrawn,” Virgil said. “She had periods of great melancholy, crying in her room and talking nonsense. It’s worse now.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He had not spoken until then, and now he chose to bring that up, and not only to bring it up but to speak with a careful detachment, as if he were describing a stranger instead of his wife.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Yes, and as you said her mother had died,” Noemi replied. “And that was years and years ago, when she was a girl.”</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_54" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 54 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164137_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Perhaps you'll find certain things come back,” he said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Although tuberculosis is hardly a death sentence, it can still be upsetting for the patient,” the doctor explained. “The isolation, the physical symptoms. Your cousin has suffered from chills and night sweats; they’re not a pretty sight, I as- sure you, and codeine provides temporary relief. You cannot expect her to be cheery and baking pies.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“T’m concerned. She’s my cousin, after all.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Yes, but if you begin to get agitated too, then we won’t be better off, will we?” the doctor said, shaking his head. “Now, I really must be going. I’ll see you next week, Virgil.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Doctor,” she said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“No, no, I will be going,” the doctor repeated, like a man who has become aware of an impending mutiny aboard a ship.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The doctor shook Noemi’s hand, grabbed his bag, and off he went, leaving her upon the grotesque settee, biting her lips and not knowing quite what to say. Virgil took the spot the doctor had vacated and leaned back, aloof. If there ever was a man who had ice in his veins, it was this one. His face was bloodless. Had he really courted Catalina? Courted anyone? She could not picture him expressing affection toward any liv- ing thing.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Dr. Cummins is a very capable physician,” he said with a voice that was indifferent, a voice that indicated he would not have cared if Cummins was the best or worst physician on Earth. “His father was the family’s doctor, and now he watches over our health. I assure you, he has never been found lacking in any way.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I’m sure he is a good doctor.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You do not sound sure.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She shrugged, trying to make light of it, thinking that if she kept a smile on her face and her words were airy, he might be more receptive. After all, he seemed to be taking this whole matter lightly. “If Catalina is ill, then she might be better off in a sanatorium close to Mexico City, somewhere where she can be tended to properly.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You don’t believe I can tend to my wife?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I didn’t say that. But this house is cold and the fog outside is not the most uplifting sight.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Is this the mission that your father gave you?” Virgil asked. “That you would come here and snatch Catalina away?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She shook her head. “No.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“It feels like it,” he said briskly, though he did not sound upset. The words remained cold. “I realize that my home is not the most modern and most fashionable there is. High</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_55" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 55 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164141_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Place was once a beacon, a shining jewel of a house, and the mine produced so much silver that we could afford to cram armoires with silks and velvet and fill our cups with the finest wines. It is not so anymore.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“But we know how to take care of ill people. My father is old, he’s not in perfect health, yet we tend to him adequately. I wouldn’t do any less for the woman I’ve married.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Still. I would like to ask, perhaps, what Catalina needs is a specialist in other matters. A psychiatrist—”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He laughed so loudly she jumped a little in her seat, for until now his face had been very serious, and the laughter was unpleasant. The laughter challenged her, and his eyes settled on her.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“A psychiatrist. And where might you find one around these parts? You think he might be summoned out of thin air? There is a public clinic in town with a single doctor and noth- ing more. You'll hardly find a psychiatrist there. You’d have to head to Pachuca, maybe even to Mexico City, and fetch one. I doubt they’d come.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“At least the doctor at the clinic might offer a second opin- ion, or he might have other ideas about Catalina.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“There’s a reason why my father brought his own doctor from England, and it’s not because the health care in this place was magnificent. The town is poor and the people there are coarse, primitive. It’s not a place crawling with doctors.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I must insist—”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Yes, yes, I do believe you will insist,” he said, standing up, the striking blue eyes still unkindly fixed on her. “You get your way in most things, don’t you, Miss Taboada? Your father does as you wish. Men do as you wish.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He reminded her of a fellow she’d danced with at a party the previous summer. They had been having fun, briskly step- ping to a danzon, and then came time for the ballads. During “Some Enchanted Evening” the man held her far too tightly and tried to kiss her. She turned her head, and when she looked at him again there was pure, dark mockery across his features.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi stared back at Virgil, and he stared at her with that same sort of mockery: a bitter, ugly stare.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“What do you mean?” she asked, challenge peppering the question.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“T recall Catalina mentioning how insistent you can be when you want a beau to do your bidding. I won't fight you. Get your second opinion if you can find it,” he said with a chill- ing finality as he walked out of the room.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_56" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 56 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164146_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She felt a little pleased to have needled him. She sensed that</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">he had expected—as had the doctor—that she would accept his words mutely. That night she dreamed that a golden flower sprouted from the walls in her room, only it wasn’t...she didn’t think it a flower. It had tendrils, yet it wasn’t a vine, and next to the not- flower rose a hundred other tiny golden forms.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Mushrooms, she thought, finally recognizing the bulbous shapes, and as she walked toward the wall, intrigued and attracted by the glow, she brushed her hands against these forms. The golden bulbs seemed to turn into smoke, bursting, rising, falling like dust upon the floor. Her hands were coated in this dust.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She attempted to clean it off, wiping her hands on her nightgown, but the gold dust clung to her palms, it went under her nails. Golden dust swirled around her, and it lit up the room, bathing it in a soft yellow light. When she looked above, she saw the dust glittering like miniature stars against the ceiling, and below, on the rug, was another golden swirl of stars.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She brushed her foot forward, disturbing the dust on the rug, and it bounced up into the air again, then fell.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Suddenly, Noemi was aware of a presence in the room. She raised her head, her hand pressed against her nightgown, and saw someone standing by the door. It was a woman in a dress of yellowed antique lace. Where her face ought to have been there was a glow, golden like that of the mushrooms on the wall. The woman’s glow grew stronger, then dimmed. It was like watching a firefly in the summer night sky.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Next to Noemi the wall had started to quiver, beating to the same rhythm as the golden woman. Beneath her the floor- boards pulsed too; a heart, alive and knowing. The golden filaments that had emerged together with the mushrooms covered the wall like a netting and continued to grow. She no- ticed, then, that the woman’s dress was not made of lace, but was instead woven with the same filaments.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The woman raised a gloved hand and pointed at Noemi, and she opened her mouth, but having no mouth since her face was a golden blur, no words came out.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi had not felt scared. Not until now. But this, the woman attempting to speak, it made her indescribably afraid.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_57" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 57 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164151_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">A fear that traveled down her spine, to the soles of her feet, for- cing Noemi to step back and press her hands against her lips.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She had no lips, and when she tried to take another step back she realized that her feet had fused to the ground. The golden woman reached forward, reached toward her, and held Noemi’s face between her hands. The woman made a noise, like the crunching of leaves, like the dripping of water onto a pond, like the buzzing of insects in the pitch-black darkness, and Noemi wished to press her hands against her ears, but she had no hands anymore.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi opened her eyes, drenched in sweat. For a minute she didn’t remember where she was, and then she recalled she had been invited to High Place. She reached for the glass of water she’d left by the bedside and almost knocked it down. She gulped down the whole glass and then turned her head.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The room was in shadows. No light, golden or otherwise, dotted the wall’s surface. Nevertheless, she had an impulse to rise and run her hands against the wall, as if to make sure there was nothing strange lurking behind the wallpaper.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_58" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 58 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164156_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">oemi’s best bet for obtaining a car was Francis.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She didn’t think Florence would give her the</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">time of day, and Virgil had been absolutely irri-</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">tated with her when they had spoken the previ- ous day. Noemi remembered what Virgil had said about men doing as she wanted. It bothered her to be thought of poorly. She wanted to be liked. Perhaps this explained the parties, the crystalline laughter, the well-coiffed hair, the rehearsed smile. She thought that men such as her father could be stern and men could be cold like Virgil, but women needed to be liked or they’d be in trouble. A woman who is not liked is a bitch, anda bitch can hardly do anything: all avenues are closed to her. “Well, she definitely did not feel liked in this house, but Francis was friendly enough.
