She found him near the kitchen, looking more washed out than the previous days, a slim figure of ivory, but his eyes were energetic. He smiled at her. When he did, he wasn’t bad looking. Not quite like his cousin—Vir- gil was terribly attractive—but then she thought most men would have had a hard time competing with Virgil. No doubt that’s what had hooked Catalina. That pretty face. Maybe the air of mystery he’d had about him too had made Catalina for- get about sensible matters.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Genteel poverty, Noemi’s father had said. That’s what that man has to offer.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Apparently also a rambling, old house where you were li- able to have bad dreams. God, the city seemed so far away.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“T’d like to ask you for a favor,” she said after they’d ex- changed morning pleasantries. As she spoke she linked her arm to his with a fluid, well-practiced motion, and they began walking together. “I want to borrow one of your cars and go into town. I have letters I’d like to post. My father doesn’t really know how I’m doing.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You need me to drive you there?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I can drive myself there.”</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_59" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 59 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164200_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Francis made a face, hesitating. “I don’t know what Virgil would say about that.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She shrugged. “You don’t have to tell him. What, you don’t think I can drive? I’ll show you my license if you want.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Francis ran a hand through his fair hair. “It’s not that. The family is very particular about the cars.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“And I’m very particular about driving on my own. Surely I don’t need a chaperone, and you’d make a terrible chaperone, anyway.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“How so?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Who ever heard of a man playing chaperone? You need an insufferable aunt. I can lend you one of mine for a weekend if you'd like. It’ll cost you a car. Will you help me, please? I’m desperate.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He chuckled as she steered him outside. He picked up the car keys hanging from a hook in the kitchen. Lizzie, one of the maids, was rolling bread upon a floured table. She did not acknowledge either Noemi or Francis even one bit. The staff at High Place was almost invisible, like in one of Catalina’s fairy tales. Beauty and the Beast, that had been it, had it not? Invisible servants who cooked the meals and laid down the sil- verware. Ridiculous. Noemi knew all the people who worked in her house by name, and they certainly were not begrudged their chatter. That she even knew the names of the staff at High Place seemed a small miracle, but she’d asked Francis, and Francis had obligingly introduced them: Lizzie, Mary, and Charles, who, like the porcelain locked in the cabinets, had been imported from England many decades ago.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">They walked toward the shed, and he handed her the car keys. “You won’t get lost?” Francis asked, leaning against the car’s window and looking down at her.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“T can manage.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">True enough. It wasn’t as if one could even attempt to get lost. The road led up or down the mountain, and down she went, to the little town. She felt quite content during the drive and rolled her window open to enjoy the fresh mountain air. It wasn’t such a bad place, she thought, once you got out of the house. It was the house that disfigured the land.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi parked the car by the town square, guessing both the post office and the medical clinic must be nearby. She was right and was quickly rewarded with the sight of a little green-and-white building that proclaimed itself the medical unit. Inside there were three green chairs and several posters explaining all matter of diseases. There was a receiving desk,</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_60" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 60 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164205_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">but it was empty, and a closed door with a plaque on it and the doctor’s name in large letters. Julio Eusebio Camarillo, it said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She sat down, and after a few minutes the door opened and out came a woman holding a toddler by the hand. Then the doctor poked his head through the doorway and nodded at her.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Good day,” he said. “How can I help you?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“T’m Noemi Taboada,” she said. “You are Dr. Camarillo?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She had to ask because the man looked rather young. He was very dark and had short hair that he parted down the middle and a little mustache that did not really age him, man- aging to make him look a bit ridiculous, like a child mimicking a physician. He also wasn’t wearing a doctor’s white coat, just a beige-and-brown sweater.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“That’s me. Come in,” he said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Inside his office, on the wall behind his desk, she indeed saw the certificate from the UNAM with his name in an ele- gant script. He also had an armoire, the doors thrown open, filled with pills, cotton swabs, and bottles. A large maguey lay in a corner in a yellow pot.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The doctor sat behind his desk and Noemi sat on a plastic chair, which matched the ones in the vestibule.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“T don’t think we’ve met before,” Dr. Camarillo said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“T’m not from around here,” she said, placing her purse on her lap and leaning forward. “I’ve come to see my cousin. She’s sick, and I thought you might take a look at her. She has tuberculosis.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Tuberculosis? In El Triunfo?” the doctor asked, sounding quite astonished. “I hadn’t heard anything about that.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Not in El Triunfo proper. At High Place.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“The Doyle house,” he said haltingly. “You are related to them?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“No. Well, yes. By marriage. Virgil Doyle is married to my cousin Catalina. I was hoping you'd go check on her.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The young doctor looked confused. “But wouldn’t Dr. Cum- mins be taking care of her? He’s their doctor.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“T’d like a second opinion, I suppose,” she said and ex- plained how strange Catalina seemed and her suspicions that she might require psychiatric attention.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Dr. Camarillo listened patiently to her. When she was done, he twirled a pencil between his fingers.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“The thing is, I’m not sure I’d be welcome at High Place if I showed up there. The Doyles have always had their own phys- ician. They don’t mingle with the townsfolk,” he said. “When the mine was operational and they hired Mexican workers,</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_61" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 61 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164210_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">they had them living at a camp up the mountain. Arthur Cum- mins senior also tended to them. There were several epidemics back when the mine was open, you know. Lots of miners died, and Cummins had his hands full, but he never requested local help. I don’t believe they think much of local physicians.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“What sort of epidemic was it?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He tapped his pencil’s eraser against his desk three times. “Tt wasn’t clear. A high fever, very tricky. People would say the oddest things, they’d rant and rave, they’d have convulsions, they’d attack each other. People would get sick, they’d die, then all would be well, and a few years later again the mystery illness would strike.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“T’ve seen the English Cemetery,” Noemi said. “There are many graves.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“That’s only the English people. You should see the local cemetery. They said that in the last epidemic, around the time the Revolution started, the Doyles didn’t even bother sending down the corpses for a proper burial. They tossed them in a pit.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“That can’t be, can it?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Who knows.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The phrase carried with it an implicit distaste. The doctor didn’t say, “Well, I believe it,” but it seemed there might be no reason why he shouldn't.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You must be from El Triunfo, then, to know all of this.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“From near enough,” he said. “My family sold supplies to people at the Doyle mine, and when they shuttered it, they moved to Pachuca. I went to study in Mexico City, but now I’m back. I wanted to help the people here.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You should start by helping my cousin, then,” she said. “Will you come up to the house?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Dr. Camarillo smiled but he shook his head, apologetic. “I told you, you'll get me in trouble with Cummins and the Doyles.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“What can they do to you? Aren’t you the town’s phys- ician?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“The health clinic is public, and the government pays for bandages, rubbing alcohol, and gauze. But El Triunfo is small, it’s needy. Most people are goat farmers. Back when the Span- iards controlled the mine, they could support themselves making tallow for the miners. Not now. There’s a church anda very nice priest here, and he collects alms for the poor.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“And I bet the Doyles place money in his contribution box and the priest is your friend,” Noemi said.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_62" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 62 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164214_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Cummins places the contributions in the box. The Doyles don’t bother with that. But it’s their money, all the same, everyone knows it.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She didn’t think the Doyles had much money left; the mine had been closed for more than three decades. But their bank account must have a modest balance, and a little bit of cash might go a long way in an isolated town like El Triunfo.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">What to do now? She thought it over, quickly, and decided to take advantage of those theater lessons her father had con- sidered a waste of money.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Then you won't help me. You're afraid of them! Oh, and here I am without a friend in the world,” she said, clutching her purse and standing up slowly, her lip quivering dramat- ically. Men always panicked when she did that, afraid she’d cry. Men were always so afraid of tears, of having a hysterical woman on their hands.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">At once the doctor made a placating motion and spoke quickly. “I didn’t say that.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Then?” she pressed on, sounding hopeful, giving him the most fetching of smiles, the one she used when she wanted to get a policeman to let her go without a speeding ticket. “Doc- tor, it would mean the world to me if you helped.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Even if I go, I’m no psychologist.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi took out her handkerchief and clutched it, a little visual reminder that she could, at any moment, break into tears and start dabbing at her eyes. She sighed.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I could head to Mexico City, but I don’t want to leave Cata- lina alone, especially if there’s no need for it. I may be wrong. You'd save me a long trip back and forth; the train doesn’t even run every day. Will you do me this little favor? Will you come?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi looked at him, and he looked back at her with a dose of skepticism, but he nodded his head. “I’ll stop by Monday around noon.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Thanks,” she said, standing up quickly and shaking his hand, and then, remembering the fullness of her errand, she paused. “By the way, do you know a Marta Duval?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Are you going around talking to every specialist in town?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Why do you say that?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“She’s the local healer.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Do you know where she lives? My cousin wanted a remedy from her.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Does she? Well, I suppose it makes sense. Marta does a lot of business with the women in town. Gordolobo tea is still a popular remedy for tuberculosis.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Does it help?”</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_63" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 63 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164219_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“It’s fine enough for coughs.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Dr. Camarillo bent down over his desk and drew a map on his notepad and handed it to her. Noemi decided to walk to Duval’s house, since he said it was nearby, and it turned out to bea good idea, because the path that led to the woman’s house would have been no good for a car and the way there was a lit- tle convoluted, the streets following no plan, growing chaotic. Noemi had to ask for directions, despite the map.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She spoke to a woman who was doing her laundry by the front door of her house, scrubbing a shirt against a bat- tered washboard. The woman put down her bar of Zote soap and informed Noemi she had to go uphill a little farther. The town’s neglect was more obvious the farther you moved from the central square and the church. The houses became shacks made of bare brick, and everything seemed gray and dusty, with scrawny-looking goats or chickens stuck behind rickety fences. Some dwellings were abandoned, with no doors or windows left. She supposed the neighbors had scavenged whatever wood, glass, and other materials they could take. When they’d driven through town, Francis must have taken the most scenic of roads, and even then her impression had been of decay.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The healer’s house was very small and stood out because it was painted white and was better taken care of. An old woman with her hair in a long braid, wearing a blue apron, sat outside by the door on a three-legged stool. She had two bowls next to her and was peeling peanuts. In one bowl she threw the dis- carded shells, in another she threw the peanuts. The woman did not look up as Noemi approached her. She was humming a tune.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Excuse me,” Noemi said. “I’m looking for Marta Duval.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The humming ceased. “You've got the prettiest shoes I’ve ever seen,” the old woman said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi glanced down at the pair of black high-heeled shoes she was wearing. “Thank you.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I don’t get many people with pretty shoes like that.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The woman cracked another peanut open and tossed it into the bowl. Then she stood up. “I’m Marta,” she said, looking up at Noemi, her eyes cloudy with cataracts.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Marta went into the house carrying a bowl in each hand. Noemi followed her inside, into a small kitchen that also served as the dining room. On a wall there was a picture of the Sacred Heart and a bookshelf held plaster figurines of saints, candles, and bottles filled with herbs. From the ceiling there</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_64" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 64 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164224_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“It’s fine enough for coughs.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Dr. Camarillo bent down over his desk and drew a map on his notepad and handed it to her. Noemi decided to walk to Duval’s house, since he said it was nearby, and it turned out to bea good idea, because the path that led to the woman’s house would have been no good for a car and the way there was a lit- tle convoluted, the streets following no plan, growing chaotic. Noemi had to ask for directions, despite the map.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She spoke to a woman who was doing her laundry by the front door of her house, scrubbing a shirt against a bat- tered washboard. The woman put down her bar of Zote soap and informed Noemi she had to go uphill a little farther. The town’s neglect was more obvious the farther you moved from the central square and the church. The houses became shacks made of bare brick, and everything seemed gray and dusty, with scrawny-looking goats or chickens stuck behind rickety fences. Some dwellings were abandoned, with no doors or windows left. She supposed the neighbors had scavenged whatever wood, glass, and other materials they could take. When they’d driven through town, Francis must have taken the most scenic of roads, and even then her impression had been of decay.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The healer’s house was very small and stood out because it was painted white and was better taken care of. An old woman with her hair in a long braid, wearing a blue apron, sat outside by the door on a three-legged stool. She had two bowls next to her and was peeling peanuts. In one bowl she threw the dis- carded shells, in another she threw the peanuts. The woman did not look up as Noemi approached her. She was humming a tune.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Excuse me,” Noemi said. “I’m looking for Marta Duval.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The humming ceased. “You've got the prettiest shoes I’ve ever seen,” the old woman said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi glanced down at the pair of black high-heeled shoes she was wearing. “Thank you.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I don’t get many people with pretty shoes like that.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The woman cracked another peanut open and tossed it into the bowl. Then she stood up. “I’m Marta,” she said, looking up at Noemi, her eyes cloudy with cataracts.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Marta went into the house carrying a bowl in each hand. Noemi followed her inside, into a small kitchen that also served as the dining room. On a wall there was a picture of the Sacred Heart and a bookshelf held plaster figurines of saints, candles, and bottles filled with herbs. From the ceiling there</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_65" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 65 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164229_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">also hung herbs and dried flowers, lavender and epazote and branches of rue.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi knew there were healers who made all sorts of rem- edies, gathering herbs for hangovers and herbs for fevers, and even tricks to cure the evil eye, but Catalina had never been the type to seek such cures. The first book that had gotten Noemi really interested in anthropology had been Witchcraft, Oracles, and Magic Among the Azande, and when she tried to discuss it with Catalina, Catalina would not hear of it. The mere word “witchcraft” gave her a fright, and a healer of Duval’s sort was two steps removed from witchcraft, not only handing out tonics but also curing the susto by placing across of holy palm on someone’s head.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">No, Catalina wouldn’t have been the type to wear a bracelet of ojo de venado on her wrist. How had she ended up at this house, talking to Marta Duval, then?</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The old woman placed the bowls on the table and pulled out a chair. When she sat down there was a sudden fluttering of wings, which startled Noemi, and a parrot swooped onto the woman’s shoulder.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Sit,” Marta said, taking a peeled peanut and handing it to the parrot. “What do you want?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi sat down across from her. “You made a remedy for my cousin, and she needs more of it.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“What was it?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“’m not sure. Her name is Catalina. Do you remember her?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“The girl from High Place.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The woman took another peanut and gave it to the parrot, which cocked its head and stared at Noemi.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Yes, Catalina. How do you know her?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I don’t. Not really. Your cousin used to come to church once in a while, and she must’ve gotten to talking with some- one there because she came to see me, told me that she needed something to help her sleep. She visited me a couple of times. Last time I saw her she was agitated, but wouldn’t tell me about her problems. She asked me to mail a letter for her, ad- dressed to someone in Mexico City.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Why didn’t she mail it herself?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I don’t know. She said, ‘Come Friday, if we don’t see each other, mail this,’ so I did. Like I said, she wouldn’t discuss her problems. She said she had bad dreams, and I tried to help with that.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Bad dreams, Noemi thought, recalling her nightmare. It wasn’t hard to have bad dreams in a house like that. She placed</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_66" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 66 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164233_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">her hands on top of her purse. “Well, whatever you gave her must have worked, because she wants more of it.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“More.” The woman sighed. “I told the girl, no tea is going to make her feel better, not for long.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“What do you mean?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“That family is cursed.” The woman touched the parrot’s head, scratching it, and the bird closed its eyes. “You haven’t heard the stories?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“There was an epidemic,” Noemi said cautiously, wonder- ing if she meant that.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Yes, there was sickness, much sickness. But that wasn’t the only thing. Miss Ruth, she shot them.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Who's Miss Ruth?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“It’s a famous story around these parts. I can tell it, but it’ll cost you alittle.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You're rather mercenary. I’m already going to pay for the medicine.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“We've got to eat. Besides, it’s a good story, and no one knows it as well as I do.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“So you're a healer and a storyteller.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Told you, young miss, we got to eat,” the woman said with a shrug.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“All right. I’ll pay for a story. You have an ashtray?” she asked, taking out her cigarettes and her lighter.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Marta grabbed a pewter cup from the kitchen and placed it before her, and Noemi leaned forward, both elbows resting on the table, and lit her cigarette. She offered the old woman a cig- arette and Marta took two, smiling, but she did not light either one, instead tucking them in her apron’s pocket. Perhaps she’d smoke the cigarettes later. Or even sell them.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Where to begin? Ruth, yes. Ruth was Mr. Doyle’s daughter. Mr. Doyle’s darling child, she wanted for nothing. Back then they had many servants. Always lots of servants to polish the silver and make teas. The bulk of those servants were people from the village, and they lived at the house, but sometimes they came down to town. For the market, for other things. And they’d talk, about all the pretty things at High Place and pretty Miss Ruth.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“She was going to marry her cousin—Michael, it was—and they’d ordered a dress from Paris and ivory head combs for her hair. But a week before the wedding, she grabbed a rifle and shot her groom, shot her mother, her aunt, and her uncle. She shot her father, but he survived. And she might have shot Vir- gil, her baby brother, but Miss Florence hid away with him. Or maybe Ruth had mercy.”</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_67" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 67 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164238_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi hadn’t seen a single weapon in the house, but then they must have tossed the rifle. There was only silver on dis- play, and she wondered, incongruously, if the bullets the mur- deress had used might not have been made of silver.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“When she was done shooting them, she took the rifle and killed herself.” The woman cracked a peanut.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">What a morbid tale! And yet, this was not a conclusion. Merely a pause. “There’s more, isn’t there?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Yes.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You're not going to tell me the rest?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“One has to eat, young miss.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“T'll pay.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You won't be stingy?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Never.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi had placed the box of cigarettes on the table. Marta extended a wrinkled hand and took another one, again tucking it in her apron. She smiled.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“The servants left after that. The people who remained in High Place were the family and trusted folks they’d employed for a long time. They stayed there, stayed out of sight. Then one day Miss Florence was suddenly at the train station, off on vacation when she had never set a foot outside the house. She came back married to a young man. Richard, he was called.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“He wasn’t like the Doyles. He was talkative; he liked to come down to town in his car and have a drink and chat. He’d lived in London and New York and Mexico City, and you got the feeling that the house of the Doyles wasn’t his favor- ite place of them all. He was talkative, all right, and then he started talking strange things.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“What sort of things?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Talk of ghosts and spirits and the evil eye. He was a strong man, Mr. Richard, until he wasn’t, and he looked rather shabby and thin, stopped coming into town and disappeared from view. They found him at the bottom of a ravine. There’re lots of ravines here, you might have noticed that, well, there he was, dead at twenty-nine, left behind a son.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Francis, she thought. Pale-faced Francis with his soft hair and his softer smile. She’d heard nothing of this long saga, but then she supposed it was not the kind of thing anyone would like to discuss.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Tt all sounds tragic, but I’m not sure I'd call it a curse.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You'd call it coincidence, wouldn’t you? Yes, I suppose you would. But the fact is everything they touch rots.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Rots. The word sounded so ugly, it seemed to stick to the tongue, it made Noemi want to bite her nails even though</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_68" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 68 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164243_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">she’d never done such a thing. She was particular about her hands; ugly nails wouldn’t have done for her. It was odd, that house. The Doyles and their servants were all an odd lot, but a curse? No.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“It couldn’t be anything but coincidence,” she said, shaking her head.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Could be.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Can you make the same remedy you made for Catalina the last time?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“It’s no easy thing. I’d have to gather the ingredients, and it would take me a little while. It wouldn’t solve the issue. It’s like I said: the problem is that house, that cursed house. Jump on that train and leave it behind, that’s what I told your cousin. I thought she’d listened, but what do I know?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Yes, I’m sure you did. What’s the price of this remedy, any- way?” Noemi asked.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“The remedy and the stories.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Yes, that too.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The woman named a sum. Noemi opened her purse and took out a few bills. Marta Duval might have cataracts, but she saw the bills clearly enough.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Tt would take me a week. Come back in a week, but I make no promises,” the woman said, extending her hand, and Noemi placed the bills in her palm. The woman folded them and tucked them in her apron’s pocket. “Can you spare another cigarette?” she added.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Very well. I hope you like them,” Noemi said, handing her one more. “They’re Gauloises.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“They’re not for me.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Then for whom?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Saint Luke the Evangelist,” she said, pointing to one of the plaster figurines on her shelves.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Cigarettes for saints?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“He likes them.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“He has expensive tastes,” Noemi said, wondering if she could find a store that sold anything even close to Gauloises in town. She’d have to replenish her stock soon.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The woman smiled, and Noemi handed her another bill. What the hell. As she’d said, everyone had to eat and God knew how many customers the old lady had. Marta seemed very pleased and smiled even more.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Well, I’m off, then. Don’t let Saint Luke smoke all the cigar- ettes at once.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The woman chuckled, and they walked outside. They shook hands. And the woman squinted.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_69" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 69 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164247_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“How do you sleep?” the woman asked.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Fine.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You have dark circles under your eyes.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“It’s the cold up here. It keeps me awake at night.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“T hope it’s that.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi thought of her odd dream, the golden glow. It had been a rather hideous nightmare, but she had not had time to analyze it. She had a friend who swore by Jung, but Noemi had never understood the whole “the dream is the dreamer” bit, nor had she cared to interpret her dreams. Now she recalled one particular thing Jung wrote: everyone carries a shadow. And like a shadow the woman’s words hung over Noemi as she drove back to High Place.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_70" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 70 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164252_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">hat evening, Noemi was summoned once again to</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">the dreary dinner table with its tablecloth of white</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">damask and the candles, and around this ancient</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">table the Doyles gathered together, Florence, Fran- cis, and Virgil. The patriarch would have supper in his room, it seemed.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi ate little, stirring the spoon around her bowl and itching for conversation, not nourishment. After a little while she could not contain herself any longer and chuckled. Three pairs of eyes settled on her.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Really, must we tie our tongues all dinner long?” she asked. “Could we speak perhaps three or four sentences?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Her voice was like fine glass, contrasting with the heavy furniture and heavy drapes and the equally weighty faces turned toward her. She didn’t mean to be a nuisance, but her carefree nature had little understanding of solemnity. She smiled, hoping for a smile in return, for amoment of levity in- side this opulent cage.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“As a general rule we do not speak during dinner, as I ex- plained last time. But it seems you are very keen on breaking every rule in this house,” Florence said, carefully dabbing a napkin against her mouth.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“What do you mean?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You took a car to town.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I needed to drop a couple of letters at the post office.” This was no lie, for she had indeed scribbled a short letter to her family. She had thought to dutifully send a missive to Hugo too, but then reconsidered. Hugo and Noemi were not a couple in the proper sense of the word, and she worried if she did send a letter he might interpret it as a sign of an impending and serious commitment.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Charles can take any letters.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“T’d rather do it myself, thank you.”</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_71" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 71 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164256_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“The roads are bad. What would you do if your car was stuck in the muck?” Florence asked.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I imagine I’d walk back,” Noemi replied, setting down her spoon. “Really, it’s not a problem.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I imagine it isn’t for you. The mountain has its dangers.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The words did not seem outright hostile, but Florence’s dis- approval was thick as treacle, coating each and every syllable. Noemi felt suddenly like a girl who'd had her knuckles rapped, and this made her raise her chin and stare back at the woman in the same way she had stared at the nuns at her school, ar- mored with poised insurrection. Florence even resembled the mother superior a little: it was her expression of utter des- pondency. Noemi almost expected her to demand she take out her rosary.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I thought I explained myself when you arrived. You must consult with me on matters concerning this house, its people, and the things in it. I was specific. I told you Charles was to drive you into town and, if not him, then perhaps Francis,” Florence said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“T didn’t think—”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“And you have smoked in your room. Don’t bother denying it. I said it was forbidden.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Florence stared at Noemi, and Noemi imagined the woman sniffing at the linens, examining a cup for traces of ashes. Like a bloodhound, out for prey. Noemi intended to protest, to say that she had smoked but twice in her room and both times she had intended to open the window, but it was not her fault it wouldn’t open. It was closed so tight you’d think they’d nailed it shut.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“It’s a filthy habit. As are certain girls,” Florence added.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Now it was Noemi’s turn to stare at Florence. How dare she. Before she could say anything, Virgil spoke.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“My wife tells me your father can bea rather strict man,” he said, all cool detachment. “He’s set in his ways.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Yes,” Noemi replied, glancing at Virgil. “At times he is.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Florence has managed High Place for decades,” Virgil said. “As we do not have many visitors, you can imagine she’s quite set in her ways too. And it’s quite unacceptable, don’t you think, for a visitor to ignore the rules of a house?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She felt ambushed; she thought they had planned to scold her together. She wondered if they did this with Catalina. She would go into the dining room and offer a suggestion—about the food, the décor, the routine—and they would politely, deli- cately silence her. Poor Catalina, who was gentle and obedient, as gently squashed down.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_72" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 72 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164301_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She had lost her appetite, which had been scant to begin with, and sipped her glass of sickly-sweet wine rather than attempt to converse. Eventually Charles walked in to inform them that Howard would like to see them after dinner, and they made their way up the stairs, like a traveling court off to greet the king.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Howard’s bedroom was very large and decorated with more of the weighty, dark furniture that abounded around the rest of the house, and thick velvet curtains that could conceal the thinnest ray of light.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The most striking feature of the room was the fireplace, with a carved wooden mantelpiece adorned with what at first glance seemed to be circles, but revealed themselves to be more of those snakes eating their tails that she’d spotted be- fore at the cemetery and in the library. A sofa had been set in front of the fireplace, and upon it sat the patriarch, swaddled in a green robe.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Howard looked even older that evening. He reminded her of one of the mummies she’d seen in the catacombs in Guana- juato, arranged in two rows for tourists to peek at. Upright they stood, preserved by a quirk of nature, and dragged from their graves when the burial tax was not paid so that they might be exhibited. He had that same withered, sunken as- pect, as though he had already been embalmed, the elements reducing him to bone and marrow.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The others walked ahead of her, each of them clasping the old man’s hand in greeting, then stepping aside.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“There you are. Come, sit with me,” the old man said, mo- tioning to her.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi sat next to Howard, giving him a vague, polite smile. Florence, Virgil, and Francis did not join them, instead choosing another couch and chairs on the other end of the room to sit down. She wondered if he always received people in this manner, picking one lucky person who would be al- lowed at his side, who would be granted an audience, while the rest of the family was brushed aside for the time being. A long time ago this room might have been filled with relatives, friends, all of them waiting and hoping Howard Doyle would curl a finger in their direction and ask them to sit with him for a little while. She had seen photographs and paintings of a large number of people around the house, after all. The paint- ings were ancient; maybe they were not all of relatives who had lived at High Place, but the mausoleum hinted at a vast family or, perhaps, the assurance of descendants who would find their way there.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_73" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 73 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164305_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Two large oil paintings hanging above the fireplace caught Noemi’s eye. They each depicted a young woman. Both were fair-haired, both very similar in looks, so much so that at first glance one might take them for the same woman. However, there were differences: straight strawberry blond hair versus honeyed locks, a tad more plumpness in the face in the woman on the left. One wore an amber ring on her finger, which matched the one on Howard’s hand.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Are these your relatives?” she asked, intrigued by the like- ness, what she supposed was the Doyle look.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“These are my wives,” Howard said. “Agnes passed away shortly after our arrival to this region. She was pregnant when disease took her away.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Tm sorry.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“It was along time ago. But she has not been forgotten. Her spirit lives on in High Place. And there, the one on the right, that is my second wife. Alice. She was fruitful. A woman’s function is to preserve the family line. The children, well, Vir- gilis the only one left, but she did her duty and she did it well.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi looked up at the pale face of Alice Doyle, the blond hair cascading down her back, her right hand holding a rose between two fingers, her face serious. Agnes, to her left, was also robbed of mirth, clasping a bouquet between her hands, the amber ring catching a stray ray of light. They stared for- ward in their silk and lace with what? Resolution? Confidence?</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“They were beautiful, were they not?” the old man asked. He sounded proud, like a man who has received a nice ribbon at the county fair for his prize hog or mare.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Yes. Although...”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Although what, my dear?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Nothing. They seem so alike.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I imagine they should. Alice was Agnes’s little sister. They were both orphaned and left penniless, but we were kin, cousins, and soI took them in. And when I traveled here Agnes and I were wed, and Alice came with us.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Then you twice married your cousin,” Noemi said. “And your wife’s sister.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Is it scandalous? Catherine of Aragon was first married to Henry VIII’s brother, and Queen Victoria and Albert were cousins.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You think you're a king, then?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Howard reached forward and patted her hand; his skin seemed paper-thin and dry, smiling. “Nothing as grandiose as that.”</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_74" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 74 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164310_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“?’m not scandalized,” Noemi said politely and gave her head a little shake.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I hardly knew Agnes,” Howard said with a shrug. “We were married and before a year had passed I was forced to organize a funeral. The house wasn’t even finished back then and the mine had been operating for a scant handful of months. Then the years passed, and Alice grew up. There were no suitable grooms for her in this part of the world. It was a natural choice. One could say preordained. This is her wed- ding portrait. See there? The date is clearly visible on that tree in the foreground: 1895. A wonderful year. So much silver that year. A river of it.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The artist had indeed carved the tree with the year and the initials of the bride: AD. Agnes’s portrait sported the same detail, the year carved on a stone column: 1885, AD. Noemi wondered if they had simply dusted off the old bride’s trous- seau and handed it to the younger sister. She imagined Alice pulling out linens and chemises monogrammed with her ini- tials, pressing an old dress against her chest and staring into the mirror. A Doyle, eternally a Doyle. No, it wasn’t scandalous, but it was damn odd.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Beautiful, my beautiful darlings,” the old man said, his hand still resting atop Noemi’s as he turned his eyes back to- ward the paintings, his fingers rubbing her knuckles. “Did you ever hear about Dr. Galton’s beauty map? He went around the British Isles compiling a record of the women he saw. He cata- logued them as attractive, indifferent, or repellent. London ranked as the highest for beauty, Aberdeen the lowest. It might seem like a funny exercise, but of course it had its logic.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Aesthetics again,” Noemi said, as she delicately pulled her hand free from his and stood up, as if to take a closer look at the paintings. Truth be told she didn’t like his touch, nor did she much enjoy the faint unpleasant odor that emanated from his robe. It might have been an ointment or medicine that he’d applied.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Yes, aesthetics. One must not dismiss them as frivolous. After all, didn’t Lombroso study men’s faces in order to recog- nize a criminal type? Our bodies hide so many mysteries and they tell so many stories without a single word, do they not?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She looked at those portraits above their heads, the serious mouths, the pointed chins and luscious hair. What did they say, in their wedding dresses as the brush stroked the canvas? Iam happy, unhappy, indifferent, miserable. Who knew. One could construct a hundred different narratives, it didn’t make them true.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_75" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 75 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164314_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You mentioned Gamio when we last spoke,” Howard said, grabbing his cane and standing up to move next to her. Noemi’s attempt at distance had been in vain; he crowded her, touched her arm. “You’re correct. Gamio believes natural selection has pressed the indigenous people of this contin- ent forward, allowing them to adapt to biological and geo- graphical factors that foreigners cannot withstand. When you transplant a flower, you must consider the soil, mustn’t you? Gamio was on the right track.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The old man folded his hands atop his cane and nodded, looking at the paintings. Noemi wished that someone would open a window. The room was stuffy, the conversations of the others were whispers. If they were conversing. Had they gone quiet? Their voices were like the buzzing of insects.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I wonder why you are not married, Miss Taboada. You are the right age for it.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“My father asks himself that same question,” Noemi said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“And what lies do you tell him? That you are too busy? That you esteem many young men but cannot find one that truly captivates you?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">This was very close to what she’d said, and perhaps if he’d intoned the words with a certain levity it might have been constructed as a joke and Noemi would have clutched his arm for a moment and laughed. “Mr. Doyle,” she would have said, and they would have talked about her father and her mother, and how she was always quarreling with her brother, and her cousins who were numerous and lively.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">But Howard Doyle’s words were harsh and his eyes had a sickening sort of animation to them. He almost leered at her, one of his thin hands brushing a strand of her hair, as if doing her a kindness—he’d found a bit of lint and tossed it away— but no. No kindness at all as he moved that lock behind her shoulder. He was a tall man even in his old age, and she didn’t like looking up at him, she didn’t like seeing him bend toward her like that. He looked like a stick insect, an insect hiding under a velvet robe. His lips curved into a smile as he leaned down closer, peering carefully at her.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He smelled foul. She turned her head, and she rested a hand against the mantelpiece. Her eyes met those of Francis, who was looking at them. She thought he was a scared bird, a pi- geon, the eyes round and startled. It was very hard to imagine he was related to the insect-man before her.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Has my son shown you the greenhouse?” Howard asked, stepping back, and his eyes lost their unpleasantness as he turned toward the fire.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_76" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 76 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164319_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I didn’t know you had a greenhouse,” she replied, a little surprised. Then again, she hadn’t opened every door in the house, nor had she looked at the place from every angle. She hadn’t wanted to, beyond her first cursory exploration of High Place. It wasn’t a welcoming home.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“A very small one and in a state of disrepair, like most things around here, but the roof is of stained glass. You might like it. Virgil, ’'ve told Noemi you will show her the green- house,” Howard said, the loudness of his voice so shocking in the quiet room that Noemi thought it might cause a small tremor.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Virgil merely nodded and, taking this as a cue, approached them. “I'll be glad to, Father,” he said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Good,” Howard said, clasping Virgil’s shoulder before he set off across the room, joining Florence and Francis, and tak- ing up the seat Virgil had been occupying.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Has my father been bothering you, telling you what he considers to be the finest type of manhood and womanhood possible?” Virgil asked, smiling at her. “The answer is tricky: the Doyles are the finest specimens around, but I try not to let it go to my head.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi was a little surprised by the smile, but she wel- comed the warmth after stomaching Howard’s odd leer and his sharp grin. “He was talking about beauty,” she said, her voice charmingly composed.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Beauty. Of course. Well, he was a great connoisseur of beauty, once, although now he can barely eat mush and stay up until nine.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She raised a hand and hid her grin behind it. Virgil traced one of the snake carvings with his index finger, looking a bit more serious as he did, his smile subdued.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I’m sorry about the other night. I was rude. And earlier today Florence made a fuss about the car. But you must not feel badly about it. You can’t be expected to know all our habits and little rules,” he said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“It’s fine.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“It’s stressful, you know. My father is very frail and now Catalina is ill too. I’m not in the best of moods these days. I don’t want you to feel we don’t want you here. We do. We very much do.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Thank you.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I don't think you quite forgive me.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">No, not quite, but she was relieved to see not all the Doyles were so damn gloomy all the time. Maybe he was telling the</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_77" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 77 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164324_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">truth, and before Catalina had fallen sick Virgil had been more disposed to merriment.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Not yet, but if you keep it up I may erase a mark or two on your scorecard.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You keep score then? As if you’re playing cards?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“A girl has to keep track of a number of things. Dances are not the only ones,” she said with that easy, genial tone of hers.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“T’ve been given to understand you are quite the dancer and the gambler. At least, according to Catalina,” he said, still smil- ing at her.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“And here I thought you might be scandalized.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You'd be surprised.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I love surprises, but only when they come with a nice, big bow,” she declared, and because he was playing nice, she played nice too and tossed him a smile.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Virgil in turn gave her an appreciative look that seemed to say, See, we may yet become friends. He offered her his arm, and they walked toward the rest of the family members, to chat for a few more minutes before Howard declared he was much too tired to entertain any more company, and they all disbanded. She had a curious nightmare, unlike any dream she’d had be- fore in this house, even if her nights had been rather restless.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She dreamed that the door opened and in walked Howard Doyle, slowly, each of his steps like the weight of iron, making the boards creak and the walls rumble. It was as if an elephant had trampled into her room. She could not move. An invisible thread anchored her to the bed. Her eyes were closed but she could see him. She gazed at him from above, from the ceiling, and then from the floor, her perspective shifting.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She saw herself too, asleep. She saw him approaching her bed and tugging at the covers. She saw this, and yet her eyes remained shut even when he reached out to touch her face, the edge of a nail running down her neck, athin hand undoing the buttons of her nightdress. It was chilly and he was undressing her.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Behind her she felt a presence, felt it like one feels a cold spot in a house, and the presence had a voice; it leaned close to her ear and it whispered.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Open your eyes,” the voice said, a woman’s voice. There had been a golden woman in her room, in another dream, but this was not the same presence. This was different; she thought this voice was young.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_78" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 78 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164328_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Her eyes were nailed shut, her hands lay flat against the bed, and Howard Doyle loomed over her, stared down at Noemias she slept. He smiled in the dark, white teeth in a dis- eased, rotting mouth.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Open your eyes,” the voice urged her.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Moonlight or another source of light hit Howard Doyle’s thin, insect-like body, and she saw it wasn’t the old man stand- ing by her bed, studying her limbs, her breasts, staring at her pubic hair. It was Virgil Doyle who had acquired his father’s leering grin, who smiled his white smile, and who looked at Noemi like a man observing a butterfly pinned against a velvet cloth.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He pressed a hand against her mouth, pushing her back against the bed, and the bed was very soft, it dipped and swayed and it was like wax, like being pressed into a bed of wax. Or perhaps mud, earth. A bed of earth.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">And she felt such sweet, sickening desire flowing through her body, making her roll her hips, sinuous, a serpent. But it was he who coiled himself around her, swallowed her shud- dering sigh with his lips, and she didn’t quite want this, not like that, not those fingers digging too firmly into her flesh, and yet it was hard to remember why she hadn’t wanted it. She must want this. To be taken, in the dirt, in the dark, without preamble or apology.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The voice at her ear spoke again. It was very insistent, jab- bing her.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Open your eyes.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She did and woke up to discover she was very cold; she had kicked the covers away and they tangled at her feet. Her pillow had tumbled to the floor. The door lay firmly closed. Noemi pressed both hands against her chest, feeling the rapid beating of her heart. She ran a hand down the front of her nightdress. All the buttons were firmly in place.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Of course they would be.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The house was quiet. No one walked through the halls, no one crept into rooms at night to stare at sleeping women. Still, it took her a long time to go back to sleep, and once or twice, when she heard a board creak, she sat up quickly and listened for footsteps.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_79" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 79 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164333_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">oemi planted herself outside the house, waiting</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">for the doctor to arrive. Virgil had told her she</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">could get a second opinion, so she had informed</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Florence the doctor would be stopping by and that she had obtained Virgil’s permission for this visit, but she didn’t quite trust any of the Doyles to greet Dr. Camarillo and had decided to serve as a sentinel.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">As she crossed her arms and tapped her foot she felt, for once, like one of Catalina’s characters in their childhood tales. The maiden gazing out the tower, waiting for the knight to ride to the rescue and vanquish the dragon. Surely the doctor would conjure a diagnosis and a solution.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She felt it necessary to be positive, to hope, for High Place was a place of hopelessness. Its shabby grimness made her want to push forward.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The doctor was punctual and parked his car near a tree, stepping out, doffing his hat and staring up at the house. There wasn’t much mist that day, as if the Earth and sky had cleared up in advance of this visitor, though it served to make the house look more forlorn, unshrouded and bare. Noemi imagined Julio’s house was nothing like this, that it was one of the shabby yet colorful little houses down the main street, with a tiny balcony and wooden shutters and a kitchen with old azulejos.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Well, this is the famous High Place,” Dr. Camarillo said. “About time I saw it, I suppose.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You haven't been here before?” she asked.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“No reason for me to come. I’ve been past where the mining camp used to be. Or what’s left of it, at any rate, when I’ve gone hunting. There’s plenty of deer around, up here. Moun- tain lions too. You have to be careful on this mountain.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“T didn’t know that,” she said. She recalled how Florence had admonished her. Could she have been worried about</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_80" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 80 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164337_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">mountain lions? Or was she more worried about her precious car?</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The doctor grabbed his bag and they went inside. Noemi had been afraid Florence might come running down the stairs, ready to glare at both Dr. Camarillo and Noemi, but the stair- case was empty, and when they reached Catalina’s room they found the woman alone.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Catalina seemed in good enough spirits, sitting in the sun- light, dressed in a simple but becoming blue dress. She greeted the doctor with a smile.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Good day, I’m Catalina.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“And I’m Dr. Camarillo. I’m pleased to meet you.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Catalina extended her hand. “Why, he looks so young, Noemi! He must be hardly older than you!”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You are hardly older than me,” Noemi said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“What are you talking about? You're a little girl.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">This sounded so like the happy Catalina of days past, ban- tering with them, that Noemi began to feel foolish for bring- ing the doctor to the house. But then, as the minutes ticked by, Catalina’s ebullience began to fade and turn into a simmer- ing agitation. And Noemi couldn't help but think that even though nothing was exactly wrong, something was definitely not right.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Tell me, how are you sleeping? Any chills at night?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“No. I feel much better already. Really, there’s no need for you to be here, it’s such a fuss over nothing. Over nothing, truly,” Catalina said. Her vehemence when she spoke had a forced cheerfulness to it. She repeatedly rubbed a finger across her wedding band.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Julio merely nodded. He talked in a steady, measured tone while he took notes. “Have you been given streptomycin and para-aminosalicylic acid?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I think so,” Catalina said, but she responded in such haste Noemi didn’t think she’d even listened to the question.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Marta Duval, did she also send a remedy for you? A tea or herb?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Catalina’s eyes darted across the room. “What? Why would you ask that?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I’m trying to figure out what all your medications are. I’m assuming you saw her for a remedy of some sort?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“There’s no remedy,” she muttered.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She said something else, but it wasn’t a real word. She bab- bled, like a small child, and then Catalina suddenly clutched her neck, as if she’d choke herself, but her grip was lax. No, it was not choking, but a defensive gesture, a woman guard-</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_81" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 81 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164342_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">ing herself, holding her hands up in defense. The movement startled them both. Julio almost dropped his pencil. Catalina resembled one of the deer in the mountains, ready to dart to safety, and neither knew what to say.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“What is it?” Julio asked, after a minute had gone by.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“It’s the noise,” Catalina said, and she slowly slid her hands up her neck and pressed them against her mouth.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Julio looked up at Noemi, who was sitting next to him.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“What noise?” Noemi asked.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I don’t want you here. I’m very tired,” Catalina said, and she gripped her hands together and placed them on her lap, closing her eyes, as if to shut her visitors away. “I really don’t know why you must be here bothering me when I should be sleeping!”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“If you will—’” the doctor began.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I can’t talk anymore, I’m exhausted,” Catalina said, her hands trembling as she attempted to clutch them together. “It’s really quite exhausting being ill and it’s even worse when people say you shouldn’t do anything. Isn’t that odd? Really... it’s...I’m tired. Tired!”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She paused, as if catching her breath. All of a sudden Cata- lina opened her eyes very wide and her face had a terrifying intensity to it. It was the visage of a woman possessed.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“There’re people in the walls,” Catalina said. “There’re people and there’re voices. I see them sometimes, the people in the walls. They’re dead.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She extended her hands, and Noemi gripped them, help- lessly, trying to comfort her, as Catalina shook her head and let out a half sob. “It lives in the cemetery, in the cemetery, Noemi. You must look in the cemetery.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Then just as suddenly Catalina stood up and went to the window, clutching a drape with her right hand and looking outside. Her face softened. It was as if a tornado had struck and spun away. Noemi didn’t know what to do, and the doctor appeared equally baffled.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I’m sorry,” Catalina said evenly. “I don’t know what I say, I’m sorry.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Catalina pressed her hands against her mouth again and began coughing. Florence and Mary, the oldest maid, walked in, carrying a tray with a teapot and a cup. Both of the women eyed Noemi and Dr. Camarillo with disapproval.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Will you be long?” Florence asked. “She’s supposed to be resting.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I was just leaving,” Dr. Camarillo said, collecting his hat and his notepad, clearly knowing himself an unwelcome in-</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_82" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 82 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164347_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">truder with these few words and Florence’s lofty tilt of the head. Florence always knew how to cut you down with the succinct efficiency of a telegram. “It was nice meeting you, Catalina.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">They stepped out of the room. For a couple of minutes nei- ther one spoke, both weary and a little rattled.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“So, what’s your opinion?” she finally asked as they began walking down the stairs.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“On the matter of tuberculosis I would have to take an X- ray of her lungs to get a better idea of her condition, and I really am no expert in tuberculosis in the first place,” he said. “And on the other matter, I warned you, I’m not a psychiatrist. I shouldn’t be speculating—”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Come on, out with it,” Noemi said in exasperation, “you must tell me something.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">They stopped at the foot of the stairs. Julio sighed. “I be- lieve you are correct and she needs psychiatric attention. This behavior is not usual with any tuberculosis patient I’ve met. Perhaps you might find a specialist in Pachuca who could treat her? If you can’t make the trek to Mexico City.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi didn’t think they’d be making the trek anywhere. Maybe if she spoke to Howard and tried to explain her con- cerns? He was the head of the household, after all. But she didn’t like the old man, he rubbed her the wrong way, and Vir- gil might think she was trying to overreach. Florence certainly would be of no help to her, but what about Francis?</p>
