      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I’m afraid I’ve left you with a worse conundrum than be- fore, haven’t I?” Julio said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“No,” Noemi lied. “No, I’m very thankful.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She was dispirited and felt silly for having expected more of him. He was no knight in shining armor nor a wizard who might revive her cousin with a magic potion. She ought to have known better.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He hesitated, seeking perhaps to provide her with more re- assurance. “Well, you know where to find me if you need any- thing else,” he concluded. “Do seek me out if it’s necessary.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi nodded, watched him as he got into his car and drove away. She recalled, rather grimly, that certain fairy tales end in blood. In Cinderella, the sisters cut off their feet, and Sleeping Beauty’s stepmother was pushed into a barrel full of snakes. That particular illustration on the last page of one of the books Catalina used to read to them suddenly came back to her, in all its vivid colors. Green and yellow serpents, the tails poking out of a barrel as the stepmother was stuffed into it.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_83" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 83 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164351_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi leaned against a tree, standing there with her arms crossed for a while. She walked back inside the house to find Virgil standing on the staircase, his hand on the banister.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“There was a man to see you.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“It was the doctor from the public health clinic. You did say he could visit.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“!’m not admonishing you,” he told her as he finished climbing down the stairs and stood in front of her. He ap- peared a little curious, and she guessed he wanted to know what the doctor had said, but she also guessed he would not ask yet, and Noemi didn’t want to blurt it all out either.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Do you think you’d have time to show me the greenhouse now?” she asked diplomatically.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Gladly.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">It was very small, the greenhouse—almost like the post- script at the end of an awkward letter. Neglect had flourished, and there were dirty glass panels and broken glass aplenty. In the rainy season the water seeped in with ease. Mold caked the planters. But a few flowers were still in bloom, and when Noemi looked up she was greeted by the striking vision of col- ored glass: a glass roof decorated with a twining serpent. The snake’s body was green, the eyes were yellow. The sight of it quite surprised her. It was perfectly designed, almost leaping off the glass, its fangs open.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Oh,” she said, pressing the tips of her fingers against her lips.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Something the matter?” Virgil asked, moving to stand next to her.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Nothing, really. I’ve seen that snake around the house,” she said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“The ouroboros.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Is it a heraldic symbol?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“It’s our symbol, but we don’t have a shield. My father hada seal made with it, though.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“What does it mean?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“The snake eats its tail. The infinite, above us, and below.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Well, yes, but why did your family pick that as your seal? It’s everywhere too.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Really?” he said nonchalantly and shrugged.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi tilted her head, trying to get a better look at the snake’s head. “I haven’t seen glass like that in a greenhouse,” she admitted. “You’d expect transparent glass.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“My mother designed it.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Chromic oxide. I’d bet that’s what gives it that green color- ation. But there must also be some uranium oxide used here,</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_84" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 84 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164356_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">because, see? Right there, it almost seems to glow,” she said, pointing at the snake’s head, the cruel eyes. “Was it manufac- tured here or shipped piece by piece from England?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“T know little of how it was built.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Would Florence know?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You're an inquisitive creature.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She wasn’t sure whether he meant it as a compliment or a defect. “The greenhouse, hmm?” he went on. “I know it’s old. I know my mother loved it more than any other part of the house.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Virgil moved toward a long table that ran along the center length of the greenhouse, crammed with yellowed potted plants, and to the back, to a bed box that held a few pris- tine pink roses. He carefully brushed his knuckles against the petals.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“She took care to cut out the weak and useless shoots, to look after each flower. But when she died, nobody much cared for the plants, and this is what’s left of it all.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Tm sorry.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">His eyes were steadfast on the roses, pulling a blighted petal. “It doesn’t matter. I do not remember her. I was a baby when she died.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Alice Doyle, who shared her initials with that other sister. Alice Doyle, blond and pale, who had been flesh once, who had been more than a portrait on a wall, who must have sketched on a piece of paper the serpent that curled above their heads. The rhythms of its scaly body, the shape of its narrowed eyes, and the terrible mouth.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“It was a violent death. We have a certain history of vio- lence, the Doyles. But we are resistant,” he said. “And it was a long time ago. It doesn’t matter.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Your sister shot her, she thought, and she could not picture it. It was such a monstrous, terrible act that she could not im- agine that it truly had happened, in this house. And afterward someone had scrubbed the blood away, someone had burned the dirty linens or replaced the rugs with the ugly scarlet splotches on them, and life had gone on. But how could it have gone on? Such misery, such ugliness, surely it could not be erased.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Yet Virgil seemed unperturbed.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“My father, when he spoke to you yesterday about beauty, he must have spoken about superior and inferior types too,” Virgil said, raising his head and looking at her intently. “He must have mentioned his theories.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“T’m not sure what theories you refer to,” she replied.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_85" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 85 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164401_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“That we have a predetermined nature.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“That sounds rather awful, doesn’t it?” she said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Yet as a good Catholic you must believe in original sin.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Perhaps I’m a bad Catholic. How would you know?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Catalina prays her rosary,” he said. “She went to church each week, before she got sick. I imagine you do the same, back home.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">As a matter of fact Noemi’s eldest uncle was a priest and she was indeed expected to attend mass in a good, mod- est black dress, with her lace mantilla carefully pinned in place. She also had a tiny rosary—because everyone did— and a golden cross on a matching chain, but she didn’t wear the chain regularly, and she had not given much thought to original sin since the days when she was busy learning her catechism in preparation for her first communion. Now she thought vaguely about the cross and almost felt like pressing a hand against her neck, to feel the absence of it.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Do you believe, then, that we have a predetermined na- ture?” she asked.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I have seen the world, and in seeing it I’ve noticed people seem bound to their vices. Take a walk around any tenement and you'll recognize the same sort of faces, the same sort of ex- pressions on those faces, and the same sort of people. You can’t remove whatever taint they carry with hygiene campaigns. There are fit and unfit people.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“It seems like nonsense to me,” she said. “That eugenicist discourse always makes my stomach turn. Fit and unfit. We are not talking about cats and dogs.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Why shouldn’t humans be the equivalent of cats and dogs? We are all organisms striving for survival, moved for- ward by the single instinct that matters: reproduction and the propagation of our kind. Don’t you like to study the nature of man? Isn’t that what an anthropologist does?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I hardly want to discuss this topic.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“What do you want to discuss?” he asked with dry amuse- ment. “I know you're itching to say it, so say it.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi had meant to be more subtle than this, more charm- ing, but there was no point in evasion now. He’d entwined her in conversation and pushed her to speak.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Catalina.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“What about her?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi leaned her back against the long table, her hands resting on its scratched surface, and looked up at him. “The doctor who came today thinks she needs a psychiatrist.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Yes, she very well may need one, eventually,” he agreed.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_86" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 86 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164405_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Eventually?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Tuberculosis is no joke. I cannot be dragging her off some- where else. Besides, she’d hardly be accepted in a psychiatric facility considering her illness. So, yes, eventually we might evaluate specialized psychological care for her. For now she seems to be doing well enough with Arthur.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Well enough?” Noemi scoffed. “She hears voices. She says there are people in the walls.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Yes. I’m aware.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You don’t seem worried.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You presume a great deal, little girl.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Virgil crossed his arms and walked away from her. Noemi protested—a curse, delivered in Spanish, escaping her lips— and quickly moved behind him, her arms brushing against brittle leaves and dead ferns. He turned abruptly and stared down at her.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“She was worse before. You did not see her three or four weeks ago. Fragile, like a porcelain doll. But she’s getting bet- ter.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You don’t know that.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Arthur knows that. You can ask him,” he said calmly.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“That doctor of yours wouldn't even let me ask two ques- tions.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“And that doctor of yours, Miss Taboada, as far as my wife tells me, looks like he can’t even grow a beard.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You talked to her?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I went to see her. That’s how I knew you had a guest.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He was correct on the point of the doctor’s youth, but she shook her head. “What does his age have to do with any- thing?” she replied.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I’m not about to listen to a boy who graduated from med- ical school a few months ago.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Then why did you tell me to bring him here?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He looked her up and down. “I did not. You insisted. Just as you are insisting on having this extremely dull conversation.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He made to leave, but this time she caught his arm, forcing him to turn and face her again. His eyes were very cold, very blue, but a stray beam of light hit them. Gold, they looked for a flickering second, before he inclined his head and the effect passed.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Well, then I insist, no, I demand, that you take her back to Mexico City,” she said. Her attempt at diplomacy was a failure and they both knew it, so she might as well speak openly. “This silly, creaky old house is no good for her. Must I—”</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_87" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 87 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164410_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You are not going to change my mind,” he said, interrupt- ing her, “and in the end she’s my wife.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“She’s my cousin.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Her hand was still on his arm. Carefully, he took hold of her fingers and pried them loose of his jacket’s sleeve, pausing for a second to look down at her hands, as if examining the length of her fingers or the shape of her nails.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I know. I also know you don’t like it here, and if you are itching to get back to your home and away from this ‘creaky’ house, you're welcome to it.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Are you throwing me out now?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“No. But you don’t give the orders around here. We'll be fine as long as you remember this,” he said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You're rude.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I doubt it.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I should go right away.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Throughout this whole conversation his voice had re- mained level, which she found very infuriating, just as she despised the smirk that marked his face. He was civil and yet disdainful.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Maybe. But I don’t think you will. Ithink it’s in your nature to stay. It’s the dutiful pull of blood, of family. I can respect that.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Maybe it’s in my nature not to back down.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I believe you are correct. Don’t bear me a grudge, Noemi. You'll see this is the best course.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I thought we had a truce,” she told him.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“That would imply we’ve been at war. Would you say that?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“No.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Then everything is fine,” he concluded and walked out of the greenhouse.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He had a way of parrying her words that was maddening. She could finally understand why her father had been so irri- tated by Virgil’s correspondence. She could imagine the letters he wrote, filled with sentences that feinted and amounted to an irritating nothing.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She shoved a pot from atop the table. It broke with a resounding crash, spilling earth upon the floor. At once she re- gretted the gesture. She could smash all the crockery, it would do her no good. Noemi knelt down, trying to see if the dam- age might be fixed, grabbing pieces of ceramic and seeing how they fit together, but it was impossible.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Damn it and damn it again. She pushed the pieces away with her foot, under the table.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_88" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 88 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164415_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Of course he had a point. Catalina was his wife, and he was the one who could make choices for her. Why, Mexican women couldn’t even vote. What could Noemi say? What could she do in sucha situation? Perhaps it would be best if her father inter- vened. If he came down here. A man would command more respect. But no, it was as she’d said: she wasn’t going to back down.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Very well. Then she must remain for a while longer. If Vir- gil couldn’t be persuaded to assist her, maybe the loathsome patriarch of the Doyle family might rule in her favor. She might be able to drag Francis onto her side of the court, she suspected that. Most of all she felt like leaving now would be betraying Catalina.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi stood up, and as she did she noticed that there was a mosaic on the floor. Stepping back and looking around the room she realized it circled the table. It was another of the snake symbols. The ouroboros slowly devouring itself. The in- finite, above us, and below, as Virgil had said.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_89" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 89 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164420_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">n Tuesday, Noemi ventured into the cemetery.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Catalina had inspired this second trip—“ You must</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">look in the cemetery,” she’d said—but Noemi</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">did not expect to find anything interesting there. She thought, however, that she might have a smoke in peace, among the tombstones, since Florence wouldn't abide a cigarette even in the privacy of her bedroom.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The mist gave the cemetery a romantic aura. She recalled that Mary Shelley had rendezvoused with her future husband in a cemetery: illicit liaisons by a tomb. Catalina had told her this story, just as she had gushed over Wuthering Heights. Sir Walter Scott, that had been another favorite of hers. And the movies. How she’d delighted in the torturous romance of Maria Candelaria.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Once upon atime Catalina had been engaged to the young- est of the Inclans but had broken it off. When Noemi had asked her why, since he seemed for all intents and purposes a very agreeable man, Catalina had told her she expected more. True romance, she said. True feelings. Her cousin had never quite lost that young-girl wonder of the world, her imagination crowded with visions of women greeting passionate lovers by moonlight. Well, except now. There wasn’t much wonder in Catalina’s eyes, though she did seem lost.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi wondered if High Place had robbed her of her illu- sions, or if they were meant to be shattered all along. Marriage could hardly be like the passionate romances one read about in books. It seemed to her, in fact, a rotten deal. Men would be solicitous and well behaved when they courted a woman, asking her out to parties and sending her flowers, but once they married, the flowers wilted. You didn’t have married men posting love letters to their wives. That’s why Noemi tended to cycle through admirers. She worried a man would be briefly impressed with her luster, only to lose interest later on. There was also the excitement of the chase, the delight that flew</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_90" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 90 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164424_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">through her veins when she knew a suitor was bewitched with her. Besides, boys her age were dull, always talking about the parties they had been to the previous week or the one they were planning to go to the week after. Easy, shallow men. Yet the thought of anyone more substantial made her nervous, for she was trapped between competing desires, a desire for a more meaningful connection and the desire to never change. She wished for eternal youth and endless merriment. == "Noemi rounded a small cluster of tombs with moss cover- ing the names and dates on them. Leaning back on a broken headstone, she reached into her pocket for her pack of cigar- ettes. She saw movement nearby, on a mound, a shape half hidden by the mist and atree.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Who’s there?” she said, hoping it wasn’t a mountain lion. That would be her luck.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The mist did not allow her to glimpse anything properly. She squinted and stood on her tiptoes, frowning. The shape. She almost thought it had a halo. A yellow or golden coloring, like light refracted for a quick second...</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">It lives in the cemetery, Catalina said. The words had not frightened her. But now, standing outside, with only a packet of cigarettes and a lighter, she felt exposed and vulnerable, and she couldn’t help but wonder exactly what lived in the cemetery.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Slugs, worms, and beetles, and nothing more, she told her- self, sliding her hand into her pocket, clasping her lighter like a talisman. The shape, gray and lacking definition, a blur of darkness against the mist, did not move toward Noemi. It re- mained still. It might be nothing but a statue. A trick of the light might have made it appear as if it were moving.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Yes, no doubt it had been a trick of the light, just like with the quickly glimpsed halo. She moved away, eager to retrace her steps and head back to the house.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She heard a rustling in the grass and, turning her head sharply, she noticed the shape had vanished. It couldn’t have been a statue.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She was suddenly, unpleasantly, aware of a buzzing, almost like a beehive but not quite. It was loud. Or no, loud was not the right word. She could hear it very clearly. Like the echo in an empty room it seemed to bounce back to her.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">It lives in the cemetery.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She ought to get back to the house. It was that way, to her right.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The mist, which had seemed insubstantial and thin as she swung the gate open, had thickened. Noemi tried to think</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_91" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 91 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164429_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">hard whether it was to her right or her left that she should head. She did not want to end up following a wrong trail and bumping into a mountain lion or sliding off a ravine.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">It lives in the cemetery.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Right, it was definitely right. The buzzing was also toward the right. Bees or wasps. Well, what if there were bees? It wasn’t as if they’d sting her. She wasn’t going to paw at their hive to get honey.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The sound, though. It was unpleasant. It made her want to go in the opposite direction. Buzzing. Maybe it was flies. Flies as green as emeralds, their fat bodies atop a piece of car- rion. Meat, red and raw, and really, why must she think these things? Why must she stand like this, with a hand in her pocket and her eyes wide, anxiously listening...</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">You must look in the cemetery.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Left, go left. Toward that mist, which seemed even thicker there, thick as gruel.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">There came the crunching of a twig under a shoe and a voice, pleasant and warm in the coldness of the cemetery.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Out for a stroll?” Francis asked.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He wore a gray turtleneck and a navy coat and a matching navy cap. A basket dangled from his right arm. He always seemed rather insubstantial to Noemi, but now, in the mist, he appeared perfectly solid and real. It was exactly what she needed.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Oh, I could kiss you, I’m so happy to see you,” she said merrily.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He blushed as red as a pomegranate, which was not becom- ing and was also, frankly, a little funny because he was a bit older than her, a man made. If anyone should play the bashful maid, it should be her. Then again she supposed there weren’t many young women fawning over Francis in this place.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She figured if she ever got him to a party in Mexico City he would be utterly thrilled or petrified, only one of two ex- tremes.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“T’m not sure I’ve done anything to deserve that,” he said, half mumbling the words.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You deserve it. I can’t seem to figure out where is what in this mist. I was thinking I’d have to spin around and hope there wasn’t a gully nearby and I’d go tumbling right into it. Can you see anything? And do you know the location of the cemetery gates?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Of course I know it,” he said. “It’s not that hard if you look down. There’re all kinds of visual markers to guide you.”</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_92" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 92 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164433_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“It feels like having a veil thrust over your eyes,” she de- clared. “I also fear there are bees nearby and they might sting me. I heard a buzzing.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He glanced down, nodding, looking at his basket. Now that he was with her, she had regained her levity, and she peered curiously at him.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“What do you have there?” she asked, pointing at the bas- ket.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“T’ve been collecting mushrooms.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Mushrooms? At a cemetery?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Sure. They’re all around.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“As long as you don’t plan to make them into a salad,” she said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“What would be wrong about that?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Only the thought of them growing over dead things!”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“But then mushrooms always grow over dead things in a way.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I can’t believe you are strolling around in this fog hunting for mushrooms growing on graves. It sounds grim, like you’re a body snatcher from a nineteenth-century dime novel.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Catalina would have liked that. Perhaps she’d gone mush- room hunting in the cemetery too. Or else she simply stood in this same spot, smiling wistfully as the wind toyed with her hair. Books, moonlight, melodrama.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Me?” he asked.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Yes. I bet you havea skull in there. You’re a character out of Horacio Quiroga’s stories. Let me see.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He had draped a red handkerchief over the basket, which he removed to allow her to inspect the mushrooms. They were a bright, fleshy orange with intricate folds, soft as velvet. She held a small one between her index finger and her thumb.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Cantharellus cibarius. They’re quite delicious, and they were not growing in the cemetery, but farther away. I merely cut through here to get back home. Locals call them duraznil- los. Smell them.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi leaned down closer to the basket. “They smell sweet.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“They’re quite lovely too. There’s an important connection between certain cultures and mushrooms, you know? The Zapotec Indians of your country practiced dentistry by giving people a mushroom which would intoxicate them and serve as an anesthetic. And the Aztecs, they too found mushrooms interesting. They consumed them in order to experience vi- sions.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Teonanacatl,” she said. “The flesh of the gods.”</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_93" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 93 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164438_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He spoke eagerly. “You know about fungi, then?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“No, not really: history. I was thinking of becoming a his- torian before I detoured and settled on anthropology. At least that is the plan now.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“T see. Well, I’d love to find those little, dark mushrooms that the Aztecs ate.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Why, you don’t strike me as that type of boy,” Noemi said, handing him back the orange mushroom.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“What do you mean?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“They’re supposed to make you feel drunk and induce a great lust. At least that’s what the Spaniard chroniclers said. Do you plan on snacking on them and going on a date?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“No, well, I would not want them, not for that,” Francis quickly babbled.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi liked to flirt and she was good at it, but she could tell by the renewed redness in Francis’s cheeks that he was a nov- ice at this. Had he ever gone dancing? She couldn't picture him heading to town for a festivity, nor could she see him sneaking kisses in a darkened cinema in Pachuca, though Pachuca was unlikely in the first place since he’d never traveled far. Who knew. Maybe she’d kiss him before the trip was over, and he would be utterly shocked by the gesture.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">But then, she was discovering she actually enjoyed his company and didn’t wish to torture the young man.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“‘’m joking. My grandmother was Mazatec, and the Mazatec ingest similar mushrooms during certain cere- monies. It’s not about lust, it’s communion. They say the mushroom speaks to you. I understand your interest in them.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Why, yes,” he said. “The world is filled with so many extra- ordinary wonders, isn’t it? You could spend a lifetime peer- ing in forests and jungles and never see one tenth of nature’s secrets.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He sounded so excited, it was a bit funny. She didn’t have a naturalist bone in her body, but rather than judging his pas- sion ridiculous she was moved by his fervor. He had such life when he spoke like that.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Do you like all plants, or are your botanical interests limited to mushrooms?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“T like all plants and have pressed a large share of flowers, leaves, ferns, and such. But mushrooms are more interesting. I make spore prints and draw a little,” he said, looking pleased.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“What’s a spore print?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You place the gills on a paper surface and they leave an impression of it. It’s used to help identify mushrooms. And</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_94" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 94 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164443_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">the botanical illustrations, they are beautiful. Such colors. I might...perhaps...”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Perhaps what?” she asked when he didn’t continue.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He clutched the red piece of cloth in his left hand tightly. “Perhaps you'd like to see the prints sometime? I’m sure it doesn’t sound exciting, but if you find yourself terribly bored it might be a distraction.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I would like to, thanks,” she said, helping him out since he seemed to have lost all knowledge of words and was looking mutely at the ground, as if the right sentence might sprout there.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He smiled at her, carefully setting the red handkerchief back on top of the mushrooms. The mist had thinned as they spoke, and now she could see the tombstones, the trees, and the shrubs.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“At last, Iam no longer blind,” Noemi said. “There is sun- light! And air.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Yes. You could make your way back on your own,” he replied, a note of disenchantment in his voice as he looked around. “Although you could keep me company for a while longer. If you are not too busy,” he added cautiously.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She had been eager to leave the cemetery a few minutes before, but now it was all peaceful and quiet. Even the mist seemed pleasant. She couldn’t believe she’d been scared. Why, the figure she had seen must have been Francis walking around and poking at the ground for mushrooms.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I might have a cigarette,” she said and lit one with nimble fingers. She offered him the pack, but Francis shook his head.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“My mother wants to have a chat with you about that,” he said, looking serious.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Is she going to tell me smoking is a filthy habit again?” Noemi asked as she took a puff and tilted her head up. She liked doing that. It enhanced her long, elegant neck, which was among her finest features and made her feel a bit like a movie star. Hugo Duarte and all the other boys fawning around her certainly thought it was a charming detail.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She was vain, yes. Though she didn’t think it was a sin. Noemi looked a bit like Katy Jurado when she struck the right pose, and of course she knew what exact pose and angle to strike. But she’d abandoned the theater classes. Nowadays she wished to be a Ruth Benedict or a Margaret Mead.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Maybe. The family insists on certain healthy habits. No smoking, no coffee, no loud music or noises, cold showers, closed curtains, mild words and—”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Why?”</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_95" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 95 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164447_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“It’s the way it’s always been done at High Place,” Francis said blandly.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“The cemetery sounds more lively,” she said. “Maybe we should fill a flask with whiskey and have a party here, under that pine tree. I’ll blow smoke rings your way, and we can try and find those hallucinogenic mushrooms. And if it turns out they do indeed induce a sort of lust or frenzy and you get fresh with me, I won’t mind one bit.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She was joking. Anybody would have known it was a joke. Her tone of voice possessed the dramatic intonation that takes place when a woman is giving a grand performance. But he took it all wrong, no longer blushing but paling.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He shook his head. “My mother, she would say that’s wrong, to suggest...it’s wrong...”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He trailed off, though it was not necessary that he elabor- ate. He seemed plainly disgusted.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She pictured him talking to his mother, whispering, the word filth on his lips and both of them nodding, agreeing. Su- perior and inferior types, and Noemi did not belong in the first category, did not belong in High Place, did not deserve any- thing but scorn.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I don’t care what your mother thinks, Francis,” Noemi said as she dropped the cigarette and crushed it under the heel of her shoe with two vicious stomps. She began walking briskly. “Tm headed back. You’re a complete bore.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">A few steps later she stopped and crossed her arms, turn- ing around.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He had followed her and was close behind.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi took a deep breath. “Let me be. I don’t need you to show me the way.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Francis bent down and carefully picked up a mushroom that she had accidentally trampled over in her mad dash to- ward the cemetery gates. It was satiny-white, the stem had broken off from the cap, and he held both in the palm of his hand.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“A destroying angel,” he muttered.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Sorry?” she asked, confused.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“A poisonous mushroom. Its spore print is white, which is how you can distinguish it from an edible one.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He placed the mushroom back on the ground and stood up, brushing away the dirt from his trousers. “I must seem ri- diculous to you,” he said quietly. “A ridiculous fool clutching his mother’s skirts. You’d be right too. I dare not do anything to upset her, or to upset Great Uncle Howard. Especially Uncle Howard.”</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_96" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 96 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164452_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He looked at her, and she realized that the contempt in his eyes hadn’t been meant for her, but for himself. She felt awful, and she remembered how Catalina had told her she was cap- able of leaving deep scars in people if she didn’t watch her scalding tongue.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">For all your intelligence, you don’t think sometimes, Catalina had said. How true. There she was, making stories up in her head when he had said nothing cruel to her.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“No. I’m so sorry, Francis. I’m the fool, a court jester,” Noemi said, attempting a sort of lightness in the delivery, hop- ing he understood she hadn’t meant it, that they might laugh this silly quarrel away.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He nodded slowly, but did not seem convinced. She stretched out a hand, touching his fingers, which were dirty from handling the mushrooms.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“IT really am sorry,” she said, this time eradicating any flip- pancy.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He looked at her with great solemnity and his fingers tight- ened around hers, and he gave her a little tug, as if to pull her closer to him. But just as quickly he released her and stepped back, grabbing the red cloth he carried atop his basket and handing it to her.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I’m afraid I’ve dirtied you,” he said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Yes,”—Noemi looked at her hand, smudged with soil—‘I guess you have.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She wiped her hands clean on the cloth and handed it back to him. Francis tucked it in a pocket and set the basket down.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You should go back,” he said, glancing aside. “I still need to collect more mushrooms.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi didn’t know if he was telling the truth or was still upset and merely wanted her gone. She couldn't blame him if he was sore with her. “Very well. Don’t let the mist swallow you,” she said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She reached the cemetery’s gate soon enough and swung it open. Noemi looked over her shoulder and saw a figure at a distance, Francis with his basket, the curling wisps of mist making his features vague. Yes, he must have been the silhou- ette she had spied in the cemetery earlier, and yet she felt it couldn't have been him.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Maybe it was a destroying angel of a different kind, Noemi mused, and immediately regretted such an odd, morbid thought. Really, what was wrong with her today?</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She retraced her steps, followed the trail back to High Place. When she walked into the kitchen she found Charles sweep- ing the floor with an old broom. Noemi smiled in greeting.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_97" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 97 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164459_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Just then, Florence walked in. She wore a gray dress, a double strand of pearls and her hair up. When she caught sight of Noemi she clasped her hands together.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Finally, there you are. Where have you been? I’ve been looking for you.” Florence frowned, glancing down. “You're trailing mud inside. Take off those shoes.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“l’m sorry,” Noemi said, looking down at her high heels, which were crusted with dirt and blades of grass. She took them off, holding them in her hands.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Charles, take those and clean them,” Florence ordered the man.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I can do it. It’s no problem.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Let him.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Charles put the broom aside and walked toward her, ex- tending his hands. “Miss,” he said, the one word.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Oh,” Noemi replied and handed him the shoes. He took them, grabbed a brush that lay upon a shelf, and sat on a stool in the corner of the room and began brushing the dirt off her high heels.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Your cousin was asking for you,” Florence said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Is she all right?” Noemi asked, immediately worried.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“She’s fine. She was bored and wanted to converse with you.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I can go upstairs right away,” Noemi said, her stockinged feet moving quickly against the cold floor.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“There’s no need for that,” Florence said. “She’s taking her nap now.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi had already stepped into the hallway. She looked back at Florence, who was walking toward her, and shrugged. “Perhaps you could go up later?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Yes, I will,” Noemi said, but she felt deflated and a little bad for not having been around when Catalina wanted her.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_98" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 98 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164504_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">n the mornings Florence or one of the maids came by to</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">deliver Noemi’s breakfast tray. She had tried to converse</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">with the maids, but they did not offer anything but</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">curt yeses and nos to Noemi. In fact, when she encoun- tered any of the staff of High Place—Lizzie or Charles or the eldest of the three servants, Mary—they inclined their head and kept walking, as if they were pretending Noemi did not exist.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The house, so quiet, with its curtains drawn, was like a dress lined with lead. Everything was heavy, even the air, and a musty scent lingered along the hallways. It felt almost as if it were a temple, a church, where one must speak in low voices and genuflect, and she supposed the servants had accli- matized to this environment and therefore tiptoed along the staircase, unwilling nuns who had made vows of silence.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">That morning, however, the quiet routine of Mary or Flor- ence knocking a single time, walking in, and setting her tray on a table was interrupted. There were three knocks, three soft rapping noises. No one walked in, and when the knocks were repeated Noemi opened the door to find Francis there, bearing her tray.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Good morning,” he said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He quite surprised her. She smiled. “Hello. Are you short staffed today?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I offered to help my mother deliver this, since she’s busy seeing about Great Uncle Howard. He’s had pain in his leg last night, and when he does he’s in a foul mood. Where should I set it down?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Over there,” Noemi said, stepping aside and pointing at the table.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Francis carefully set the tray down. Afterward he slid his hands into his pockets and cleared his throat.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I was wondering if you'd be interested in looking at those spore prints today. If you have nothing better to do, that is.”</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_99" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 99 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164509_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">This was a great chance for Noemi to ask him for a ride, she thought. A bit of socializing and he was sure to do as she said. She needed to go into town.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Let me talk to my secretary. I have a very full social agenda,” Noemi replied cheekily.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He smiled. “Should we meet in the library? In, say, an hour?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Very well.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">It was close to a social outing, this trek to the library, and it invigorated her, for she was an eager social creature. Noemi changed into a polka-dot day dress with a square neck. She had misplaced the matching bolero jacket and really she ought to have been wearing white gloves with it, but their location being what it was, it was not as if a little faux pas like that would be noticed and make it into the society pages.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">As she brushed her hair she wondered what everyone was up to back in the city. No doubt her brother was still behaving like a baby with that broken foot of his, Roberta was probably trying to psychoanalyze their whole circle of friends as she always did, and she was sure by now Hugo Duarte had found a new girl to take to recitals and parties. The thought needled her for a second. Hugo was, truth be told, a good dancer and decent company during social functions.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">As she descended the stairs she was amused by the thought of a party at High Place. No music, of course. The dancing would have to take place in silence, and everyone would be dressed in gray and black, as if attending a funeral.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The hallway that led to the library was profusely decorated with photographs of the Doyles rather than paintings, as was the case on the second floor. It was hard, however, to look at them because the hallway was kept in semi-darkness. She’d need to hold a flashlight or candle to them to see anything properly. Noemi had an idea. She walked into the library and the office and pulled the curtains aside. The light filtering out through the open doors of these rooms lit up a stretch of wall, providing her a chance to examine the images.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She looked at all those strange faces that seemed neverthe- less familiar, echoes of Florence and Virgil and Francis. She recognized Alice, in a pose very similar to the one she sported in the portrait above Howard Doyle’s fireplace, and Howard himself, rendered young, his face devoid of wrinkles.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_100" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 100 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164515_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">There was a woman, her hands tightly held in her lap, her light hair pinned up, who regarded Noemi with large eyes from her picture frame. She looked about Noemi’s age, and perhaps it was that, or the way her mouth was set so hard, somewhat between aggrieved and doleful, that made Noemi lean closer to the photograph, her fingers hovering above it.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I hope I haven’t kept you waiting,” Francis said as he ap- proached her, a wooden box under one arm and a book under the other.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“No, not at all,” Noemi said. “Do you know who this is here?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Francis looked at the picture she was examining. He cleared his throat. “That is...that was my cousin Ruth.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I’ve heard about her.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi had never seen the face of a killer; she wasn’t in the habit of perusing the newspapers for stories about criminals. She recalled what Virgil had said, about people being bound to their vices and how their faces reflected their nature. But the woman in the photograph seemed merely discontent, not murderous.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“What have you heard?” Francis asked.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“She killed several people, and herself.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi straightened herself up and turned to him. He placed the box on the floor, and his expression was distant.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Her cousin, Michael.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Francis pointed at a picture of a young man standing ram- rod straight, the chain of a pocket watch glinting against his chest, his hair neatly parted, a pair of gloves in his left hand, his eyes rendered almost colorless in the sepia photograph.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Francis pointed at the photograph showing Alice, who looked like Agnes. “Her mother.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">His hand darted between two pictures, a woman with her light hair swept up and a man in a dark jacket. “Dorothy and Leland. Her aunt and uncle, my grandparents.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He went quiet. There was nothing more to say; the litany of the dead had been recited. Michael and Alice, Dorothy, Leland, and Ruth, all of them resting in that elegant mausoleum, the coffins gathering cobwebs and dust. The thought of the party without music, the funerary clothes, now seemed extremely morbid and apt.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Why did she do it?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I wasn't born when it happened,” Francis said quickly, turning his head away.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Yes, they must have told you something, there must have been—”</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_101" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 101 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164520_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I told you, I wasn’t even born then. Who knows? This place could drive anyone crazy,” he said angrily.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">His voice sounded loud in this quiet space of faded wall- paper and gilded frames; it seemed to bounce off the walls and return to them, scraping their skin with its harshness, almost booming. It startled her, this acoustic effect, and it also seemed to affect him. He hunched his shoulders, shrank down, trying to make himself smaller.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I’m sorry,” he said. “I shouldn’t raise my voice like that. The sound carries here, and I’m being very rude.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“No, I’m being rude. I can understand you wouldn’t want to talk about such a thing.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Another time, perhaps I might tell you about it,” he said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">His voice was now velvet soft and so was the silence that settled around them. She wondered if the gunshots had boomed through the house just as Francis’s voice had boomed, leaving a trail of echoes, and then the same plush silence.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">You have a devious mind, Noemi, she chided herself. No won- der you dream awful things.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Yes. Well, what about those prints you have with you?” she told him, because she did not want any more grimness.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">They went into the library, and he spread on a table before her the treasures he carted in the box. Sheets of paper with brown, black, and purplish blotches upon the surface. They reminded her of the Rorschach images Roberta—the same friend who swore on Jung—had shown her. These were more accurate; there was no subjective meaning to be assigned. These prints told a story, as clear as her name written on a chalkboard.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He also showed her pressed plants, lovingly collected be- tween the pages of a book. Ferns, roses, daisies, dried and catalogued with an immaculate handwriting that put Noemi’s shabby penmanship to shame. She thought their mother su- perior would have adored Francis, with his neatness and his organized spirit.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She informed him of this, told him how the nuns at her school would have fussed about him.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“TI always got stuck at ‘I believe in the Holy Spirit,” she said. “I couldn't name its symbols. There was the dove and maybe a cloud and holy water, and oh, then I’d forget.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Fire, which transforms whatever it touches,” Francis said helpfully.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I told you, the nuns would have loved you.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I’m sure they liked you.”</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_102" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 102 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164524_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“No. Everyone says they like me well enough, but that’s be- cause they have to. No one is going to declare they hate Noemi Taboada. It would be crass to state such a thing while you're nibbling at a canapé. You have to whisper it in the foyer.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Then, in Mexico City, at your parties, you spend the whole time feeling people don’t like you?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I spend the time drinking good champagne, dear boy,” she said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Of course.” He chuckled, leaning against the table and looking down at his spore prints. “Your life must be exciting.” “I don’t know about that. I have a good time, I suppose.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Aside from the parties, what do you do?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Well, I’m attending university, so that eats up much of my day. But you’re asking what I doin my spare time? I like music. I often get tickets to the Philharmonic. Chavez, Revueltas, Lara, there’s so much good music to listen to. I even play alittle piano myself.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Do you, truly?” he asked, looking dazzled. “That is amaz- ing.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I don't play with the Philharmonic.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Yes, well, it still sounds exciting.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“It’s not. It’s so dull. All those years of scales and trying to hit the right keys. I’m so dull!” she declared, as one must. To seem too eager about anything was a little vulgar.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You aren't. Not at all,” he assured her quickly.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You're not supposed to say that. Not like that. You sound much too honest. Don’t you know anything?” she asked.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He shrugged, as if apologizing, unable to match her high spirits. He was bashful and a little odd. And she liked him ina different way than she liked the bold boys she knew, different than Hugo Duarte, whom she liked mostly because he danced well and resembled Pedro Infante. This was a warmer feeling, more genuine.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You think me spoiled now,” she said and allowed herself to sound rueful, because she actually did want him to like her, and it wasn’t for show.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Not at all,” he replied, again with that disarming honesty, as he leaned down on the table and fiddled with a couple of spore prints.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She rested her elbows on the table and leaned forward, smiling, until her eyes were level with his. They looked at each other.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You'll think it in a minute because I have to ask you for a favor,” she said, unable to forget the question she had on her mind.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_103" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 103 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164529_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“What?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I want to go into town tomorrow, and your mother said I can’t take the car. I was thinking you might give me a ride there and pick me up, say, a couple of hours later.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You want me to drop you off in town.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Yes.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He looked away, evading her gaze. “My mother will not have it. She’ll say you need a chaperone.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Are you going to chaperone me?” Noemi asked. “I’m not a child.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“TI know.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Francis slowly walked around the table, stopping close to her and leaning down, inspecting one of the plant specimens on display. His fingers brushed lightly over a fern.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“They’ve asked me to keep an eye on you,” he said, his voice low. “They say you’re reckless.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I suppose you agree and you think I need a babysitter,” she replied, scoffing.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I think you can be reckless. But maybe I can ignore them this one time,” he said, almost whispering, his head lowered as if to reveal a secret. “We should leave early tomorrow, around eight o’clock, before they’re up and about. And don’t tell any- one we're going out.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I won't. Thank you.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“It is nothing,” he replied and turned his head to look at Noemi.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">This time his gaze lingered on her for one long minute before, skittish, he stepped back and rounded the table again, returning to his original position. A bundle of nerves is what he was.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">A heart, raw and bleeding, she thought, and the image lin- gered in her mind. The anatomical heart, like in the Loteria cards, red, with all veins and arteries, rendered bright crim- son. What was the saying? Do not miss me, sweetheart, I’ll be back by bus. Yes, she had spent many lazy afternoons playing Loteria with her cousins, declaiming the popular rhyme that went with each card as they played and made their bets.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Don’t miss me, sweetheart.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Could she get Loteria cards in town? It might give her and Catalina an activity to pass the time. It would be something they’d done before, which would conjure memories of more pleasant days.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The door to the library opened and Florence walked in, Liz- zie following her behind with a pail and a rag. Florence’s gaze</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_104" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 104 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164534_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">swept across the room, coldly assessing Noemi and settling on her son.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Mother. I didn’t realize you’d be cleaning the library today,” Francis said, quickly standing up straight and shoving his hands in his pockets.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You know how it is, Francis. If we do not keep on top of things they fall apart. While a few may consign themselves to idleness, others must observe their duties.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Yes, of course,” Francis said and began gathering his things.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“T’d be happy to watch over Catalina while you do your cleaning,” Noemi offered.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“She’s resting. Mary is with her, anyway. There’s no need for you.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Still, I’d like to make myself useful, as you say,” she de- clared, uttering a challenge. She wasn’t going to let Florence complain that she did nothing.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Follow me.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Before Noemi exited the library, she glanced over her shoulder and shot Francis a smile. Florence marched Noemi into the dining room and gestured to the display cases crammed with silver.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You were interested in these items. Perhaps you might polish them,” she said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The Doyles’ silver collection was quite staggering, each shelf lined with salvers, tea sets, bowls, and candlesticks that sat dusty and dull behind glass. A lone person could not hope to tackle this whole task alone, but Noemi was determined to prove herself in front of this woman.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“If you give me arag and some polish, I'll do it.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Since the dining room was very dark, Noemi had to light several lamps and candles in order to see exactly what she was doing. Then she set about meticulously working the pol- ish into every crevice and curve, sliding the rag over enameled vines and flowers. A sugar bow] proved to be exceedingly diffi- cult, but for the most part she managed well.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">When Florence returned, many pieces lay gleaming on the table. Noemi was carefully polishing one of two curious cups that were shaped in the form of stylized mushrooms. The base of the cups was decorated with tiny leaves and even a beetle. Maybe Francis could tell her if this was based on a real mush- room species and which one.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Florence stood there, watching Noemi. “You're industri- ous.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Like a little bee, when I feel like it,” Noemi replied.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_105" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 105 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164538_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Florence approached the table, running her hands over the items Noemi had polished. She picked one of the cups up and spun it between her fingers, inspecting it. “You expect to win my praise this way, I think. It would take more.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Your respect, perhaps. Not your praise.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Why would you need my respect?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I don't.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Florence set the cup down and clasped her hands together, her eyes admiring the metal objects, almost reverently. Noemi had to admit it was a bit overwhelming to look at so many glittering riches on display, though it seemed a pity they had all been locked away, dusty and forgotten. What good were mountains of silver if you didn’t use them? And the people in the town, they had so little. They didn’t keep silver locked in cabinets.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Most of this is made from silver from our mines,” Flor- ence said. “Do you have any idea how much silver our mine produced? God, it was dizzying! My uncle brought all the ma- chinery, all the knowledge to dredge it from the dark. Doyle is an important name. I don’t think you realize how lucky your cousin is to be part of our family now. To be a Doyle is to be someone.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi thought of the rows of old pictures in the hallway, the dilapidated household with its dusty alcoves. And what did she mean that to be a Doyle meant to be someone? Did that make Catalina no one before she came to High Place? And was Noemi therefore confined to a faceless, luckless band of nobodies?</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Florence must have noticed the skepticism in Noemi’s face, and she fixed her eyes on the young woman. “What do you talk about with my son?” the woman asked abruptly, clasping her hands again. “Back there, in the library. What were you talking about?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Spore prints.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“That’s all?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Well, I can’t recall it all. But now, yes, spore prints.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You talk about the city, perhaps.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Sometimes.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">If Howard reminded her of an insect, then Florence brought to mind an insectivorous plant about to swallow a fly. Noemi’s brother had once owned a Venus flytrap. It had scared her a little, as a child.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Don’t give my son any ideas. They will bring him pain. Francis is content here. He doesn’t need to hear about parties,</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_106" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 106 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164543_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">music, and booze, and whatever other frivolities you choose to share with him about Mexico City.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“T’ll be sure to discuss with him the preapproved subjects you dictate. Perhaps we can erase all the cities on the terres- trial globe and pretend they don’t exist,” Noemi said, because although Florence was intimidating, she was not willing to hide in a corner, like a baby.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You're very cheeky,” Florence said. “And you think you have a special power simply because my uncle thinks you pos- sess a pretty face. But that’s not power. It’s a liability.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Florence leaned over the table, looking at a large, rectangu- lar serving tray with a border of stylized floral wreaths. Flor- ence’s face, reflected on the silver surface, was elongated and deformed. She ran a finger around the tray’s border, touching the flowers.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“When I was younger, I thought the world outside held such promise and wonders. I even went away for a bit and met a dashing young man.
