I thought he’d take me away, that he would change everything, change me,” Florence said, and her face softened for the briefest moment. “But there’s no denying our natures. I was meant to live and die in High Place. Let Fran- cis be. He’s accepted his lot in this life. It’s easier that way.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Florence fixed her blue eyes on Noemi. “I’ll put away the silver, no further assistance is required,” she declared, ending their conversation abruptly.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi went back to her room. She thought about all the times Catalina had narrated fairy tales. Once upon a time there was a princess in a tower, once upon a time a prince saved the girl from the tower. Noemi sat on the bed and con- templated the notion of enchantments that are never broken.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_107" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 107 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164547_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">oemi heard a heart beating, as loud as a drum, calling for her. It woke her up. Carefully she ventured outside her room to find the place where it was hiding. She felt it be- neath her palm, when she pressed her hand against the walls; felt the wallpaper grow slippery, like a strained muscle, and the floor beneath her was wet and soft. It was a sore. A great sore she walked upon, and the walls were sores too. The wall- paper was peeling, revealing underneath sickly organs instead of brick or wooden boards. Veins and arteries clogged with se- cret excesses.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She followed the beating of the heart and a thread of red on the carpet. Like a gash. A line of crimson. A line of blood. Until she stopped in the middle of the hallway and saw the woman staring back at her.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Ruth, the girl from the photograph. Ruth, in a white dress- ing gown, her hair like a golden halo, her face bloodless. A slim, alabaster pillar in the darkness of the house. Ruth held a rifle between her hands and she stared at Noemi.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">They began walking together, side by side. Their move- ments were perfectly synchronized; even their breathing was identical. Ruth brushed a lock of hair away from her face and Noemi brushed her hair too.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The walls around them were glowing, a dim phosphor- escence that nevertheless guided their steps, and the carpet underneath their feet was squishy. She noticed, too, markings on the walls—walls that were made of flesh. Traceries of fuzzy mold, as if the house were an overripe fruit.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The heart kept beating faster.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The heart pumped blood and groaned and shivered, and it beat so loudly Noemi thought she’d go deaf.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Ruth opened a door. Noemi grit her teeth because this was the source of the noise, the beating heart lay inside.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_108" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 108 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164552_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The door swung open, and Noemi saw aman on a bed. Only it wasn’t truly aman. It was a bloated vision of aman, as if he’d drowned and floated to the surface, his pale body lined with blue veins, tumors flowering on his legs, his hands, his belly. A pustule, not a man, a living, breathing, pustule. His chest ris- ing and falling.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The man could not possibly be alive but he was, and when Ruth opened the door he sat up in bed and extended his arms toward her, as if demanding an embrace. Noemi remained by the doorway, but Ruth approached the bed.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The man extended his hands, his greedy fingers quivering, while the girl stood at the foot of the bed and stared at him.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Ruth raised her rifle, and Noemi turned her head away. She did not want to see. But even as she turned she heard the hor- rid noise of the rifle, the muffled scream of the man followed by athroaty moan.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He must be dead, she thought. He has to be.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She looked at Ruth, who had walked past Noemi and was now standing in the hallway, and the young woman looked back at Noemi.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I’m not sorry,” Ruth said, and she pressed the rifle against her chin and pulled the trigger.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">There was blood, the dark splatter marking the wall. Noemi watched Ruth fall, her body bending like the stem of a flower. The suicide, however, did not unnerve Noemi. She felt that this was the way things should be; she felt soothed, she even thought to smile.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">But the smile froze on Noemi’s face when she saw the fig- ure standing at the end of the hallway, watching her. It was a golden blur, it was the woman with the blur of a face, her whole body rippling, liquid, rushing toward Noemi with a huge open mouth—although she had no mouth—ready to un- leash a terrible scream. Ready to eat her alive.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">And now Noemi was afraid, now she knew terror, and she raised her palms to desperately ward off—</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">A firm hand on her arm made Noemi jump back.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Noemi,” Virgil said. She looked quickly behind her, then back at him, trying to make sense of what had happened.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She was standing in the middle of a hallway, and he stood in front of her, holding an oil lamp in his right hand. It was long and ornate, and its glass was milky green.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi stared at him, speechless. The golden creature had been there a second before, but now it was gone! Gone, and in its stead it was him, wearing a plush velvet robe with a pattern of golden vines running up the fabric.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_109" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 109 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164557_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She was in her nightgown. It was supposed to be part of a gown-peignoir set, but she was not wearing the cover-up. Her arms were bare. She felt exposed and she was cold. She rubbed her arms.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“What’s happening?” she asked.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Noemi,” he repeated, her name so smooth on his lips, like a piece of silk. “You were sleepwalking. One is not supposed to wake a sleepwalker. They say it can cause the sleeping per- son a great shock. But I was worried you'd hurt yourself. Did I frighten you?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She did not understand his question. It took her a minute to comprehend what he was saying.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She shook her head. “No. That’s quite impossible. I haven't done that in years. Not since I was a child.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Maybe you hadn't noticed.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I would’ve noticed.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“T’ve been following you for a few minutes now, trying to decide whether to shake you awake or not.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I wasn’t sleepwalking.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Then I must have been mistaken, and you were simply walking around in the dark,” he said coolly.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">God, she felt stupid, standing there in her nightgown, gawping at him. She did not want to argue with him; there was no point in it. He was right, and besides, she dearly wished to get back to her room. It was too cold and dark in this hall- way; she could hardly see anything. They could be sitting in the belly of a beast for all she knew.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">In the nightmare, they had been in a belly, had they not? No. A cage made of organs. Walls of flesh. That’s what she had seen, and who knew. If she tried to touch the wall right now it might ripple beneath her palm. She ran a hand through her hair.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Fine. Maybe I was sleepwalking. But—”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She heard it then, a throaty moan like in her dream, low but undeniable. It made her jump again, jump back. She almost collided with him.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“What was that?” she asked and looked down the hallway, then turned to stare at him anxiously.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“My father is ill. It’s an old wound which never quite healed and pains him. He’s been having a rough night,” he said, look- ing very composed, adjusting the flame of the oil lamp, mak- ing it bloom a little brighter. She could now see the wallpaper, its drawing of flowers, faint traces of mold marring its surface.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">No veins pumping through the walls.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_110" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 110 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164602_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Damn it, and yes, Francis had told her something similar earlier that day, about Howard being ill. But was she in that area of the house, close to the old man’s bed? So far from her room? She thought she might have taken a few paces outside her door, not wandered from one side of the house to the other.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You should call for a doctor.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Like I explained, sometimes it pains him. We are used to it. When Dr. Cummins stops by for his weekly visit he can exam- ine him, but my father is simply an old man. I’m sorry if he startled you.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Old, yes; 1885 was when they arrived in Mexico. Even if Howard Doyle had been a young man back then, almost sev- enty years had passed. And how old was he, exactly? Ninety? Closer to a hundred? He must have been an old man already when he had Virgil. She rubbed her arms again.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Here, you must be cold,” he said, setting the oil lamp down on the floor and untying his robe.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I’m fine.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Put this on.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He took off the robe, placing it around her shoulders. It was too large. He was tall and she was not. It never bothered her much, tall men. She simply looked them up and down. But she did not feel very confident right this second, still unnerved by that ridiculous dream. Noemi crossed her arms and looked down at the carpet.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He picked up the oil lamp. “T’ll walk you back to your room.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You don’t have to.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I do. Otherwise you'll be liable to hurt your shins in the dark. And it’s quite dark.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He was, once again, correct. The few wall sconces with working bulbs gave off a dim light, but there were large pools of blackness in between them. The glow of Virgil’s lamp was an eerie green, but on the other hand she felt grateful for its more potent illumination. This house, she was sure, was haunted. She wasn’t one for believing in things that go bump in the night either, but right that second she firmly felt every spook and demon and evil thing might be crawling about the Earth, like in Catalina’s stories.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He was quiet as they walked, and even if the silence of the house was unpleasant, and the creaking of each board made her wince, it was better than having to talk to him. She simply could not converse at a time like this.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">I’m a baby, she thought. Boy, would her brother laugh at her if he saw her. She could picture him, telling everyone</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_111" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 111 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164606_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi now practically believed in el coco. The memory of her brother, her family, Mexico City, it was good. It warmed her better than the robe.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">When they got to her room, she finally felt at ease. She was back. All was well. She opened the door.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“If you want, you can keep this,” he said, pointing to the lamp.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“No. Then you'd be the one hitting your shins in the dark. Give mea minute,” she said, reaching for the top of the dresser, by the door, where she'd left the gaudy silver candelabra with the cherub. She grabbed the box of matches and lit the candle.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Let there be light. See? It’s fine.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She began to take the robe off. Virgil stilled her, setting a hand on her shoulder and then carefully running his fingers down the edge of the wide lapel. “You look very fine in my clothes,” he said with that voice that was made of silk.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The comment was mildly inappropriate. In the daylight, with other people, it might have been a joke. At night, and the way he said it, it didn’t seem at all decent. And yet, though subtly wrong, she found herself unable to reply. Don’t be silly, she thought to say. Or even, I don’t want your clothes. But she didn’t say a thing, because it wasn’t really that bad of a com- ment, a few words, and she didn’t wish to start a fight in the middle of a dark hallway over what amounted to almost, but not quite, nothing.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Well, good night, then,” he said, unhurriedly releasing the lapel and taking a step back.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He held the oil lamp at eye level and smiled at her. Virgil was an attractive man and the smile was a pleasant smile —almost teasing, in a good-humored way—but there was an edge to his expression that the smile could not mask. She did not like it. She was suddenly reminded of her dream, and she thought of the man in the bed holding his arms outstretched, and she thought there was a golden cast to his eyes, a glimmer of gold among the blue. She turned her head abruptly, blinking and staring at the floor.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You won't bid me good night?” he asked, sounding amused. “Nor grant me a thank-you? It would be rude not to.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She turned toward him, looking at him in the eye. “Thanks,” she said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Better lock your door so you don’t end up wandering around the house again, Noemi.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He adjusted the level of the oil lamp once more. His eyes were blue without a hint of gold when he gave her a final glance and stepped away, marching back down the hallway.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_112" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 112 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164612_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi watched the green glow float away with him, the sud- den flash of color disappearing and plunging the house into darkness.</p>
      <h2 style="color:#2d3748; margin:1.5rem 0 0.75rem 0; font-size:1.4rem;">1 hr 52 mins left in book 39%</h2>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_113" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 113 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164617_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">t’s funny how daylight could change her mind so ut-</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">terly. At night, after her sleepwalking episode, Noemi</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">had been scared, pulling the covers up to her chin. Con-</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">templating the sky through her window, scratching her left wrist, she found the whole episode embarrassing and pro- saic.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Her room, when viewed with the curtains wide open and the sun streaming in, was worn and sad, but couldn't conceal ghosts or monsters. Hauntings and curses, bah! She dressed in a long-sleeved button-down blouse in pale cream and a navy skirt with a kickpleat, put on a pair of flats, and headed down- stairs long before the predetermined hour. Bored, she once again walked around the library, pausing before a bookcase filled with botanical tomes. She imagined Francis must have obtained his knowledge of mushrooms this way, scavenging for wisdom among moth-eaten papers. She brushed a hand along the silver frames of the pictures in the hallways, feeling the whorls and swirls beneath her fingertips. Eventually, Fran- cis came downstairs.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He wasn’t very talkative that morning, so she limited her- self to a couple of comments and fiddled with her cigarette, not quite willing to light it yet. She didn’t like smoking on an empty stomach.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He dropped her off by the church, which, she gathered, was where they’d dropped off Catalina each week when she went into town.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“T’ll pick you up at noon,” he said. “Will that be sufficient time?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Yes, thank you,” she told him. He nodded at her and drove away.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She made her way to the healer’s house. The woman who had been doing the washing the other day wasn’t out; her clothesline lay empty. The town remained quiet, still half asleep. Marta Duval, however, was awake, setting out tortillas</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_114" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 114 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164622_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">to dry in the sun next to her doorway, no doubt to be used for preparing chilaquiles.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Good morning,” Noemi said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Hello,” the old woman replied with a smile. “You’ve come back at exactly the right time.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You have the remedy?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I have it. Come inside.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi followed her into the kitchen and sat at the table. The parrot was not there that day. It was only the two of them. The woman wiped her hands against her apron and opened a drawer, then placed a small bottle in front of Noemi.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“One tablespoon before bedtime should be enough for her. I made it stronger this time, but there’s no harm in two table- spoons, either.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi held the bottle up, staring at its content. “And it’ll help her sleep?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Help, yes. It won't solve all her problems.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Because the house is cursed.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“The family, the house.” Marta Duval shrugged. “Makes no difference, does it? Cursed is cursed.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi set the bottle down and ran a nail across the side of it. “Do you know why Ruth Doyle killed her family? Did you ever hear any rumors about that?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You hear all kinds of things. Yes, I heard. Do you have any more cigarettes?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Tl run out of them if I don’t ration them.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I bet you were going to buy more.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I don’t think you can buy these here,” Noemi said. “Your saint has expensive tastes. Where’s the parrot, by the way?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi took out her pack of Gauloises and handed one to Marta, who placed it next to the statuette of the saint. “Still in his cage under his blanket. I’ll tell you about Benito. Do you want coffee? It’s no good telling tales without a drink.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Sure,” Noemi said. She still wasn’t hungry, but she sup- posed coffee might restore her appetite. It was funny. Her brother said she always breakfasted as if food was going to go out of fashion, and yet for the past two days she’d hardly touched a morsel in the mornings. Not that she’d had much in the evenings, either. She felt slightly ill. Or rather, it was the preface of an illness, like when she could predict she was about to get a cold. She hoped that was not the case.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Marta Duval set a kettle to boil and ruammaged among her drawers until she produced a small tin can. When the water boiled, she poured it into two pewter mugs, added the proper amount of coffee, and placed both cups on the table. Marta’s</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_115" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 115 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164626_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">house smelled strongly of rosemary, and the scent mixed with the scent of the coffee.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I take mine black, but do you want sugar in yours?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“T’m fine,” Noemi said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The woman sat down and settled her hands around her mug.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Do you want the short version or the long one? Because the long means going back quite a bit. If you want to know about Benito, then you need to know about Aurelio. That is, if you want to tell the tale properly.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Well, lam running out of cigarettes but not out of time.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The woman smiled and sipped her coffee. Noemi did the same.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“When the mine reopened, it was big news. Mr. Doyle had his workers from England, but those weren’t enough to run a mine. They could oversee the work and others could work in the house he was building, but you can’t open a mine and build a house like High Place with sixty Englishmen.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Who used to run the mine before him?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Spaniards. But that had been ages ago. People were happy when the mine reopened. It meant work for the locals, and folks came from other parts of Hidalgo for the chance at a job too. You know how it is. Where there’s a mine, there’s money, the town grows. But right away folks complained. The work was tough, but Mr. Doyle was tougher.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“He treated the workers poorly?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Like animals, they said. He was better with the ones build- ing the house. At least they were not in a hole beneath the earth. The Mexican mining crews, he had no mercy on those. Him, Mr. Doyle, and his brother, both of them bellowing at the workers.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Francis had pointed Leland, Howard’s brother, out in the photos, but she could not recall what he looked like, and any- way, all the people in the family seemed to have that similar physiognomy, which she was dubbing in her head “the Doyle look.” Like the Habsburg jaw of Charles II, only not quite as concerning. Now that had been a case of severe mandibular prognathism.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“He wanted the house built quickly and he wanted a great garden, in the English style, with rose beds. He even brought boxes filled with earth from Europe to make sure the flowers would take. So there they were, working on the house and trying to mine the silver, when there came a sickness. It hit the workers at the house first and then the miners, but soon enough they were all heaving and feverish. Doyle had a doc-</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_116" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 116 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164631_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">tor, who he’d brought along, just like his soil, but his precious doctor didn’t help much. They died. Lots of the miners. Some of the people working at the house, and even Howard Doyle’s wife, but mostly a great deal of miners dropped dead.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“That’s when they built the English cemetery,” Noemi said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Yes. That’s right.” Marta nodded. “Well, the sickness passed. New folks were hired. People from Hidalgo, yes, but having heard there was an Englishman with a mine here, there also came more Englishmen who were working around other mines, or were simply trying to make their fortune, lured by silver and a good profit. They say Zacatecas is for sil- ver? Well, Hidalgo does well enough too.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“They came and again there were full crews and by now the house was finished, which meant it had a large staff for a proper large house. Things went along well enough—Doyle was still hard, but he paid on time and the miners also got their little quota of silver, which is the way it’s always been done around here—miners expected the partido. But it was around the time Mr. Doyle married again that things started to turn sour.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She recalled the wedding portrait of Doyle’s second wife: 1895. Alice, who looked like Agnes. Alice, the little sister. Now that she considered it, it was odd that Agnes had been im- mortalized with a stone statue, while Alice received no such treatment. Yet Howard Doyle had said he hardly knew her. It was his second wife who had lived with him for many years, who birthed him children. Did Howard Doyle like her even less than his first wife? Or was the statue insignificant, a memorial created on a whim? She tried to remember if there had been a plaque near the statue discussing Agnes. She didn’t think so, but there could be. She hadn't looked closely.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“There was another wave of sickness. Lord, it hit them worse than before. They were dropping like flies. Fevers and chills and quickly to their deathbeds they went.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Is that when they buried them in mass graves?” Noemi asked, remembering what Dr. Camarillo had told her.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The old woman frowned. “Mass graves? No. The locals, their families took them to the cemetery in town. But there were many people without kin working the mines. When someone didn’t have family in town, they buried them in the English cemetery. The Mexicans didn’t get a headstone, though, not even a cross, which I guess is why people talked about mass graves. A hole in the ground with no wreath nor proper service might as well be a mass grave.”</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_117" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 117 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164635_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Now that was a depressing thought. All those nameless workers, buried in haste, and no one to ever know where and how their lives had ended. Noemi set her pewter cup down and scratched her wrist.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Anyway, that was not the only problem at the mine. Doyle had decided to end the custom of letting the workers have a bit of silver along with their wages. There was a man and his name was Aurelio. Aurelio was one of the miners who didn’t like the change none, but unlike others who would grumble to themselves, Aurelio grumbled to the others.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“What did he say?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Told them what was obvious. That the camp where they worked was shit. That the doctor the Englishman brought with him had never cured anyone and they needed a good doc- tor. That they were leaving behind widows and orphans and hardly any money for them, and on top of that Doyle wanted to fatten up his pockets more so he’d taken away their partido and was hoarding all the silver. Then he asked the miners to go on strike.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Did they?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Yes, they did. Of course, Doyle thought he could bully them back to work real easy. Doyle’s brother and Doyle’s trusted men, they went over to the mining camp with rifles and threats, but Aurelio and the others fought back. They threw stones at them. Doyle’s brother got away by the skin of his teeth. Soon after that, Aurelio was found dead. They said it was a natural death, but no one really believed that. The strike leader dies one morning? It didn’t sound right.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“There was an epidemic, though,” Noemi pointed out.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Sure. But people who saw the body said his face looked awful. You’ve heard about people dying of fright? Well, they said he died of fright. That his eyes were bulging and his mouth was open and he looked like a man who's seen the devil. It scared everyone good, and it also ended the strike.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Francis had mentioned strikes and the closure of the mine, but Noemi had not thought to ask him more about them. Per- haps she should remedy that, but for now she focused her at- tention on Marta.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You said that Aurelio was connected to Benito, though. Who was that?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Patience, girl, youll make me lose my train of thought. At my age, it’s no easy thing to try and remember what was when and how it happened.” Marta took several long sips of her coffee before speaking. “Where was I? Oh, yes. The mine went on. Doyle had remarried and eventually his new wife</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_118" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 118 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164640_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">gave birth toa girl, Miss Ruth, and many years later a baby boy. Doyle’s brother, Mr. Leland, he also had children. A boy anda girl. The boy was engaged to Miss Ruth.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Kissing cousins, again,” Noemi said, disturbed by this no- tion. The Habsburg jaw was a more apt comparison than she’d thought, and things had not ended well for the Habsburgs.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Not much kissing, I think. That was the problem. That is where Benito comes in. He was a nephew of Aurelio and went to work in the house. This was years after the strike, so I sup- pose it’s not like Doyle cared that he was related to Aurelio. Or a dead miner didn’t matter to him none, or else he didn’t know. In any case, he worked in the house, tending to the plants. By that time instead of a garden the Doyles had settled on a greenhouse.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Benito had a lot in common with his dead uncle. He was smart, he was funny, and he didn’t know how to keep out of trouble. His uncle had organized a strike, and he did an even more horrifying thing: he fell in love with Miss Ruth and she fell in love with him.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I can’t imagine her father was pleased,” Noemi said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He’d probably given his daughter the eugenics talk. Super- ior and inferior specimens. She pictured him by the fireplace in his room admonishing the girl, and she with her eyes fixed on the floor. Poor Benito had not stood a chance. It was funny, though, that if Doyle was truly that interested in eugenics he’d insist on all these marriages to close relations. Maybe he was imitating Darwin, who'd also married within his family.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“They say when he found out he almost killed her,” Marta muttered.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Now she pictured Howard Doyle wrapping his fingers around the girl’s slim neck. Strong fingers, digging deep, press- ing hard, and the girl incapable of even uttering a protest because she couldn’t breathe. Papa, don’t. It was such a vivid image that Noemi had to close her eyes for a moment, gripping the table with one hand.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Are you all right?” Marta asked.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Yes,” Noemi said, opening her eyes and nodding at the woman. “I’m fine. A little tired.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She raised the cup of coffee to her lips and drank. The warm liquid was pleasant in its bitterness. Noemi set the cup down. “Please, go on,” she said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“There’s not much more to say. Ruth was punished, Benito vanished.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“He was killed?”</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_119" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 119 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164645_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The old woman leaned forward, her cloudy eyes fixed on Noemi. “Even worse: disappeared, from one day to the next. Folks said he’d run off because he was afraid of what Doyle would do to him, but others said Doyle had done the disap- pearing.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Ruth was supposed to get married that summer to Michael, that cousin of hers, and Benito’s disappearance didn’t change that one bit. Nothing would have changed that. It was the middle of the Revolution, and the upheaval meant the mine was operating with a small crew, but it was still operat- ing. Someone had to keep the machinery going, pumping the water out, or it would flood. It rains so much here.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“And up at the house, someone had to keep changing the linens and dusting the furniture, so in many ways I guess things hadn’t changed over a war, so why would they change over a missing man? Howard Doyle ordered trinkets for the wedding, acting as though nothing was amiss. As though Be- nito’s disappearance didn’t matter. Well, it must have mat- tered to Ruth.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“None can be sure what happened, but they said she put a sleeping draught in their food. I don’t know where she got it from. She was clever, she knew many things about plants and medicine, so it could be she mixed the draught herself. Or per- haps her lover had procured it for her. Maybe in the beginning she had thought to put them to sleep and run away, but after- ward she changed her mind. Once Benito disappeared. She shot her father while he slept, because of what he’d done to her lover.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“But not just her father,” Noemi said. “She shot her mother and the others. If she was avenging her dead lover, wouldn’t she have only shot her father? What did the others have to do with that?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Maybe she thought they were also guilty. Maybe she’d gone mad. We can’t know. They’re cursed, I tell you, and that house is haunted. You're very silly or very brave living in a haunted house.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">I’m not sorry, that’s what the Ruth in her dream had said. Had Ruth been remorseless as she wandered through the house and delivered a bullet to her kinfolk? Just because Noemi had dreamed it, it didn’t mean it had happened that way. After all, in her nightmare the house had been distorted and mutated in impossible ways.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi frowned, looking at her cup of coffee. She’d taken few sips. Her stomach was definitely not cooperating that morning.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_120" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 120 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164649_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Trouble is there’s not much you can do about ghosts nor hauntings. You might burn a candle at night for them and maybe they’d like that. You know about the mal de aire? Your mama ever tell you about that in the city?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I’ve heard one thing or another,” she said. “It’s supposed to make you sick.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“There’re heavy places. Places where the air itself is heavy because an evil weighs it down. Sometimes it’s a death, could be it’s something else, but the bad air, it’ll get into your body and it’ll nestle there and weigh you down. That’s what’s wrong with the Doyles of High Place,” the woman said, concluding her tale.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Like feeding an animal madder plants: it dyes the bones red, it stains everything inside crimson, she thought.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Marta Duval rose and began opening kitchen drawers. She grabbed a beaded bracelet and brought it back to the table, handing it to Noemi. It had tiny blue and white glass beads, and a larger blue bead with a black center.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“It’s against the evil eye.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Yes, I know,” Noemi said, because she had seen such trin- kets before.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You wear it, yes? It might help you, can’t hurt. I’ll be sure to ask my saints to watch over you too.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi opened her purse and placed the bottle inside. Then, because she didn’t want to hurt the old woman’s feelings, she tied the bracelet around her wrist as she’d suggested. “Thank you.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Walking back toward the town center, Noemi considered all the things she now knew about the Doyles and how none of it would assist Catalina. Ultimately even a haunting, if you accepted it as real and not the result of a feverish imagin- ation, didn’t mean anything. The fear of the previous night had cooled away, and now all there was left was the taste of dissatisfaction.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi pulled her cardigan’s sleeve up, scratching her wrist again. It itched something awful. She realized there was a thin, raw, red band of skin around her wrist. As though she’d burned herself. She frowned.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Dr. Camarillo’s clinic was nearby, so she decided to stop by and hope he didn’t have a patient. She was in luck. The doc- tor was eating a torta in the reception area. He didn’t have his white coat on; instead he wore a simple, single-breasted tweed jacket. When she stopped in front of him, Julio Cama- rillo quickly set the torta on a table next to him and wiped his mouth and hands on his handkerchief.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_121" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 121 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164654_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Out for a walk?” he asked.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Of a sort,” she said. “Am I interrupting your breakfast?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“It’s not much of an interruption, seeing as it’s not very tasty. I made it myself and did a bad job. How’s your cousin? Are they finding a specialist for her?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“T’m afraid her husband doesn’t think she needs any other doctor. Arthur Cummins is enough for them.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Do you think it might help if I talked to him?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She shook her head. “It might make it worse, to be honest.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“That’s a pity. And how are you?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“T’m not sure. I have this rash,” Noemi said, holding up her wrist for him to see.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Dr. Camarillo inspected her wrist carefully. “Odd,” he said. “Tt almost looks like you came in contact with mala mujer, but that doesn’t grow here. It’s a sure recipe for dermatitis if you touch the leaves. Do you have allergies?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“No. My mother says it’s almost indecent how healthy I am. She told me when she was a young girl everyone thought it was very fashionable to suffer a bout of appendicitis and girls went on a tapeworm diet.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“She must have been joking about the tapeworm,” Dr. Ca- marillo said. “That’s a made-up story.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“It always did sound quite horrifying. Then I’m allergic to something? A plant or shrub?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“It could be a number of things. We'll wash the hand and put on a soothing ointment. Come in,” he said, directing her into his office.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She washed her hands in the little sink in the corner, and Julio applied a zinc paste, bandaged her wrist, and told her she should not scratch the affected area because it would make it worse. He advised her to change the bandage the next day and apply more zinc paste.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“It'll take a few days for the inflammation to go away,” he said, walking her back toward the entrance, “but you should be fine after a week. Come see me if it doesn’t improve.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Thanks,” she said and placed the tiny jar of zinc paste he’d gifted her inside her purse. “I have another question. Do you know what could cause a person to begin sleepwalking again?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Again?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I sleepwalked when I was very young, but I haven’t done it in ages. But I sleepwalked last night.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Yes, it’s more common for children to sleepwalk. Have you been taking any new medication?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“No. I told you. I’m scandalously healthy.”</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_122" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 122 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164659_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Could be anxiety,” the doctor said, and then he smiled a little.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I had the oddest dream when I was sleepwalking,” she said. “It didn’t feel like when I was a kid.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">It had also been an extremely morbid dream and then, afterward, the chat with Virgil had not helped soothe her. Noemi frowned.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I see I’ve failed to be helpful once again.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Don’t say that,” she replied quickly.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Tell you what, if it happens again you come and see me. And you watch that wrist.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Sure.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi stopped at one of the tiny little stores set around the town square. She bought herself a pack of cigarettes. There were no Loteria cards to be had, but she did find a pack of cheap naipes. Cups, clubs, coins, and swords, to lighten the day. Someone had told her it was possible to read the cards, to tell fortunes, but what Noemi liked to do was play for money with her friends.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The store owner counted her change slowly. He was very old, and his glasses had a crack running down the middle. At the store’s entrance sat a yellow dog drinking from a dirty bowl. Noemi scratched its ears on the way out.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The post office was also in the town square, and she sent a short letter to her father informing him of the current situ- ation at High Place: she’d obtained a second opinion from a doctor who said Catalina needed psychiatric care. She did not write that Virgil was extremely reluctant to let anyone see Catalina, because she did not want to worry her father. She also did not mention anything about her nightmares, nor the sleepwalking episode. Those, along with the rash blooming on her wrist, were unpleasant markers of her journey, but they were superfluous details.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Once these tasks were done, she stood in the middle of the town square glancing at the few businesses there. There was no ice cream shop, no souvenir store selling knickknacks, no bandstand for musicians to play their tunes. A couple of store- fronts were boarded up, with For Sale painted on the outside. The church was still impressive, but the rest was really quite sad. A withered world. Had it looked this way in Ruth’s day? Had she even been allowed to visit the town? Or was she kept locked inside High Place?</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi headed back to the exact spot where Francis had dropped her off. He arrived a couple of minutes later, while she sat on a wrought-iron bench and was about to light a cigarette.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_123" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 123 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164704_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You're quick to fetch me,” she said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“My mother doesn’t believe in tardiness,” he said as he stood in front of her and took off the felt hat with the navy band he’d put on that morning.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Did you tell her where we went?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I didn’t go back to the house. If I had, my mother or Virgil might have started asking why I'd left you alone.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Were you driving around?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“A bit. I parked under a tree over there and took a nap too. Did anything happen to you?” he asked, pointing at her ban- daged wrist.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“A rash,” Noemi said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She extended her hand so that he might help her up, andhe did. Without her monumental high heels, Noemi’s head barely reached his shoulder. When such a height difference pre- sented itself, Noemi might stand on her tiptoes. Her cousins teased her about it, calling her “the ballerina.” Not Catalina, because she was too sweet to tease anyone, but cousin Mari- lulu did it all the time. Now, reflexively, she did that, and that little meaningless motion must have startled him, because he let go with the hand that had been holding his hat, and a gust of wind blew it away.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Oh, no,” Noemi said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">They chased after the hat, running for a good two blocks before she managed to get hold of it. In her tight skirt and stockings this was no small feat. The yellow dog she’d seen at the store, amused by the spectacle, barked at Noemi and cir- cled her. She pressed the hat against her chest.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Well, I suppose now I’ve done my daily calisthenics,” she said, chuckling.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Francis seemed amused too and watched her with an un- usual levity. There was a sad and resigned quality to him that struck her as odd for someone his age, but the midday sun had washed his melancholy away and gave color to his cheeks. Virgil was good looking, Francis was not. He had an almost nonexistent upper lip, eyebrows that arched a little too much, heavy-lidded eyes. She liked him nevertheless.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He was odd and it was endearing.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She offered him the hat, and Francis turned it in his hands carefully. “What?” he asked, sounding bashful, because she was looking at him.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Won't you thank me for rescuing your hat, dear sir?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Thank you.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Silly boy,” she said, planting a kiss on his cheek.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_124" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 124 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164715_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She was afraid he’d drop the hat and they’d have to chase it again, but he managed to hold on to it and smiled as they walked back to the car.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You finished the errands you needed to run in town?” he asked.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Yes. Post office, doctor. I was also talking to someone about High Place, about what happened there. You know, with Ruth,” she told him. Her mind kept going back to Ruth. It really should be no concern of hers, this decades-old murder, but there it was, the nagging thought, and she wanted to talk about it. Who better than him?</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Francis tapped the hat twice against his leg as they walked. “What about her?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“She wanted to run away with her lover. Instead, she ended up shooting her whole family. I don’t understand why she’d do what she did. Why didn’t she run away from High Place? Surely she could have simply left.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You can’t leave High Place.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“But you can. She was an adult woman.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You're a woman. Can you do anything you want? Even if it upsets your family?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Technically I can, even if I wouldn't every single time,” Noemi said, though she immediately remembered her father’s issues with scandals and the fear of the society pages. Would she ever risk an outright rebellion against her family?</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“My mother left High Place, she married. But she came back. There’s no escaping it. Ruth knew as much. That’s why she did what she did.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You sound almost proud,” she exclaimed.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Francis placed the hat on his head and looked at her gravely. “No. But truth be told Ruth ought to have burnt High Place to the ground.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">It was such a shocking pronouncement that she thought she must have heard him wrong, and she would have been able to convince herself this was the case if they had not driven back to the house in a bubble of silence. That piercing silence more than anything affirmed his words. It underlined them and made her turn her face toward the window. In her hand she held her unlit cigarette, watching the trees, light streaming through the branches.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_125" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 125 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164720_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">oemi decided they’d have themselves a mini ca-</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">sino night. She’d always loved casino night.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">They’d sit in the dining room, and everyone</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">dressed the part, using old clothes picked from their grandparents’ trunks, pretending they were high rollers in Monte Carlo or Havana. All the children played, and even when they were way too old for games of pretend, the Taboada cousins would gather around the table and put the record player to good use, tapping their feet to a snappy tune and carefully laying down their cards. It couldn’t be quite like that at High Place since they had no records to play, but Noemi de- cided the spirit of their casino nights could be captured if they tried.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She slipped the deck into one large sweater pocket and the little bottle into the other, and then she peeked her head in- side Catalina’s room. Her cousin was alone and she was awake. Perfect.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I have a treat for you,” Noemi said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Catalina was sitting by the window. She turned and looked at Noemi. “Do you, now?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You must choose, the left or right pocket, and then you'll have a reward,” Noemi said, approaching her.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“What if I choose the wrong one?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Catalina’s hair fell loose past her shoulders. She had never taken to short hairstyles. Noemi was glad. Catalina’s hair was sleek and lovely, and she had fond memories of brushing it and braiding it when she was a little girl. Catalina had been so patient with her, allowing Noemi to treat her like a living doll.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Then you'll never know what was in the other pocket.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You silly girl,” Catalina replied, smiling. “I’ll play your game. Right.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Ta-da.”</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_126" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 126 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164725_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi placed the pack of cards on Catalina’s lap. Her cousin opened the pack and smiled, taking out a card and holding it up.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“We can play a few hands,” Noemi said. “I’ll even let you win the first one.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“As if! I never met a more competitive child. And it’s not like Florence would let us play late into the night.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“We might still play at least a little.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I have no money to bet, and you don’t play if there’s no money on the table.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You're looking for excuses. Are you afraid of that dreadful, nagging Florence?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Catalina stood up quickly and went to stand by her vanity, tilting its mirror and setting the pack of cards next to a hair- brush while she looked at her reflection. “No. Not at all,” she said, grabbing the brush and running it through her hair a couple of times.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Good. Because I have a second present for you, and I wouldn’t give it to a scaredy-cat.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi held the green bottle up. Catalina turned around, wonder in her eyes, and carefully grabbed the bottle. “You did it.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I told you I would.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Dearest, thank you, thank you,” Catalina said, pulling her into an embrace. “I should know you would never abandon me. We thought monsters and ghosts were found in books, but they’re real, you know?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Her cousin released Noemi and opened a drawer. She took out a couple of handkerchiefs, a pair of white gloves, before finding her prize: a small silver spoon. Then she proceeded to pour herself a teaspoon, her fingers trembling a little, then an- other and a third. Noemi stopped her at the fourth, taking the bottle from her hands and setting it on the dresser, along with the spoon.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Jeez, don’t have so much. Marta said you might have one tablespoon and that would be enough,” Noemi chided her. “I don’t want you snoring for ten hours straight before we even get a chance to play a single hand.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Yes. Yes, of course,” Catalina said, smiling weakly.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Now, shall I shuffle or will you do the honors?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Let me see.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Catalina slid a hand across the deck. Then she stopped; she lifted her hand and her fingers remained hovering above the pack of cards, as if she’d been frozen in place. Her hazel eyes were open wide and her mouth was closed tight. She looked</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_127" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 127 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164729_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">so strange. Like a woman who has gone into a trance. Noemi frowned.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Catalina? Are you unwell? Do you want to sit down?” she asked.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Catalina did not reply. Noemi gently grabbed her by the arm and attempted to maneuver her toward the bed. Catalina wouldn’t move. Her fingers curled into a fist, and she con- tinued to stare forward, those large eyes of hers looking wild. Noemi might as well have attempted to shove an elephant. It was impossible to get her to budge a single inch.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Catalina,” Noemi said. “Why don’t—”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">There was a loud crack—dear God, Noemi thought it might be a joint cracking—and Catalina began to shiver. She shivered from the top of her head to the soles of her feet, one sweeping, rippling motion. Then the shiver became more frenzied, and she was convulsing, she was pressing her hands against her stomach and shaking her head, and the most vicious scream escaped her lungs.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi attempted to hold her, to drag her toward the bed, but Catalina was strong. It was amazing how strong she was considering how frail she looked, yet she managed to resist Noemi, and they both ended up on the floor, Catalina’s mouth opening and closing spasmodically, her arms rising and fall- ing, the legs shaking wildly. A trail of saliva slid down the cor- ner of her mouth.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Help!” Noemi yelled. “Help!”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi had gone to school with a girl who had epilepsy and although the girl never had a fit on school grounds, she remembered how she once told her she carried a little stick in her purse so that she might place it in her mouth if she had a seizure.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">With Catalina’s attack growing in intensity—which seemed impossible yet was undeniably happening—she snatched the silver spoon from the dresser and placed it in Catalina’s mouth to keep her from biting her own tongue. She knocked down the card deck, which had also been resting on the dresser. The cards spilled and fanned out on the floor. The knave of coins stared at Noemi accusingly.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi ran to the hallway and began yelling, “Help me!”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Had no one heard the commotion? She rushed forward, banging on doors, and yelling as loud as she could. Suddenly Francis appeared and behind him came Florence.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Catalina is having a seizure,” she told them.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">They all ran back to the room. Catalina was still on the floor; the tremors had not stopped. Francis sprang forward</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_128" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 128 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164734_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">and sat her up, placing his arms around her, attempting to subdue her. Noemi was going to help him, but Florence stood in her way.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Get out,” she ordered.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I can help.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Out, out now,” she ordered, shoving Noemi back and slam- ming the door in her face.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi knocked furiously but no one opened. She could hear murmurs and once in a while a loud word or two. She began pacing the hallway.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">When Francis came out he quickly closed the door behind him. Noemi hurried to his side.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“What’s happening? How is she?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“She’s in bed. I’m going to fetch Dr. Cummins,” Francis said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">They walked briskly toward the stairs, his long stride meaning she had to take two to keep up with him.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“T’ll go with you.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“No,” Francis said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I want to do something.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He stopped and shook his head before clutching her hands together. He spoke softly. “You come with me, it’ll be worse. Go to the sitting room, and when I return, I’ll fetch you. I won’t be long.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Promise?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Yes.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He dashed down the stairs. She rushed down the staircase too and pressed her hands against her face when she reached the bottom, tears prickling her eyes. By the time she walked into the sitting room, the tears were falling hard, and she sat on the carpet, clutching her hands together. The minutes ticked by. She wiped her nose with her sweater’s sleeve, wiped her tears with the palms of her hand. She stood up and waited.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He lied. It was a long time. What was worse, when Francis returned it was in the company of Dr. Cummins and Florence. At least there had been enough time for Noemi to compose herself.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“How is she?” Noemi asked, swiftly walking up to the doc- tor.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“She’s asleep now. The crisis has passed.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Thank God,” Noemi said, and she sank onto one of the settees. “I don’t understand what happened.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“What happened was this,” Florence said sharply, holding up the bottle Noemi had fetched from Marta Duval. “Where did you get it?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“It’s a sleeping tonic,” Noemi said.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_129" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 129 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164738_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Your sleeping tonic made her sick.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“No.” Noemi shook her head. “No, she said she needed it.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Are you a medical professional?” Dr. Cummins asked her. He was distinctly displeased. Noemi felt her mouth go dry.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“No, but—”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“So you have no idea what was inside this bottle?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I told you, Catalina said she needed medicine to help her sleep. She asked me for it. She’s taken it before, it couldn’t have made her ill.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Tt did,” the doctor told her.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“An opium tincture. That’s what you shoved down your cousin’s throat,” Florence added, pointing an accusing finger at Noemi.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I did no such thing!”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“It was very ill-advised, very ill-advised, indeed,” Dr. Cum- mins muttered. “Why I couldn’t begin to understand what you were thinking, procuring a filthy potion like that. And then, attempting to put a spoon in your cousin’s mouth. I suppose you heard that silly tale of people swallowing their tongues? Nonsense. All nonsense.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">ee</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Where did you get the tincture?” Florence asked.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Tell no one, Catalina had said, and so Noemi did not reply even if the mention of Marta Duval might have shifted the burden of her guilt. She gripped the back of the settee with one hand, digging her nails into the fabric.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You could have killed her,” the woman said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“T wouldn't!”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi felt like crying again but could not allow herself this release, not in their presence. Francis had moved to stand be- hind the settee, and she felt his fingers upon her hand, almost ghostly. It was a comforting gesture, and it gave her the cour- age to clamp her mouth shut.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Who did you procure the potion from?” the doctor asked.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi stared at them and kept on gripping the settee.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I should slap you,” Florence said. “I should slap that disres- pectful look off your face.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Florence stepped forward. Noemi felt that perhaps she really did mean to slap her. She pushed Francis’s hand aside, ready to stand up.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“If you could please go check on my father I would be very grateful, Dr. Cummins. All the noise tonight has him a little anxious,” Virgil said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He had strolled rather casually into the room, and his voice was cool as he ventured toward the sideboard and inspected a</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_130" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 130 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164743_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">decanter, as if he were alone and pouring himself a drink like any other evening.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Yes. Why yes, of course,” the doctor said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“It’s best if you two go with him. I wish to speak to Noemi alone.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I am not—’” Florence began.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I wish to be alone with her,” Virgil replied acridly. The smooth silk of his voice was now sandpaper.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">They left, the doctor mumbling a “yes, at once,” Florence walking in a somber silence. Francis was the last to step out, slowly closing the doors of the sitting room before throwing her one nervous glance.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Virgil filled his glass, swirling the liquid in it, staring at its contents, before approaching her and taking a place on the same settee she occupied. His leg brushed against hers when he sat down.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Catalina once told me you were a very strong-willed crea- ture, but I didn’t quite understand how strong-willed until now,” he said, setting the drink down on an oblong side table. “Your cousin is a bit of a weakling, isn’t she? But you have a certain mettle in your bones.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He spoke so blithely it made her gasp. He talked as if this were a game. As if she were not sick with worry. “Have a little respect,” she said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I think it’s another person who should be showing respect. This is my home.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Tm sorry.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You're not sorry at all.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She could not read the expression in his eyes. Perhaps it was contempt. “I am sorry! But I was trying to help Catalina.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You have a funny way of showing it. How dare you con- stantly upset my wife?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“What do you mean I constantly upset her? She is glad to have me around, she told me so.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You bring strangers to look at her and then you bring her poison.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“For God’s sake,” she said and stood up.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He immediately grabbed her by the wrist and yanked her down. It was her bandaged wrist, and it hurt when he touched her; the skin burned for a second and she winced. He tugged at her sleeve, revealing the bandage, and smirked.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Let go.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Dr. Camarillo’s work, perhaps? Just like the tincture? Was it him?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Don’t touch me,” she ordered.</p>
    </div>
