    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_131" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 131 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164748_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">But he did not release her; instead he leaned forward. He clasped her arm tight. She thought Howard looked like an in- sect and Florence was an insectivorous plant. But Virgil Doyle, he was a carnivore, high up the food chain.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Florence is right. You deserve to be slapped and taught a few lessons,” he muttered.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“If anyone is slapped in this room, I assure you, it won't be me.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He threw his head back and laughed a loud, savage laugh, and he reached blindly for his drink. A few dark drops of liquor spilled on the side table as he lifted the glass. The sound of his voice practically made her jump. At least he had released her.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You're mad,” she said, rubbing her wrist.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Mad with worry, yes,” he replied, downing the wine. Ra- ther than placing the glass on the table again, he carelessly tossed it on the floor. It did not shatter, but rolled across the carpet. But what if it had shattered? It was his glass. His to break if he wanted. Like everything else in this house.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Do you think you are the only person who cares what happens to Catalina?” he asked, his eyes fixed on the glass. “I imagine you do. When Catalina wrote to your family, did you think, ‘ah, at last we can pry her away from that troublesome man’? And right now you must think ‘I knew he was bad.’ Your father certainly didn’t like me for a groom.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“When the mine was open, he would have been glad to see Catalina married to me. Back then I would have been worthy. He wouldn't have thought me inconsequential. It must still irk him, and you, to know Catalina picked me. Well, I’m no two- bit fortune hunter, I’m a Doyle. It would be good of you to re- member that.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I don’t know why you bring all this up.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Because you believe I am so inadequate that you had to go and medicate Catalina. You thought the care I give her is so atrocious that you must sneak behind my back and pour gar- bage into her mouth. Did you think we wouldn’t notice? We know everything that goes on in this house.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“She asked for this medicine. I told your aunt and the doc- tor already, I didn’t realize this would happen.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“No, you don’t know very much, and yet you act as if you know everything, don’t you? You’re a spoiled brat and you’ve hurt my wife,” he said with a brutal finality.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He stood up and picked up the glass, setting it upon the fireplace mantel. She felt twin flames inside her heart, anger and shame. She hated the way he was talking to her, hated this entire conversation. And yet had she not done a foolish</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_132" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 132 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_164753_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">thing? Hadn’t she earned a reprimand? She did not know how to reply and felt tears pooling in her eyes again as she recalled poor Catalina’s face.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He must have noticed her turmoil or else he was simply done berating her, because his voice wavered a little. “You al- most made me a widower tonight, Noemi. You will forgive me if I don’t feel very gracious at this time. I should head to bed. It’s been a long day.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He did look tired, frankly exhausted. His blue eyes were very bright, with the brightness of a sudden fever. It made her feel even worse about the whole mess.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I must ask you to leave Catalina’s medical care to Dr. Cum- mins and never bring any other tonics or remedies into this house. Are you listening to me?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I am,” she replied.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Will you follow this simple directive?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She clenched her hands. “I will,” she said, and she felt very much like a child.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He took a step closer to her, carefully looking at her, as though trying to discern a lie, but there was none. She spoke in earnest, and yet he brushed close to her, like a scientist who must analyze and jot down every detail of an organism, taking in her face, her pursed lips.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Thank you. There are many things you cannot under- stand, Noemi. But let me make it clear that Catalina’s well- being is of the utmost importance to us. You’ve harmed her and in harming her you’ve harmed me.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi turned her head away. She thought he’d leave. In- stead, he lingered at her side. Then, a small eternity later, he stepped away from her and out of the room.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_133" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 133 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_182435_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">nasense all dreams foretell events, but some more clearly</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">than others.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi circled the word dreams with her pencil. She en-</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">joyed writing in the margins of her books; she loved reading these anthropological texts, sinking into the lush paragraphs and the forest of footnotes. But not now. Now she couldn’t concentrate. She rested her chin on the back of her hand and placed the pencil in her mouth.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She had spent hours in waiting, trying to find things to do, books to read, perfecting tricks to distract herself. She checked her watch and sighed. It was close to five o’clock.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She had attempted to speak to Catalina early in the morn- ing, but Florence had told Noemi that her cousin was resting. Around noon Noemi had tried again. She had been dismissed a second time, and Florence had made it clear there would be no visits with the patient until nighttime.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi must not push and pry and attempt to wedge her way into that room, as much as she wanted to. She simply couldn’t. They’d toss her out if she did, and besides, Virgil was correct. She’d done wrong and felt ashamed.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">How she wished there were a radio around the house. She needed music, conversation. She thought of the parties she at- tended with her friends, leaning against a piano with a cock- tail in her hand. Also her classes at the university and the lively discussions at the cafés downtown. What she now had was a silent house and a heart riddled with anxiety.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">...and dreams about ghosts, not recorded in this book, inform people about happenings among the dead.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She took the pencil out of her mouth and put the book aside. Reading about the Azande did her no good. It offered no distraction. She kept remembering her cousin’s face, her con- torted limbs, the hideous episode of the previous day.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi grabbed a sweater—the one Francis had given her —and stepped outside. She thought to smoke a cigarette, but</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_134" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 134 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_182723_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">once she stood in the shadow of the house she decided she needed a bit more distance from it. It lingered too close; it was hostile and cold and she did not wish to parade before its windows, which felt, to her, like lidless, eager eyes. She fol- lowed the path that snaked behind the house and led up to the cemetery.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Two, three, four steps, it seemed it didn’t take her long until she stood before the iron gates and walked inside. She’d been utterly lost in the mist before, but she did not bother to consider what she’d do if she should lose her way again.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">In fact, a part of her very dearly wished to be lost.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Catalina. She’d hurt Catalina, and even now she did not know how her cousin was faring. Florence was tight-lipped; she’d seen nothing of Virgil. Not that she wished to see him very much.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He’d been beastly.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">You almost made me a widower tonight, Noemi.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She hadn’t meant it. But, intent or not, what did it matter? What mattered were the facts. That’s what her father would tell her, and now Noemi felt doubly ashamed. She’d been sent to fix a problem, not to make an even bigger mess. Was Cata- lina angry with her? What might Noemi say when they finally saw each other? Sorry, dear cousin, I almost poisoned you, but you're looking better.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi walked among tombstones and moss and wild- flowers, her chin lowered, tucked close against the folds of the sweater. She saw the mausoleum and in front of it the stone statue of Agnes. Noemi peered up at the statue’s face and her hands, which were weathered with speckles of black fungus.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She had wondered if there was a plaque or marker with the name of the deceased on it, and she saw that there was. Noemi had overlooked it during her previous visit, although she could hardly be blamed for missing it. The plaque was hid- den by an overgrown clump of weeds. She plucked the weeds away and brushed the dirt off the bronze plaque.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Agnes Doyle. Mother. 1885. That was all Howard Doyle had chosen to leave behind to commemorate the passing of his first wife. He had said he had not known Agnes well, that she’d died within a year of their marriage, yet it seemed odd to have a statue carved of her and then not even compose a proper line or two about her passing.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">It was the nature of the one word etched beneath the woman’s name that bothered her too. Mother. But as far as Noemi knew, Howard Doyle’s children were born of his sec- ond marriage. Why choose “mother” as the epithet, then?</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_135" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 135 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_182727_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Perhaps she was making too much of this. Inside the mau- soleum, where the woman’s body rested, there might be a proper plaque and a proper message about the deceased. Yet it was unsettling in a way she could not define, like noticing a crooked seam or a tiny stain on a pristine tablecloth.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She sat there, at the statue’s feet, and tugged at a blade of grass wondering if someone ever bothered setting flowers in- side the mausoleum or at any of the graves. Could the families of everyone buried in the cemetery be gone from the area? But then, most of the English folk must have come alone, and therefore there had been no kin to bother with such things. There were also unmarked graves for the local workers and with no gravestone to their name, there could be no crowns of flowers for them.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">If Catalina dies, she thought, she’ll be buried here, and her tomb will be bare.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">What a horrid thought. But she was horrid, wasn’t she? Simply horrid. Noemi dropped the blade of grass and took a deep breath. The silence in the cemetery was absolute. No birds sang in the trees, no insects flapped their wings. Every- thing was muffled. It was like sitting at the bottom of a deep well, shielded by the earth and stone, from the world.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The merciless silence was broken by the sound of boots upon grass and the crunching of a twig. She turned her head and there was Francis, hands deep in his thick corduroy jacket’s pockets. He looked, as he often did, quite fragile, the faint sketch of a man. Only in a place such as this, in a ceme- tery with drooping willows and mist licking at the stones, could he acquire any substance. In the city, she thought, he’d be shattered by the banging of the Klaxon and the rumbling of motors. Fine china, tossed against a wall. But she liked the look of him in that old jacket, frail or not, his shoulders hunched a little.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I thought you'd be here.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Hunting for mushrooms again?” Noemi asked, setting her hands on her lap and steadying her voice so that it was not strained. She’d nearly wept in front of them last night. She did not wish to do it now.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I saw you leaving the house,” he confessed.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Did you need anything?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“My old sweater; you are wearing it,” he said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">It wasn’t quite the answer she was expecting. She frowned. “You want it back?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Not at all.”</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_136" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 136 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_182733_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She hiked up the too-long sleeves and shrugged. Any other day she would have taken this as a cue to engage in charming banter. She would have teased him and enjoyed watching him grow flustered. Now she picked at blades of grass.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He sat down next to her. “It really isn’t your fault.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You'd be the only person who thinks that. Your mother won't even tell me if Catalina is awake, and Virgil wants to throttle me. I wouldn’t be surprised if your uncle Howard wished to do the same.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Catalina woke up for a bit, but then went backto sleep. She had a little broth. She’ll be fine.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Yes, I’m sure,” Noemi muttered.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“When I say it’s not your fault I truly mean it,” he assured her, his hand falling upon her shoulder. “Please, look at me. It isn’t. It’s not the first time. This happened before.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“What do you mean?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">They stared at each other. It was his turn to pick a blade of grass and spin it between his fingers.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Well, come on. What do you mean?” she repeated, snatch- ing the piece of grass away.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“She'd taken that tincture...she’d had a reaction to it be- fore.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Are you telling me she’s made herself ill, the same way? Or else that she’s tried to kill herself? We are Catholic. It’s a sin. She wouldn’t, never ever.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I don’t think she wants to die. I mention it because you seem to think you did this to her, and you didn't. It’s not your presence that has made her ill; it’s not you at all. She’s miser- able here. You should take her away immediately.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Virgil wouldn’t let me do that before and he certainly won't let me do it now,” Noemi said. “She’s under lock and key, at any rate, isn’t she? As if I could even see her for a minute right now. Your mother is furious at me—”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Then you should leave,” he said brusquely.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I can’t leave!”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">First she considered the immense disappointment she’d cause her father. He’d sent her as an ambassador, to squelch scandal and provide answers, and she would return home empty-handed. Their deal would be void—no master’s degree for her, ever—and worse than that, she hated the taste of fail- ure.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Besides, she didn’t dare to go anywhere with Catalina in this state. What if she should need her? How could she hurt Catalina and then run off? How could she leave her all alone, racked in pain?</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_137" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 137 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_182737_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“She’s my family,” Noemi said. “You must stand by your family.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Even if you can’t possibly help her?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You don’t know that.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“This is no place for you,” he assured her.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Have they asked you to chase me away?” she asked, stand- ing up quickly, irritated by his sudden vehemence. “Are you trying to get rid of me? Do you dislike me so much?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I like you very much and you know it,” he said, his hands sliding into his jacket’s pockets again as he looked down.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Then you'll help me and take me into town now, won't you?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Why do you need to go into town?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I want to find out what was in the tincture Catalina drank.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“It won't do you any good.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Even if it doesn’t, I want to go. Will you take me?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Not today.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Tomorrow, then.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“The day after tomorrow, perhaps. Perhaps not.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Why not make it in a month,” she replied angrily. “I can walk into town without you if you have no desire to help me.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She meant to stomp away and succeeded only in stum- bling. Francis offered her his arm to steady herself, and he let out a sigh when her fingers caught his sleeve.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“T have every desire to help you. I’m tired. We all are. Uncle Howard has been keeping us awake at nights,” he said, shaking his head.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">His cheeks seemed more hollowed out and the dark circles under his eyes were almost purplish. Again she felt selfish and awful. She thought of no one but herself and did not pause to imagine that other people at High Place had problems of their own. For example, who knew if at nights Francis was called to tend upon his sickly uncle. She could picture Florence, direct- ing her son to hold up an oil lamp as she pressed cold com- presses against the old man’s face. Or perhaps other tasks were handed to the young men. Virgil and Francis, undressing the frail, bleached body of Howard Doyle, applying ointments and medications in a closed room reeking of impending death.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi flexed her fingers, raising them to her mouth, and recalling, vaguely, that horrid nightmare in which the pale man had extended his arms toward her.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Virgil said he has an old wound. But what is the injury?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Ulcers that won't heal. But it won't be the end of him. It'll never be the end of him.” Francis let out a low, sad chuckle, his</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_138" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 138 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_182742_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">eyes on the stone statue of Agnes. “I’ll take you to town early tomorrow, before the others wake up. Before breakfast, like the last time. And if you should want to take your suitcase—”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You'll have to think of a more creative way to get me to leave,” she replied.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">They began to drift, quietly, back toward the iron gates of the cemetery. She brushed the cool tops of the headstones as they walked. At one point they passed a dead gray oak tree that lay upon the ground, clumps of honey-colored mushrooms growing upon the rotten bark. Francis bent down, running a finger across the smooth caps, just as she’d touched the head- stones.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“What has made Catalina so miserable?” she asked. “She was happy when she married. Stupidly happy, my father would have said. Is Virgil cruel to her? Last night, when I spoke to him, he was hard, he had no pity.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“It’s the house,” Francis murmured. By now the black gates with their snakes were in sight. The ouroboros, cast- ing shadows upon the ground. “It wasn’t made for love, the house.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Any place is made for love,” she protested.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Not this place and not us. You look back two, three gener- ations, as far as youcan. You won't find love. We are incapable of sucha thing.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">His fingers curled around the intricate iron bars, and he stood there, for a second, looking at the ground, before he opened the gate for her.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">That night she had another curious dream. She could not even classify it as a nightmare because she felt calm. Numb, even.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The house had metamorphosed in the dream, but it was not a thing of meat and sinew on this occasion. She walked upon a carpet of moss, the flowers and vines crept up the walls, and long, thin stacks of mushrooms glowed a pale yellow, lightening up the ceiling and the floor. It was as if the forest had tiptoed into the house in the middle of the night and left a part of itself inside. As Noemi descended the stair- case, her hands brushed upon a banister covered in flowers.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She walked down a hallway that was thick with clumps of mushrooms as high as her thighs and peered at paintings that were hidden under layers of leaves.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">In the dream she knew where she ought to go. There were noiron gates to greet her at the cemetery, but why would there be any? This was the time before the cemetery, when they were building a rose garden upon the mountain slope.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_139" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 139 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_182747_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">A garden, though no flowers grew here yet. No flowers had taken. It was peaceful here, at the edge of the pine forest, with the mist shrouding rocks and shrubs.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi heard voices, very loud, and then a piercing scream, but everything was so still, so calm, that it calmed her too. Even when the screams changed in pitch and seemed to grow in intensity she did not fear.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She reached a clearing and beheld a woman who lay on the ground. Her belly was huge and distended, and she ap- peared to be in the midst of her labor, which would explain the screams. Attending her were several women who were holding her hand, brushing the limp hair from her face, mut- tering to her. Men held candles in their hands, others carried lanterns.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi noticed a little girl sitting on a chair, her blond hair tied in a pigtail. She carried a white cloth in her arms, meant to swaddle an infant. A man sat behind the child, with his ringed hand on her shoulder. A ring of amber.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The scene was a little ridiculous. A woman, panting and giving birth in the dirt while the man and the child sat in velvet-upholstered chairs, as if they were observing a theater performance.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The man tapped his finger on the child’s shoulder. One, two, three times.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">How long had they sat there, in the dark? How long since the labor had begun? But it wouldn’t be long now. The time had come.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The pregnant woman clutched someone’s hand and let out a long, low moan, and there was a wet sound, the slap of flesh against the damp earth.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The man stood up and approached the woman, and when he moved, the people surrounding her stepped aside, as ifa sea were parting.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Slowly he bent down and carefully held the child the woman had birthed.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Death, overcome,” the man said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">But when he raised his arms, Noemi saw he held no child. The woman had given birth to a gray lump of flesh, al- most egg-shaped, covered in a thick membrane and slick with blood.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">It was a tumor. It did not live. Yet it pulsated gently. The lump quivered, and as it did the membrane ruptured and slid aside. It burst, sending a golden cloud of dust into the air, and the man breathed in the dust. The woman’s attendants, the people with their candles and lanterns, and all the onlookers</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_140" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 140 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_182751_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">moved closer, holding their hands up, as if to touch the golden dust, which slowly, ever so slowly, fell upon the ground.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Everyone had forgotten the woman, focused now, singu- larly, on the lump that the man was hoisting above his head.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Only the little girl paid the shivering, exhausted figure on the ground any attention. The child approached her, pressed the cloth she had been holding against the womans face, as if veiling a bride, and held it tight. The woman convulsed, un- able to breathe; she tried to scratch the child, but the woman was exhausted and the child, her cheeks red, held on tight. As the woman quivered and suffocated, the man repeated the same words.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Death, overcome,” he said, and he raised his eyes, staring at Noemi.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">It was then, when he looked at her, that she remembered to be afraid, that she remembered revulsion and horror, and turned her face away. Her mouth had the coppery taste of blood, and in her ears there was a faint buzzing sound.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">When Noemi woke up she was standing at the foot of the stairs, moonlight streaming through the colored glass win- dows, tinting her pale nightgown yellow and red. A clock struck the hour and floorboards creaked, and she rested a hand on the banister, listening intently.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_141" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 141 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_182757_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">oemi knocked and waited, and waited some</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">more, but no one came to the door. She stood</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">outside Marta’s house, nervously pulling at her</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">purse’s strap before finally conceding defeat and walking back toward Francis, who was looking at her curi- ously. They’d parked the car near the town square and walked over together, even though she’d told him he could wait for her just like last time. But he said he could use the walk. She wondered if he was trying to keep an eye on her.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“No one seems to be home,” Noemi said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Do you want to wait?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“No. Ineed to stop by the health clinic.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He nodded, and they slowly headed back toward what con- stituted the downtown section of El Triunfo, where there was areal road instead of muddy paths. Noemi was afraid the doc- tor wouldn’t be in yet either, but as they reached the door of the clinic, Julio Camarillo rounded the corner.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Dr. Camarillo,” she said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Good morning,” he replied. He was carrying a paper bag under one arm and his medical bag in the other. “You’re up early. Will you hold this for amoment?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Francis stretched out his hands and grabbed the medical bag. Dr. Camarillo took out a set of keys and unlocked the door, holding it open for them. Then he walked toward the counter, placed the paper bag behind it, and smiled at them.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“T don’t think I’ve met you officially,” Julio said, “but I’ve seen you at the post office before, with Dr. Cummins. You’re Francis, aren’t you?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The blond man nodded. “I’m Francis,” he said simply.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Yes, when I took over Dr. Corona’s practice in the winter he actually mentioned you and your father. I think they might have played cards together. A good chap, Dr. Corona. But, any- way, is that hand bothering you, Noemi? Is that why you’re here?”</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_142" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 142 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_182802_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Could we talk? Do you have time?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Sure. Come in,” the doctor said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi followed him into his office. She turned her head to see if Francis planned to accompany her, but he was sitting on one of the chairs in the lobby, his hands in his pockets, his gaze on the floor. If he wanted to keep an eye on her, he wasn’t doing that good a job; he could easily have eavesdropped on her entire conversation. She was relieved to think he wasn’t interested in that. Noemi closed the door and sat across from Dr. Camarillo, who settled behind his desk.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Now, what is the matter>”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Catalina had a seizure,” Noemi said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“A seizure? Is she epileptic?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“No. I purchased a tonic, a medicine from that woman, Marta Duval. Catalina asked me for it, she said it would help her sleep. But when she drank it she had a seizure. I went to see Marta this morning, but she wasn’t there. I wanted to ask if you’ve heard of something like that happening around town before, if her medications have made anyone ill like that.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Marta goes to Pachuca to see her daughter, or she goes on herb collection trips, which is probably why you didn’t find her. But as to this happening before, I haven’t heard of such a thing. Dr. Corona would have mentioned it, I’m sure. Did Ar- thur Cummins examine your cousin?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“He said an opium tincture caused the seizure.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Dr. Camarillo grabbed a pen and twirled it between his fin- gers. “You know, opium was used to treat epilepsy not so long ago. There’s always the possibility of allergic reactions with any medicine, but Marta is very careful with this sort of stuff.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Dr. Cummins called her a quack.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He shook his head and set the pen back on the desk. “She’s no quack. Many people go to Marta for remedies, and she helps them well enough. If I thought she was endangering the health of the townsfolk I wouldn't allow it.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“But what if Catalina took too much of the tincture?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“An overdose? Yes, of course an overdose would be quite awful. She might lose consciousness, she might vomit, but the thing is, Marta wouldn’t be able to procure an opium tincture.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“What do you mean?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Dr. Camarillo laced his hands together, his elbows resting on the desk. “It’s not the kind of medical care she provides. An opium tincture is a remedy you could find at a drugstore. Marta makes remedies using local herbs and plants. There are no poppies here with which to make a tincture.”</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_143" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 143 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_182806_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Then you're saying it must have been something else that caused her to be sick?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I can’t say with certainty.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She frowned, unable to make heads or tails of this infor- mation. She’d gone looking for an easy answer, but there was none. Nothing seemed to be easy here.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I’m sorry I can’t be of more help. Maybe I can look at your rash before you leave? Have you changed the bandage?” Dr. Ca- marillo asked.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“T haven't. It has completely slipped my mind.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She hadn’t even opened the little jar with the zinc paste. Julio took off the bandage, and Noemi expected to see the same raw, red skin. Perhaps it would look even worse than the last time. Instead, her wrist was completely healed. There was not a single bump blemishing her skin. It seemed to startle the doctor.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Well, this is quite the surprise. Why, it’s vanished,” Dr. Ca- marillo said. “I don’t think I’ve seen such a thing before. Usu- ally it takes seven or ten days, sometimes weeks, for the skin to clear. It’s barely been two days.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I must be lucky,” she ventured.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Extremely,” he said. “What a thing. Do you need anything else? If not, Ican tell Marta you were looking for her.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She thought about her odd dream and the second sleep- walking episode. Yet she didn’t feel the doctor would be able to assist her with that either. It was as he’d said: he wasn’t very useful at all. She was beginning to think Virgil had been right when he told her Camarillo was too young and inexperienced. Or maybe she was being grumpy. She was most definitely tired. The anxiety of the previous day hit her suddenly.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“That would be an utter kindness,” she said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi had expected to return to her room without anyone noticing, but of course that was too much to ask. Not even an hour after Francis and Noemi had parked the car, Florence came looking for her. She had Noemi’s lunch tray with her, which she deposited on the table. She didn’t say anything un- pleasant, but her face was fiercely sour. It was the face of a warden ready to squelch a riot.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Virgil would like to speak with you,” she said. “I assume you can eat and be presentable in an hour?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Of course,” Noemi replied.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Good. I’ll come to fetch you.”</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_144" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 144 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_182811_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She did indeed return in exactly one hour and proceeded to guide Noemi to Virgil’s room. When they stopped in front of his door, Florence knocked once, her knuckles so soft upon the wooden surface that Noemi thought he’d never hear them, but he spoke, loud and clear.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Enter.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Florence turned the doorknob and held the door open for Noemi. Once the young woman stepped inside, Florence care- fully closed the door again.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The first thing she noticed upon walking into Virgil’s room was an imposing painting of Howard Doyle, hands clasped together, an amber ring on his finger, staring down at her across the room. Virgil’s bed was half hidden behind a three- fold painted screen depicting branches of lilacs and roses. The divider created a sitting area, with a faded rug and a pair of shabby leather chairs.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You've gone to town again this morning,” Virgil said. His voice came from behind the divider. “Florence dislikes it when you do that. Just off, without a word.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She approached the painted screen. She noticed that among the flowers and the ferns there lay a snake. It was clev- erly hidden, the eye peeking from behind a clump of roses. It lay in wait, like the snake in the garden of Eden.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I thought driving alone into town was the issue,” Noemi replied.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“The roads are bad, and the rains will grow stronger any day now. Torrential rains. The soil turns into a sea of mud. The rain flooded the mines the year I was born. We lost every- thing.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“It does rain, I’ve noticed. And the road is not good. But the roads are not impassable.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“They will be. There’s been a lull in the rain, but it will fall ferociously very soon. Fetch me the robe on the chair, please.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She grabbed the heavy crimson robe that had been left on one of the chairs and walked back toward the wooden screen. She was startled to see Virgil hadn’t bothered putting on a shirt and stood there half naked and nonchalant. This was much too casual; it was frankly immodest, and she blushed in shame.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“How, then, will Dr. Cummins make his way here? He’s sup- posed to visit every week,” she said, averting her gaze quickly as she held out the robe. She tried to maintain a cool tone of voice despite the warmth on her cheeks. If he wanted to mor- tify her, he must try harder.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_145" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 145 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_182815_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“He has a truck. Do you honestly think the cars we have are fit for driving constantly up and down the mountain?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I assumed Francis would let me know if he felt it was hazardous.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Francis,” Virgil said. She glanced at him when he said the name. He tied the robe’s belt. “It seems you spend most of your time with him rather than with Catalina.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Was he reproaching her? No, she thought it was slightly different. He was assessing her, the same way a jeweler might gaze at a diamond, trying to measure its clarity or an ento- mologist would look at a butterfly’s wings under the micro- scope.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I have spent a reasonable amount of time with him.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Virgil smiled without any pleasure. “You are so careful with your words. So poised in front of me. I picture you, in your city of cocktail parties and careful words. Do you ever lower your mask there?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He gestured for her to sit down in one of the leather chairs. She pointedly ignored the gesture. “It’s funny, here I thought you could teach me a thing or two about masquerades,” she replied.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“What do you mean?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“It’s not the first time Catalina was ill like this. She drank the same tincture and had the same bad reaction.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She had thought to say nothing of the matter, but she wanted to gauge his reaction. He’d assessed her. Now it was her turn.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You have indeed been spending time with Francis,” Virgil said, distaste clear on his face. “Yes, I forgot to mention that previous episode.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“How convenient.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“What? The doctor explained to you that she has depres- sive tendencies, and you thought it was all lies. If ’'d told you she was suicidal—”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“She’s not suicidal,” Noemi protested.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Well, of course, since you seem to know everything,” Vir- gil muttered. He looked a little bored and waved a hand, as if shooing an invisible insect. Shooing her. The gesture made her furious.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You took Catalina from the city and brought her here, and if she is suicidal then it’s your fault,” she replied.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She wished to be cruel. She wanted to repay him with the same coin he’d used with her before, but once she had spit her venom she regretted the words, because for once he seemed</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_146" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 146 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_182834_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">upset. He looked as if she’d physically stricken him, a pure mo- ment of pain or perhaps shame.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Virgil,” she began, but he shook his head, silencing her.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“No, you're correct. It’s my fault. Catalina fell in love with me for the wrong reasons.” Virgil sat very straight in his seat, his eyes fixed on her, his hands resting on the arms of the chair. “Sit, please.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She was not ready to concede. She did not sit. Instead she stood behind her chair, leaning forward on its back. She had a vague thought that it would also be easier to run out of the room if she was standing. She wasn’t sure why she thought this. It was a disquieting thought, that she must be ready to spring up like a gazelle and escape. It was, she concluded, that she did not like to be speaking to him alone in his room.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">His terrain. His burrow.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She suspected Catalina had never set foot in this room. Or if she had, it had been a brief invitation. No trace of her re- mained. The furniture, the great large painting bequeathed by his father, the wooden screen, the ancient wallpaper streaked with faint traces of mold, these all belonged to Virgil Doyle. His taste, his things. Even his features seemed to complement the room. The blond hair was striking against the dark leather, his face seemed made of alabaster when framed with folds of red velvet.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Your cousin has a wild imagination,” Virgil said. “I think she saw in me a tragic, romantic figure. A boy who lost his mother at a young age in a senseless tragedy, whose family’s fortune evaporated in the years of the Revolution, who grew up with a sick father in a crumbling mansion in the moun- tains.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Yes. It must have pleased her. At first. He had a vehemence that Catalina would have found appealing and, in his home, with the mist outside and the glint of silver candelabra inside, he would have shined very brightly. How long, Noemi won- dered, until the novelty wore off?</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He, perhaps sensing her question, smirked. “No doubt she pictured the house as a delightful, rustic refuge which, with a little effort, could be made cheerier. Of course, it is not as if my father would allow even a single curtain to be changed. We exist at his pleasure.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He turned his head to gaze at the painting bearing How- ard Doyle’s likeness, a finger tapping gently against the chair’s armrest.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“And would you want to change a single curtain?”</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_147" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 147 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_182838_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I'd change a number of things. My father hasn’t left this house in decades. To him this is the ideal vision of the world and nothing more. I’ve seen the future and understand our limitations.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“If that is the case, if change is possible—”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Change of a certain type,” Virgil agreed. “But not a change so grand that I’d become something I am not. You can’t change the essence of a thing. That is the problem. The point, I suppose, is that Catalina wanted someone else. She didn’t want me, flesh and blood and flawed. She was immediately unhappy, and yes, it is my fault. I could not live up to her ex- pectations. What she saw in me, it was never there.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Immediately. Why, then, had Catalina not returned home? But even as she asked herself the question she knew the an- swer. The family. Everyone would have been appalled, and the society pages would have been filled with the most poisonous ink. Exactly as her father now feared.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“What did you see in her?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi’s father had been sure it was money. She didn’t think Virgil would admit it, but she felt confident that she might be able to discern the truth, to read between the lines. To approach the answer, even if it remained veiled.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“My father is ill. He is, in fact, dying. Before passing away, he wanted to see me married. He wanted to know I would have a wife and children; that the family line would not die out. It was not the first time he asked this of me, and it was not the first time I complied. I was married once before.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I did not know that,” Noemi said. It quite surprised her. “What happened?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“She was everything my father thought an ideal wife should be, except that he forgot to consult me on the matter,” Virgil said with a chuckle. “She was, in fact, Arthur’s daughter. My father had gotten it in his head since we were children that we would marry. ‘One day, when you're married,’ they’d tell us. Such repetition didn’t help. It had the opposite effect. When I turned twenty-three we were wed. She disliked me. I found her dull.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Nevertheless, I suppose we might have managed to build something of value between us if it hadn’t been for the mis- carriages. She had four of them, and they wore her down. She abandoned me.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“She divorced you?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He nodded. “Yes, and eventually, implicitly, I realized my father wanted me to remarry. I took a few trips to Guadalajara and then to Mexico City. I met women who were interesting</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_148" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 148 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_182843_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">and pretty, who would have no doubt pleased my father. But Catalina was the one who really caught my attention. She was sweet. It’s not a quality that is in great abundance at High Place. I liked that. I liked her softness, her romantic notions. She wanted a fairy tale, and I wanted to give her that.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Then, of course, it all went wrong. Not merely her illness, but her loneliness, her bouts of sadness. I thought she under- stood what it would mean to live with me and I understood what it would be like living with her. I was wrong. And here we are.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">A fairy tale, yes. Snow White with the magical kiss andthe beauty who transforms the beast. Catalina had read all those stories for the younger girls, and she had intoned each line with great dramatic conviction. It had been a performance. Here was the result of Catalina’s daydreams. Here was her fairy tale. It amounted to a stilted marriage that, coupled with her sickness and her mental tribulations, must place an exhaust- ing burden on her shoulders.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“If it’s the house she dislikes, you could take her some- where else.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“My father wants us at High Place.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You must make your own life one day, no?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He smiled. “My own life. I don’t know if you’ve noticed, but none of us can have our own lives. My father needs me here, and now my wife is sick, and it is the same story. We have to stay. You do realize the difficulty of the situation?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi rubbed her hands together. Yes, she did. She didn’t like it, but she did. She was tired. She felt like they kept going in circles. Perhaps Francis was correct, and it was best to pack her bags. But, no, no, she refused.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He turned his gaze on her. Blue and intense, the blue of lapis lazuli, carefully ground. “Well, we seem to have drifted from the topic I had in mind when I called you here. I wanted to apologize to you for my words the last time we met. I was not in a good frame of mind. I still am not. Anyway, if I’ve upset you then I am very sorry,” Virgil said, quite surprising her.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Thank you,” she replied.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I hope we can be friendly. There is no need to act as though we are enemies.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I know we aren't.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“We've gotten off on the wrong foot, I’m afraid. Perhaps we might try again. I promise I’ll ask Dr. Cummins to begin asking about psychiatrists in Pachuca, as an option down the</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_149" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 149 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_182848_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">line. You can help me pick one; we might even write to him together.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“T’d like that.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“A truce, then?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“We're not at war, remember?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Ah, yes. Nevertheless,” he said, extending his hand. Noemi hesitated, then stepped from behind the chair and shook it. His grip was firm, the hand large, covering her own tiny one.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She excused herself and left. When she was walking back to her room she saw Francis standing in front of a door, open- ing it. Her footsteps made him halt, and he looked at her. He inclined his head, in a mute greeting, but said nothing.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She wondered if Florence had chided him for doing Noemi’s bidding. Maybe he would be summoned to stand be- fore Virgil, and he’d tell Francis the same thing he’d said to Noemi: It seems you spend most of your time with her. She pictured a quarrel. Muted. Howard didn’t like loud noises, and confrontations must take place in whispers.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He won’t help me again, she thought as she gazed at his hesi- tant face. I’ve exhausted his goodwill.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Francis,” she said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He pretended not to hear her. Gently the young man closed the door behind him and disappeared from sight. He was swal- lowed by one of the many chambers of the house, into one of the bellies of this beast.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She pressed a palm against the door, thought better of it, and kept on walking, keenly aware that she had already caused too much trouble. She wanted to make it better. She decided to seek out Florence and found her talking with Lizzie in the kit- chen, their voices a whisper.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Florence, do you have a minute?” she asked.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Your cousin is napping. If you want—”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“It’s not about Catalina.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Florence gestured toward the maid, then turned to Noemi and motioned for her to follow her. They went into a room she had not visited before. It had a large, solid table with an old- fashioned sewing machine set atop it. Open shelves held sew- ing baskets and yellowed fashion magazines. On a wall you could see old nails, signaling the spot where there had once been paintings and now there were smudges, faint traces of frames. But the room was very tidy, very clean.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“What do you need?” Florence asked.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I asked Francis to take me to town this morning. I know you don’t like it when we leave without speaking to you. I</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_150" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 150 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_182852_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">wanted you to know it was my fault. You shouldn’t be angry at him.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Florence sat down on a large chair that was set next to the table, her fingers laced together, and stared at Noemi. “You think me harsh, don’t you? No, don’t deny it.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Strict would be the most appropriate word,” Noemi said politely.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“It is important to maintain a sense of order in one’s house, in one’s life. It helps you determine your place in the world, where you belong. Taxonomical classifications help place each creature atop its right branch. It’s no good to forget yourself, nor your obligations. Francis has duties, he has chores. You pull him away from those chores. You make him forget his obligations.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“But surely he doesn’t have chores all day long.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Doesn't he? How would you know? Even if his days are made of leisure, why should he spend them with you?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I don’t mean to take up all his time, but I don’t see—”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“He’s silly when he is with you. He completely forgets who he’s supposed to be. And do you think Howard would let him have you?” Florence shook her head. “The poor boy,” she mut- tered. “What do you want, hmm? What do you want from us? There’s nothing left to give.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I wanted to apologize,” Noemi said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Florence pressed a hand against her right temple and closed her eyes. “You have. Go, go.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">And like the wretched creature that Florence had men- tioned, that does not know its place nor how to find it, Noemi sat on the stairs for a while, staring at the nymph on the newel post and contemplating the motes of dust dancing in a ray of light.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_151" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 151 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_182857_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">lorence would not let Noemi sit alone with Catalina.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">One of the maids, Mary, had been ordered to stand</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">guard in a corner. Noemi was not to be trusted ever</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">again. Nobody said that was the case, but while she approached her cousin’s bed the maid moved slowly around, arranging the clothes in the armoire, folding a blanket. Need- less tasks.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Could you do that later, please?” she asked Mary.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“No time for it in the morning,” the maid replied, her voice even.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Mary, please.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Don’t worry about her,” Catalina said. “Sit.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Oh, I...Yes, it doesn’t matter,” Noemi said, trying not to be upset about this. She wanted to maintain a positive facade for Catalina. Besides, Florence had said she could have a half hour with Catalina, nothing more, and she wanted to make the best of it. “You look much better.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Liar,” Catalina said, but she smiled.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Should I fluff your pillows? Hand you your slippers so tonight you can dance like one of the Twelve Dancing Prin- cesses?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You liked the illustrations in that book,” Catalina said softly.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I did. I admit I'd read it right now if I could.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The maid began fussing with the curtains, turning her back to them, and Catalina gave Noemi an eager look. “Maybe you'd read me poetry? There’s my old book of poems there. You know how I like Sor Juana.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She did remember the book, which rested on the night table. Like the tome packed with fairy tales, this was a familiar treasure. “Which one should I read?” Noemi asked.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Foolish Men.’”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi turned the pages. There it was, the well-worn pages as she remembered. And there too was an unusual element. A</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_152" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 152 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_182901_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">yellow, folded piece of paper tucked against the pages. Noemi glanced at her cousin. Catalina said nothing, her lips were pressed tight, but in her eyes Noemi read a naked fear. She glanced in Mary’s direction. The woman was still busy with the curtains. Noemi pocketed the piece of paper and began reading. She went through several poems, keeping her voice steady. Eventually Florence arrived at the doorway carrying a silver tray with a matching teapot and a cup and a handful of cookies on a porcelain plate.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Tt’s time to let Catalina rest,” Florence said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Of course.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi clapped the book shut and docilely bid her cousin goodbye. When Noemi reached her room, she noticed that Florence had been in there. There was a tray with a cup of tea and the handful of cookies, like Catalina’s.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi ignored the tea and closed the door. She didn’t have an appetite and had forgotten to smoke a cigarette in ages. This whole situation was souring her to everything.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi unfolded the piece of paper. She recognized Cata- lina’s handwriting on a corner. “This is proof,” she said. Noemi frowned and unfolded the letter a second time, wondering what Catalina had written. Would it be a repeat of that strange missive she had sent to Noemi’s father? The letter that had started it all.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The letter, however, was not her cousin’s as she’d thought. It was older, the paper brittle, and it seemed to have been torn from a journal. It was not dated, although it seemed to be the page of a diary entry.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">I put these thoughts down on paper because it is the only way</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">to remain firm in my resolve. Tomorrow I may lose courage but</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">these words should anchor me to the here and now. To the pre- sent moment. I hear their voices constantly, whispering. They glow at night. Perhaps that might be endurable, this place would be endurable, if not for him. Our lord and master. Our</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">God. An egg, split asunder, and a mighty serpent rising from</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">it, expanding wide its jaws. Our great legacy, spun in cartil-</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">age and blood and roots so very deep. Gods cannot die. That is what we have been told, what Mother believes. But Mother cannot protect me, cannot save any of us. It is up to me.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Whether this is sacrilege or simple murder, or both. He beat</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">me when he found out about Benito but I swore there and then</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">that I would never bear a child nor do his will. I believe firmly</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">this death will be no sin. It is a release and my salvation. R.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_153" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 153 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_182906_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">R, a single letter as a signature. Ruth. Could this truly be a page from Ruth’s journal? She didn’t think Catalina would have faked such a thing, even if it bore an uncanny resem- blance to the rambling letter her cousin had penned. But where would Catalina have found it? The house was large and old. She could picture Catalina walking the darkened hallways. A loose floorboard lifted, the elusive diary fragment hidden beneath the wooden plank.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Head bent over the letter, she bit her lips. Reading this scrap of paper with those eerie sentences could make anyone start believing in ghosts or curses or both. Of course, she’d never given credence to the idea of things that go bump in the night. Fantasies and fancies, that’s what she told herself. She’d read The Golden Bough, nodding at its chapter on the expulsion of evils, she’d curiously leafed through a journal detailing the connection between ghosts and sickness in Tonga, and been amused by a letter to the editor of Folklore detailing an en- counter with a headless spirit. She was not in the habit of be- lieving in the supernatural.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">This is proof, Catalina had said. But proof of what? She laid the letter on the table and smoothed it out. She read it again.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Put the facts together, you fool, she told herself, chewing on a nail. And what were the facts? That her cousin spoke about a presence in this house, including voices. Ruth also described voices. Noemi had heard no voices, but she’d had bad dreams and sleepwalked, which she hadn't done in years.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">One could conclude this was a case of three silly, nervous women. Physicians of old would have diagnosed it as hyster- ics. But one thing Noemi was not was hysterical.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">If the three of them were not hysterical, then the three of them had truly come in contact with something inside this house. But must it be supernatural? Must it be a curse? A ghost? Could there be a more rational answer? Was she see- ing a pattern where there wasn’t any? After all, that’s what humans did: look for patterns. She could be weaving three dis- parate stories into a narrative.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She wanted to talk to someone else about this because otherwise she was going to wear the soles of her shoes off walking back and forth in her room. Noemi slid the paper into her sweater’s pocket, grabbed her oil lamp, and went to find Francis. He had been avoiding her for the past couple of days —she assumed Florence had also given him the speech about chores and duties—but she didn’t think he’d slam the door in her face if she went to him, and, anyway, it wasn’t as if she was</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_154" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 154 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_182910_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">going to ask him for a favor this time. She simply wished to chat. Emboldened, she sought him out.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He opened his door, and before he could properly greet her she spoke. “May I come in? I need to talk to you.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Now?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Five minutes. Please?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He blinked, unsure; cleared his throat for good measure. “Yes. Yes, of course.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The walls in his room were covered with colorful draw- ings and prints of botanical specimens. She counted a dozen butterflies carefully pinned under glass and five lovingly painted watercolors of mushrooms, their names in tiny print beneath them. There were two bookcases laden with leather- bound volumes and books stacked on the floor in tidy piles. The smell of weathered pages and ink permeated the room, like the perfume from an exotic bouquet.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Virgil’s room had a sitting area, but Francis’s did not. She could see the narrow bed with a dark green coverlet and a richly carved headboard festooned with leaves, the pervasive motif of the snake eating its tail at the center. There was a matching desk, covered with more books. On a corner of the desk, an empty cup and a plate. That is where he must have his meals. He didn’t utilize the table in the middle of the room.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">As she walked next to it, she realized why: the table was covered with papers and drawing instruments. She looked at the sharpened pencils, the bottles of india ink, and the nibs of pens. A box with watercolors, the brushes sitting inside a cup. There were many charcoal drawings, but others were inked. Botanical sketches, the lot of them.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You're an artist,” she said, touching the edge of a drawing showing a dandelion while she held the oil lamp with her other hand.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I draw,” he said, sounding abashed. “I’m afraid I have nothing to offer you. I’ve finished my tea.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I despise the tea they brew here. It’s terrible,” she said, looking at another drawing, this one of a dahlia. “I tried my hand at painting once. I thought it made sense, you know? My father being in the dye and paint business, after all. But I was no good. Plus, I like photos better. They capture the thing in the moment.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“But painting is the repeated exposure to a thing. It cap- tures the essence of the object.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You're poetic too.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He looked embarrassed. “Let’s sit,” he said, taking the lan- tern from her hand and setting it down on the desk where he</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_155" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 155 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_182915_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">had already placed a few candles. Another oil lamp, very much like her own, larger, rested on his night table. The glass on it was tinted yellow, and it varnished the room in warm amber tones.</p>
