Except, perhaps, for that golden presence that she still could not identify and that she could not even declare a person. It didn’t feel like a person. Not like Ruth.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Can you hear me?” she asked. “Or are you like the grooves in a vinyl record?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She wasn’t afraid of the girl. She was a young woman, abused and abandoned. Her presence wasn’t malicious, merely anxious.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“T’m not sorry,” Ruth said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“My name is Noemi. I’ve seen you before, but I’m not sure you understand me.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Not sorry.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi didn’t think the girl was going to offer her more than those scant words, but suddenly Ruth lifted her head and stared at her.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Mother cannot, will not protect you. No one will protect you.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Mother is dead, Noemi thought. You killed her. But she doubted there was any point in reminding someone who was a corpse, long buried, about such things. Noemi stretched out a hand, touching the girl’s shoulder. She felt real under her fingers.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You have to kill him. Father will never let you go. That was my mistake. I didn’t do it right.” The girl shook her head.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“How should you have done it?” Noemi asked.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I didn’t do it right. He is a god! He is a god!”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The girl began sobbing and clasped both hands against her mouth, rocking back and forth. Noemi tried to embrace her, but Ruth flung herself against the floor and curled up there, her hands still covering her mouth. Noemi knelt down next to her.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_205" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 205 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_183306_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Ruth, don’t cry,” she said, and as she spoke Ruth’s body turned gray, white speckles of mold spreading across her face and hands, and the girl wept, black tears sliding down her cheeks, bile trickling out of her mouth and nose.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Ruth began to tear at herself with her nails, letting out a hoarse scream. Noemi pushed herself backward, bumping against the bed. The girl was writhing; now she scratched at the floor, her nails tearing at the wood, driving splinters into her palms.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi clacked her teeth together in fear and thought to cry too, but then she recalled the words, the mantra.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Open your eyes,” Noemi said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">And Noemi did. She opened her eyes, and the room was dark. She was alone. It rained again. She stood up and slid the curtain away. The distant sound of thunder was unsettling. Where was her bracelet? The bracelet against the evil eye. But that would do no good now. Inside the night table’s drawer she found her pack of cigarettes and her lighter; those were still there.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi flicked the lighter on, watching the flame bloom, and then closed it, returning it to the drawer.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_206" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 206 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_183311_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">rancis came back to see her the next morning, giving</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">her another small amount of the tincture and point-</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">ing out the items that were safe to eat. When night</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">fell, he reappeared with a tray of food and told her that after she finished her dinner they were supposed to speak with Virgil, who awaited them in the office.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">It was too dark, even with the oil lamp in Francis’s hands, to look at the portraits running along the wall that led toward the library, but she wished she could have stopped and gazed at Ruth’s picture. It was an impulse born of curiosity and sym- pathy. She had been a prisoner, like her.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi was struck by the unpleasant scent of moldy books as soon as Francis opened the door to the office. Funny how she’d gotten used to it and barely noticed it in days past. She wondered if that meant the tincture was doing its job.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Virgil sat behind the desk. The subdued lighting in the pan- eled room gave him the appearance of a Caravaggio painting and rendered his face almost bloodless. There was a stillness to his body, like that of a wild animal camouflaging itself. His fingers were laced together, and when he saw them he leaned forward in greeting, smiling.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You seem to be doing better,” Virgil said. Noemi sat before him, Francis at her side, her mute stare the one answer to his question. “I’ve asked you here because we need to clarify a few points. Francis says you understand the situation and you're willing to cooperate with us,” Virgil continued.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“If you mean I realize I can’t leave this horrid house, yes, that has become unfortunately clear.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Don’t be sore about it, Noemi—it’s quite a lovely house once it gets to know you. Now, I guess the question is whether you are determined to be a nuisance or whether you'll will- ingly join the family?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">On the walls the three deer heads cast long shadows. “You have a very interesting notion of ‘willingly, ” Noemi said. “Are</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_207" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 207 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_183315_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">you offering any other option to me? I don’t think so. I’ve de- cided to stay alive, if that is what you’d like to know. I wouldn’t want to end up in a pit, like those poor miners.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“We didn’t dump them in a pit. They’re all buried in the cemetery. And they needed to die. You must make the soil fertile.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“With human bodies. Mulch, isn’t that right?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“They would have died anyway. It was an assortment of underfed peasants, riddled with lice.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Was your first wife also a peasant, riddled with lice? Did you also use her to make the soil more fertile?” Noemi asked. She wondered if her portrait was hanging outside, with the pictures of all the other Doyles. A wretched young woman with her chin up, trying to maintain her smile for the camera.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Virgil shrugged. “No. But she was inadequate all the same, and I can’t say that I miss her.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“How charming.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You won't make me feel bad about that, Noemi. The strong survive, the weak are left behind. I think you're quite strong,” he said. “And what a pretty face you have. Dark skin, dark eyes. Such a novelty.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Dark meat, she thought. Nothing but meat, she was the equivalent of a cut of beef inspected by the butcher and wrapped up in waxed paper. An exotic little something to stir the loins and make the mouth water.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Virgil stood up, rounding the desk and standing behind them, a firm hand resting on the back of each of their chairs. “My family, as you might know, has strived to keep the blood- line clean. Our selective breeding has allowed us to transmit the most desirable traits. Our compatibility with the fungi in this house is the result of that. There’s one tiny problem.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Virgil began walking around, circling them, looking down at the desk and toying with a pencil. “Do you know that chestnut trees that stand alone are sterile? They require cross- pollination from another tree. This seems to have become the case with us too. My mother gave my father two living chil- dren, yes, but she had many stillbirths. It’s the same story when you look back in time. Stillbirths, crib deaths. Before Agnes, my father had two other wives, neither of which was any good.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“On occasion you need to inject new blood into the mix, so to speak. Of course my father has always been very stubborn about these things, insisting that we must not mingle with the rabble.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Superior and inferior traits, after all,” Noemi said dryly.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_208" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 208 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_183320_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Virgil smiled. “Exactly. The old man even brought earth from England to ensure the conditions here would be like the ones in our motherland; he wasn’t about to entertain the lo- cals. But the way things have gone, it has become a necessity. A question of survival.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Hence Richard,” Noemi said. “And hence Catalina.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Yes. Although if I’d seen you before, I might have picked you rather than her. You're healthy, young, and the gloom ra- ther likes you.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I suppose my money doesn’t hurt.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Well, that’s obviously a prerequisite. Your stupid Revolu- tion robbed us of our fortune. We must get it back. Survival, as I said.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Murder, I think that’s the word. You murdered all those miners. You made them sick, you didn’t tell them what was wrong with them, and your doctor, he let them die. And you must have killed Ruth’s lover too. Although she paid you back for that.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You're not being very nice, Noemi,” he said, his eyes fixed on hers. He sounded peeved and turned to Francis. “I thought you had smoothed things out with her.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Noemi won’t try running again,” Francis said, sliding his hand upon her own.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“That’s a good first step. The second step is that you are going to write a letter to your father, explaining that you will remain here until Christmas, to keep Catalina company. Come Christmas, you'll inform him that you’ve been married and in- tend to live with us.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“My father will be upset.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Then you'll have to write a few more letters, to assuage his concerns,” Virgil said smoothly. “Now, why don’t you start writing that first letter.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Now?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Yes. Come here,” Virgil said, patting the chair he had been occupying behind the desk.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi hesitated but stood up and took the seat he was offering. There was a sheet of paper ready and a pen. Noemi stared at the writing instruments but did not pick them up.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Go on,” Virgil said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I don’t know what to say.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Write a convincing message. Because we wouldn’t want your father visiting us and maybe falling ill with an odd dis- ease, would we?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You wouldn’t,” she whispered.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_209" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 209 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_183324_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Virgil leaned down, gripping her shoulder tight. “There’s plenty of space in the mausoleum, and as you pointed out, our physician is not very good at treating illnesses.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi shoved his hand aside and began writing. Virgil turned away.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She kept scribbling, finally signing the letter. When she was done Virgil came back to her side and read the letter, nodding.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Are you happy?” Francis asked. “She’s done her bit.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“She’s far from done her bit,” Virgil muttered. “Florence is rummaging around the house, trying to find Ruth’s old wed- ding dress. We're to have ourselves a wedding ceremony.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Why?” Noemi asked. Her mouth felt dry.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Howard is a stickler for those kind of details. Ceremonies. He does love them.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Where will you find a priest?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“My father can officiate; he’s done so before.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“So I'll be wed in the Church of the Holy Incestuous Mush- room?” she intoned. “I doubt that's valid” “Don’t worry, we will of course drag you to the magistrate at one point.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Drag is the right word.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Virgil slammed the letter down on the desk, startling Noemi. She winced. She recalled his strength. He’d carried her into the house as if she were as light as a feather. His hand, resting against the desk, was large, capable of inflicting tre- mendous damage.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You should consider yourself lucky. I did tell my father Francis might as well tie you to the bed and fuck you tonight, without any preamble, but he doesn’t think that would be right. You're a lady, after all. I disagree. Ladies are not wan- ton, and as we both know, you aren’t exactly a little innocent lamb.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I have no idea—”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Oh, you definitely have a few ideas.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Virgil’s fingers grazed her hair. The slightest touch, which sent a shiver down her body, a dark and delicious feeling coursing down her veins, like imbibing champagne much too quickly. Like in her dreams. She thought of sinking her teeth into his shoulder and biting down, hard. A ferocious pang of desire and hatred.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi jumped up, pushing the chair between herself and Virgil. “Don’t!”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Don’t what?”</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_210" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 210 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_183329_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Stop this,” Francis said, hurrying to her side. He clutched her hand, assuaging her, quickly reminding her with one look that they had, after all, a plan, and then, turning to Virgil, he spoke firmly. “She’s my bride. You need to show her respect.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Virgil seemed unamused by his cousin’s words, that thin, tart smile of his widening, ready to turn into a snarl. She was certain he would push back, but he surprised her by raising his hands in the air in sudden, theatrical surrender.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Well, I guess for once in your life you’ve actually grown a pair of balls. Fine,” Virgil said. “I'll be polite. But she needs to mind her words and learn her place.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“She will. Come,” Francis said, quickly guiding her out of the office, oil lamp in hand, shadows wavering and shifting due to the sudden movement of the light source.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Once outside, he turned to her. “Are you all right?” he asked in a whisper, switching to Spanish.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She did not reply. Noemi pulled him down the hallway, into one of the unused, dusty rooms with chairs and settees covered by white sheets. A huge floor-to-ceiling mirror re- flected them, its top embellished with elaborate carvings of fruits and flowers and the ever-present snake that lurked around every corner in this house. Noemi stopped in her tracks as she stared at the snake, and Francis almost bumped into her, whispering an apology.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You said you'd get supplies for us,” she told him, her eyes on the decoration surrounding the mirror, the fearful snake. “But what about weapons?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Weapons?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Yes. Rifles and guns?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“There are no rifles, not after what happened with Ruth. My uncle Howard keeps a gun in his room, but I wouldn’t be able to have access to it.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“There must be something!”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She was startled by her own vehemence. In the mirror, Noemi saw her face reflected, anxious, and turned away, dis- gusted by the sight of it. Her hands were trembling, and she had to hold on to the back of a chair to steady herself.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Noemi? What is it?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I don't feel safe.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“T reali—”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“It’s a trick. I don’t understand your mind games, but I know I’m not entirely me when Virgil is around,” she said, her hands fluttering up as she brushed the hair away from her face nervously. “Not lately. Magnetic. That’s how Catalina de-</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_211" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 211 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_183333_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">scribed him. Well, no wonder. But it’s not charm alone, is it? You said the house can induce you to do certain things...”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She trailed off. Virgil brought out the worst in Noemi, she disliked him immensely, and yet as of late he also awoke a de- praved thrill in her. Freud talked of death drives: that impulse that makes someone, standing at the edge of a cliff, suddenly want to jump off it. It was surely this ancient principle at work, Virgil tugging at a subconscious string she’d been ignor- ant of. Playing with her.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She wondered if it was like this for the cicadas Francis had mentioned. Singing their mating songs even as they were consumed alive from within, their organs turning to powder while they rocked against each other. Perhaps chirping even more loudly, the shadow of death creating a frenzy of need inside their small bodies, urging them on toward their own destruction.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">What Virgil inspired was violence and carnality, but alsoa heady delight. The joy of cruelty and a velvet black decadence she had tasted only slightly before. This was her greedy, most impulsive self.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Nothing will happen to you,” Francis assured her, setting down his oil lamp on a table shrouded in white.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You don’t know that.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Not when I’m around.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You can’t be around all the time. You weren't there when he grabbed me in the bathroom,” she said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Francis clenched his jaw, almost imperceptibly, shame and anger washing over his features, his face flushing richly. His gallantry was misplaced. He wanted to be her knight and could not. Noemi crossed her arms, tucking her chin down.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“There must be a weapon, please, Francis,” she insisted.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“My straight razor, perhaps. I could give you that. If it would make you feel safer.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“It would.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Then you can have it,” he said; he sounded genuine.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She realized this was but a small gesture, which did not solve her problems. Ruth had carried a rifle, and that did not save her. If this was truly a death drive, a defect of her psy- che now amplified or twisted by the house, then no ordinary weapon could protect her. Yet she appreciated his willingness to help her.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Thank you.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“It’s nothing. I hope you don’t mind bearded men, since I won't be able to shave if you’ve got my razor,” he said, trying to make a quip, trying to lighten the mood.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_212" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 212 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_183338_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“A bit of stubble now and then never hurt anyone,” she re- plied, matching his tone.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He smiled, and the smile, like his voice, was genuine. Everything in High Place was gnarled and begrimed, but he’d been able to grow bright and mindful, like an odd plant that is carried onto the wrong flower bed.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You truly are my friend, aren’t you?” she said. She hadn't quite believed it, half expecting a ruse, but she didn’t think there was one.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You should know the answer by now,” he replied, but not unkindly.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“It’s very difficult, in this place, to discern what’s real from what’s false.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I know.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">They looked at each other, quiet. Noemi began walking around the room, running her hand atop the shrouded fur- niture, feeling the decorations carved into the wood beneath, upsetting the dust that had collected upon the drop-sheets. She raised her head and saw him staring at her, his hands in his pockets. Noemi tugged at one of the white sheets, reveal- ing a sofa upholstered in blue, and sat on it, her feet tucked up under her.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He sat next to her. The mirror that dominated the room was now right in front of them, but it was cloudy with age and distorted their reflections, turning them into phantoms.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Who taught you Spanish?” she asked.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“My father. He liked learning new things, learning lan- guages. He used to tutor me; he even tried tutoring Virgil a little, but he had no interest in such lessons. After he died, I’d help Arthur with documents or errands. Since he also speaks Spanish, I was able to practice with him. I always assumed I’d take Arthur’s place.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Serving in town as your family’s middleman.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“It’s what I was given to expect.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You've had no other desire but that? To serve your fam- ily?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“When I was younger I dreamed I'd go away. But it was the sort of dream only a small child can have, like thinking one day you might join the circus. I didn’t pay it any heed, lately. It was pointless. After what became of my father, I figured, well, he had a stronger personality than I have, he was more auda- cious, and even he could do nothing but obey the will of High Place.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">As he spoke, Francis reached into his jacket’s pocket and took out the little portrait she’d seen before. She leaned down,</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_213" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 213 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_183343_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">looking at it with more care than the first time. It was part of an enamel locket, one side painted blue, decorated with golden lilies of the valley. She traced a flower with a nail.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Did your father know about the gloom?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Before coming to High Place, you mean? No. He married my mother, and she brought him here, but she obviously didn’t mention it. He didn’t know for a while. By the time he learned the whole truth it was too late, and he eventually agreed to stay.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“The same setup that they are offering me, I suppose,” Noemi said. “A chance to be a part of the family. Not that he had much of a choice.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“He loved her, I guess. He loved me. I don’t know.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi handed him back the locket, and he tucked it into his pocket. “Will there truly be a wedding ceremony? A bridal dress?” she asked.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She recalled the rows of pictures in the hallways, fixing each generation in time. And the bridal portraits in Howard’s room. If they could, they would have painted Catalina’s por- trait in the same style. They would have painted Noemi’s por- trait too. Both paintings would have hung side by side atop a mantelpiece. There would also have been a photo of the newly- weds, decked in their fine silks and velvet.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The mirror offered her a vague impression of what such a wedding picture might have looked like, for it captured both Noemi and Francis, their faces solemn.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“It’s tradition. In the old days there would have been a great feast, and every person attending would have given you a gift of silver. Mining has always been our trade, and it all began with silver.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“In England?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Yes.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“And you came chasing more silver here.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Tt had run out, over there. Silver, tin, and our luck. And the people back in England, they suspected us of odd doings. How- ard thought they’d ask fewer questions here, that he’d be able to do as he wished. He wasn’t wrong.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“How many workers died?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“It’s impossible to know.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Have you wondered about it?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Yes,” he whispered, his voice thick with shame.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">This house had been built atop bones. And no one had noticed such an atrocity, rows and rows of people streaming into the house, into the mine, and never leaving. Never to be mourned, never to be found. The serpent does not devour its</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_214" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 214 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_183347_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">tail, it devours everything around it, voracious, its appetite never quenched.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She gazed at the wide-open fangs of the snake surrounding the mirror, and she turned her face and rested her chin on his shoulder. And like that they sat for a long time, she dark and he pale, making an odd contrast amidst all the snowy-white sheets, and around them, like a vignette, the darkness of the house blurring the borders.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_215" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 215 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_183352_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">ow that there was no need for pretense, they let</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">her talk to Catalina without the watchful maid</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">to spy on them. Francis was her companion in-</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">stead. She supposed they saw them as a unit. Two symbiotic organisms, tethered together. Or else, jailer and prisoner. Whatever their reasoning, she appreciated the chance to speak to her cousin and pulled her chair closer to the bed where Catalina was resting. Francis stood on the other side of the room, glancing out the window and tacitly offering them privacy as they spoke in whispers.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I’m sorry I didn’t believe you when I read the letter,” Noemi said. “I should have known.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You couldn’t know,” Catalina said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Still, if ’'d simply fetched you, despite their protestations, we wouldn't be here.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“They wouldn’t have let you. Noemi, it’s enough that you came. Your presence makes me better. It’s like in those stories I used to read: it’s as if you've broken a spell.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">More likely it was the tincture Francis was administering, but Noemi nodded and grasped her cousin’s hands. How she wished that it were true, though! The fairy tales Catalina had shared with her always had good endings. The wicked were punished, order was restored. A prince climbed a tower and fetched down the princess. Even the dark details, such as the cutting of the wicked stepsisters’ heels, faded into oblivion once Catalina declared that everyone lived happily ever after.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Catalina could not recite those magical words—happily ever after—and Noemi had to hope the escape they had formu- lated was not a tall tale. Hope was all they had.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“He knows something is wrong,” Catalina said suddenly, blinking slowly.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The words unsettled Noemi. “Who?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Catalina pressed her lips shut. This had happened before too, that she suddenly, dramatically grew quiet or seemed to</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_216" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 216 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_183356_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">lose her train of thought. As much as Catalina might want to say that she was getting better, she was not herself yet. Noemi brushed a strand of hair behind Catalina’s ear.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Catalina? What’s wrong?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Catalina shook her head and then lay back on the bed, turning her back toward Noemi. Noemi touched her cousin’s shoulder, but Catalina shoved her hand away. Francis walked over toward the bed.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I think she’s tired,” he said. “We should walk back to your room. My mother said she wanted you to try that dress on.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She had not really pictured the dress. It had been the fur- thest thing from her mind. Having no preconceptions, any- thing should have sufficed. Yet she was still surprised when she saw it laid out on her bed, and she regarded it with worry. She did not wish to touch it.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The dress was silky chiffon and satin, the high neck adorned with a collar of Guipure lace, and a long line of tiny mother-of-pearl buttons running down the back. It had rested in a large, dusty box for years and years, and one might have expected moths to have feasted on this creation, but although the fabric had yellowed a little, it was intact.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">It wasn’t ugly. That wasn’t what repulsed her. But it seemed to her it represented the youthful fancies of another girl, of a dead girl. Perhaps two girls. Had Virgil’s first wife worn this too?</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">It reminded her of an abandoned snake’s skin. Howard would slough off his own skin, would sink into a new body, like a blade entering warm flesh. Ouroboros.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You must try it on so the alterations can be made,” Flor- ence said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I have nice dresses. My purple taffeta—”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Florence stood very straight, her chin slightly raised, her hands clasped beneath her bosom. “The lace at the collar, you see it? That was taken from an older dress, incorporated in the final design. And the buttons, they came from another dress too. Your children will reuse this dress. It is the way things are done.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Leaning down carefully, Noemi noticed there was a tear on the waist and a couple of small holes on the bodice. The dress’s perfection was deceiving.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She grabbed the dress and ventured into the bathroom, changing there, and when she emerged Florence regarded her with a critical eye. Measurements were taken, alterations in- dicated with the required pins; tuck this, tuck that. Florence muttered a few words to Mary, and the maid opened another</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_217" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 217 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_183401_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">dusty box, producing a pair of shoes and a veil. The veil was in a much sorrier state than the dress. It had aged to a creamy ivory color, and the lovely flower-and-scroll design running near the edges had been marred by ugly mildew stains. The shoes were also hopeless, and besides, they were a size too big.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“It will do,” Florence said. “As shall you,” she added deri- sively.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“If you find me displeasing, maybe you could kindly ask your uncle to stop this wedding.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You silly creature. You think he’d desist? His appetite has been whetted,” she said, touching a lock of Noemi’s hair.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Virgil had touched her hair too, but the gesture had had a different meaning. Florence was inspecting her. “Fitness, he says. Germ plasm and the quality of the bloodstream.” She let go of Noemi’s hair and gave her a hard look. “It’s the com- mon lust of all men. He simply wants to have you, like a little butterfly in his collection. One more pretty girl.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Mary was quietly putting aside the veil, folding it as though it were a precious treasure and not a stained, wrecked bit of clothing.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“God knows what degenerative strain runs through your body. An outsider, a member of a disharmonic race,” Florence said, flinging the soiled shoes on the bed. “But we must accept it. He has spoken.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Et Verbum caro factum est,” Noemi said automatically, re- membering the phrase. He was lord and priest and father, and they were all his children and acolytes, blindly obeying him.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Well. At least you’re learning,” Florence replied, a slight smile on her face.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi did not reply, instead locking herself in the bath- room again and peeling off the dress. She changed back into her clothes and was glad when the women laid the dress back in its box and silently departed.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She put on the heavy sweater Francis had gifted her and reached into a pocket, clutching the lighter and the crumpled pack of cigarettes she’d taken to hiding there. Touching these objects made her feel more secure; they reminded her of home. With the mist outside obscuring the view, trapped between the walls of High Place, it seemed very easy to forget that she’d come from a different city and that she would ever see it again.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Francis came by a little while later. He brought with him a tray with her dinner and his razor wrapped in a handkerchief. Noemi joked that it was a terrible wedding present, and he chuckled. They sat side by side on the floor as she ate, the tray on her lap, and he managed a few more quips, and she smiled.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_218" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 218 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_183406_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">A distant, unpleasant groan dried up their mirth. The noise seemed to send a shiver down the house. It was followed by more groans, then silence. Noemi had heard such moaning be- fore, but it seemed especially acute tonight.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“The transmigration must take place soon,” Francis said, as if reading the question in her eyes. “His body is falling apart. It never healed right since that day when Ruth shot him; the damage was too awful.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Why did he never transmigrate before? When he was shot, then?” she asked.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“He couldn’t. There was no new body he could inhabit. He needs an adult body. The brain must grow up to a certain point. Twenty-four, twenty-five, that’s the point at which the transmigration may take place. Virgil was a baby. Florence was still a girl, and even if she’d been older, he would never transmigrate into a woman's body. So he held on, and his body stitched itself back together into a semblance of health.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“But he could have transmigrated as soon as Virgil turned twenty-four or twenty-five instead of staying an old man.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“It’s all connected. The house, the fungus running through it, the people. You hurt the family, you hurt the fungus. Ruth damaged the whole fabric of our existence. Howard wasn’t healing alone, everything was healing. But now he’s strong enough and he will die, his body will fruit, and he’ll begin a new cycle.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She thought of the house growing scar tissue, breathing slowly, blood flowing between the floorboards. It reminded her of one of her dreams, in which the walls palpitated.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“And that’s why I won't go with you,” Francis continued, fiddling with the cutlery, spinning a fork between his fingers and setting it down, ready to grab the tray and depart. “We’re all interconnected, and if I fled, they’d know, maybe even fol- low us and find us with ease.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“But you can’t stay here. What will they do to you?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Probably nothing. If they do, it won’t be your problem any- more.” He clutched the tray. “Let me take that and I’ll—”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You can’t be serious,” she said, snatching the tray away and laying it on the floor, shoving it aside.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He shrugged. “I’ve been gathering supplies for you. Cata- lina tried to run away, but she was ill prepared. Two oil lamps, a compass, a map, perhaps a pair of warm coats so that youcan walk to town without freezing. You have to think about your- self and your cousin. Not about me. I don’t really count. The fact of the matter is this is all the world I’ve ever known.”</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_219" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 219 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_183410_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Wood and glass and a roof do not constitute a world,” she countered. “You're not an orchid growing in a hothouse. I’m not letting you stay. Pack your prints or your favorite book or whatever you wish, you are coming with us.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You don’t belong here, Noemi. But I do. What would I do outside?” he asked.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Anything you want.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“But that is a deceiving idea. You are right to think that I was grown like an orchid. Carefully manufactured, carefully reared. I am, yes, like an orchid. Accustomed to a certain cli- mate, a certain amount of light and heat. I’ve been fashioned for a single end. A fish can’t breathe out of water. I belong with the family.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You're not an orchid or a fish.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“My father tried to escape, and you see how he fared,” he countered. “My mother and Virgil, they came back.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He laughed without joy, and she could very well believe he would stay behind, a cephalophore martyr of cold marble who'd let the dust accumulate on his shoulders, who'd allow the house to gently, slowly devour him.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You'll come with me.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“But—”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“But nothing! Don’t you want to leave this place?” she in- sisted.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">His shoulders were hunched, and he looked as if he would bolt out the door any second, but then he took a shaky breath.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“For God’s sake, you can’t be that blind?” he replied, his voice low and harrowed. “I want to follow you, wherever you may go. To the damn Antarctic, even if I’d freeze my toes off, who cares? But the tincture can sever your link between you and the house, not mine. I’ve lived too long with it. Ruth tried to find a way around it, tried to kill Howard to escape. That didn’t work. And my father’s gambit didn’t work either. There’s no solution.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">What he said madea terrible amount of sense. Yet she stub- bornly refused to concede. Was everyone in this house a moth caught in a killing jar and then pinned against a board?</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Listen,” she said. “Follow me. I’ll be your pied piper.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Those who follow the pied piper don’t meet a good end.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I forget which fairy tale it is,” Noemi said angrily. “But fol- low me all the same.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Noemi—”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She raised a hand and touched his face, her fingers gliding along his jaw.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_220" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 220 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_183415_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He looked at her, mutely, his lips moving though he uttered no words, gathering his courage. He reached out for her, pull- ing her closer to him ina gentle motion. His hand trailed down her back, palm pressed flat, and she rested her cheek against his chest.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The house was quiet, a quiet that she disliked, for it seemed to her all the boards that normally creaked and groaned had stopped creaking, the clocks on the walls did not tick, and even the rain against the window panes was shushed. It was as if an animal waited to pounce on them.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“They’re listening, aren’t they?” she whispered. They couldn’t understand them since they were speaking in Span- ish. Yet it still disturbed her.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Yes,” he said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He was scared too, she could tell. In the silence his heart beat loud against her ear. At length she lifted her head and looked at him, and he pressed his index finger to his lips, rising and stepping back from her. And she wondered if in addition to being able to listen, the house might not have eyes too.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The gloom, shivering and waiting, like a spider’s web, and them sitting on a silvery bit of silk. The lightest movement would reveal their presence and the spider would pounce on them. Such a dreadful thought, and yet she considered the possibility of entering that cold, foreign space willingly, which she’d never done before.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">It terrified her.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">But Ruth existed in the gloom, after all, and she wanted to speak to her again. She wasn’t sure how to accomplish that. After Francis left, Noemi lay in bed with her hands at her side, listening to her own breathing, and tried to visualize the young womans face as it looked in her portrait.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Eventually she dreamed. They were in the cemetery, she and Ruth, walking among the tombstones. The mist was thick around them, and Ruth carried a lantern, which glowed a sickly yellow. They paused before the entrance to the mauso- leum, and Ruth raised the lantern, and they both raised their heads to look at the statue of Agnes. The lantern could not provide sufficient illumination, and the statue remained half in shadow.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“This is our mother,” Ruth said. “She sleeps.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Not your mother, Noemi thought, for Agnes had died young, as had her child.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Our father is a monster who comes at night, creeping around this house. You can hear his footsteps outside the door,” Ruth said, and she raised the lantern higher, the light</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_221" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 221 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_183419_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">shifting the pattern of lights and shadow, obscuring the statue’s hands, her body, but revealing the face. Unseeing eyes and the lips pressed tightly together.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Your father can’t hurt you anymore,” Noemi said. Because at least there was that mercy, she supposed. Ghosts cannot be tortured.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">But the girl grimaced. “He can always hurt us. He never stops hurting us. He will never stop.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Ruth turned her lantern toward Noemi, making her squint and hold a hand up to shield her eyes. “Never, ever, never. I’ve seen you. I think I know you.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The conversation was fragmented, yet it remained more coherent than any other exchange Noemi had conducted with the girl before. In fact, it was the first time she had the im- pression she was speaking to an actual person rather than the faint carbon copy of one. But that’s what she was, wasn’t she? A faded carbon copy, the original manuscript long destroyed. One could not blame Ruth if she didn’t make much sense, if she muttered and lowered the lantern and raised it again re- peatedly like a wind-up doll.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Yes, you’ve seen me around the house,” Noemi said, still- ing Ruth with a gentle touch on the arm. “I need to ask youa question, and I hope you'll have an answer. How strong is the bond between the house and your family? Could a Doyle leave and never come back?” she asked, because she kept thinking about what Francis had told her.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Ruth tilted her head and looked at Noemi. “Father is power- ful. He knew something was wrong, sent Mother to stop me... and the others, the others too. I tried to keep my mind clear. I wrote my plan down, concentrated on my words.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The diary page. Like a mnemonic device? Was that the key to the gloom? Tricking it in such a way? Focus on commands and instructions and let them lead your steps?</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Ruth, could a Doyle ever leave this house?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Ruth had stopped listening to her; her eyes were glassy. Noemi stood directly in front of her.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You thought about running away, no? With Benito?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">And the young woman blinked and nodded. “Yes, I did,” she whispered. “Perhaps you could. I thought I could. But it’s a compulsion. It’s in the blood.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Like the cicadas Francis talked about. I’ll carry him out if I need to, she thought and her resolve grew firmer even if Ruth’s words had hardly been the solid reassurance she’d sought. There was at least a possibility he could be pulled from the grip of Howard Doyle and his noxious house.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_222" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 222 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_183424_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“It’s dark here, isn’t it?” Ruth said, looking up at the sky. There were no stars, no moon. Only mist and night. “Take this,” Ruth said and handed Noemi the lantern.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi grabbed it, her fingers curling around the metal handle. Ruth sat down at the foot of the statue, touching its feet and contemplating it. She lay next to the base of the statue, as if she were about to take anap ona bed made of mist and grass.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Remember to open your eyes,” Ruth told her.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Open your eyes,” Noemi whispered.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">When she did, turning her head toward the window, she saw that the sun was out. She was to be married that evening.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_223" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 223 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_183428_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">t occurred in reverse, the farce of a marriage. First the</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">banquet, then the ceremony.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">They gathered in the dining room, Francis and Noemi</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">sitting side by side, Florence and Catalina across from the groom and bride, and Virgil at the head of the table. Nei- ther Howard nor Dr. Cummins was present.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The servants had lit many candles, and dishes were piled upon the white damask tablecloth. Wildflowers were crammed into high turquoise glass vases. The plates and the cups that evening were silver, and though carefully polished, they looked very old, older than the silver Noemi had cleaned. They must have used these to feast some four hundred years ago. Perhaps even more. Treasure troves from their vault, care- fully placed in crates, just like the dark earth Howard had packed, so that they might reassemble the world where they’d reigned as masters.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">At Noemi’s right Francis sat dressed in his double-breasted gray frock coat suit, white waistcoat and dark gray necktie. She wondered if this outfit had belonged to Ruth’s groom, or whether it was a relic of another relative. In Noemi’s case, they had found a proper veil for her somewhere in a chest. It was a white tulle bandeau that covered her forehead, head combs and pins holding it in place.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi did not eat, and she drank only water; she did not speak, and neither did the others. The magic rule of silence had been reinstated, the susurrus of hands upon a napkin the only interruption. Noemi glanced in Catalina’s direction, and her cousin looked back at her.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The scene reminded her of a picture in one of her childhood fairy tale books, when the wedding banquet is in place and an evil fairy walks into the room. She recalled the table laden with meats and pies, the women wearing high headdresses, and the men in box coats with huge sleeves. She touched her silver cup and once again wondered at the age of it and</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_224" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 224 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_183433_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">whether Howard had been born three hundred, four hundred, five hundred years in the past and might have walked around in a jerkin and hose. She’d seen him in a dream, but the dream had been vague, or it had grown vaguer in the days since. How many times had he died, acquired a new body? She looked at Virgil, and he returned the look, raising his cup, which prompted Noemi to stare at her plate.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The clock marked the hour, and that was their cue. They rose. Francis took her hand and they walked together, up the stairs, a tiny wedding procession winding its way to Howard’s room. She’d known instinctively that this must be their des- tination, yet she still recoiled at the entrance and clutched Francis’s hand so hard she must have hurt him. He whispered in her ear.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“We're together,” he said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">They walked in. The air was foul with the stench of food gone sour, and Howard still lay on the bed, his lips black and covered with pustules, but this time he was under the covers and Dr. Cummins stood at his side. In a church there would have been the smell of incense. Here was the perfume of putre- faction.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">When Howard caught sight of Noemi, the old man grinned. “You look beautiful, my dear,” he said. “One of the prettiest brides I’ve had the chance to gaze upon.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She considered exactly how many that might be. Another pretty girl for his collection, Florence had said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Loyalty to the family is rewarded, and impertinence is punished. Remember that and you shall be very happy,” the old man continued. “And now, here, the two of you must be wed. Come.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Cummins stepped aside, and they took his place by the bed. Howard proceeded to speak in Latin. Noemi had no idea what he said, but at one point Francis knelt, and she knelt with him. This choreographed obeisance to the father had mean- ing. Repetition, Noemi thought. Tracing the same path over and over again. Circles.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Howard offered Francis a lacquered box, and the young man opened it. On plush velvet rested two tiny, dried pieces of yellow mushrooms.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You must eat,” Howard said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi held a tiny mushroom piece in her hand and Francis did the same. She was reluctant to place it in her mouth, lest it inhibit or reduce the progress of the tincture she’d been se- cretly imbibing, but more than that its provenance disturbed her. Had it been collected from the grounds near the house, or</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_225" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 225 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_183437_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">had it come from the cemetery, riddled with corpses? Or else had it grown upon Howard’s flesh and been plucked with nim- ble fingers, blood flowing when the stem was severed?</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Francis touched her wrist, motioning for her to feed him the mushroom, and then it was her turn, he placing the mush- room in her mouth. It seemed to her this was a strange parody of the communion wafer, and the thought of it almost made her giggle. She was so nervous.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She swallowed quickly. The mushroom had no taste, but the cup of wine that Francis pressed against her lips was sickly sweet, though she hardly had a sip. It was more the scent of it that assailed her nostrils, mixing with that other scent per- vading the room, the miasma of sickness and decay.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“May I kiss you?” Francis asked, and she nodded.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Francis leaned forward, and it was a delicate touch, barely there, like gossamer, before he stood up and gave her his hand so that she might rise with ease.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Let us instruct the young couple,” Howard said, “that they may be bountiful.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">They had exchanged only a handful of words through the wedding ceremony, and it was apparently all over. Virgil now motioned for Francis to follow him, while Florence took hold of Noemi and led her out of the room and into her own cham- ber. In Noemi’s absence one of the servants had decorated it. They’d placed flowers in more of those tall vases, left a bou- quet tied with an old ribbon on the bed, and lit many long candles. It was a parody of romanticism. The scent here was of misplaced spring, of flowers and wax.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“What instruction did he mean?” Noemi asked.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“The Doyle brides are proper girls, chaste and modest. What happens between a man and a woman is a great mystery to them.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi doubted that was the case. Howard had been a lecher, Virgil the same. Maybe they saved certain bits for last, but they did not deny themselves entirely.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I can name all body parts,” Noemi replied.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Then you'll do fine.” Florence raised her hands to help Noemi take off her veil, but she brushed the woman’s hand away even though she suddenly felt a bit unsteady, and the as- sistance might have proven useful.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I can manage alone. You can go.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Florence, hands clasped under her breast, stared at Noemi and walked out.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Thank God, Noemi thought.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_226" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 226 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_183442_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi ventured into the bathroom and looked in the mir- ror, removing pins and head combs and tossing the piece of tulle to the ground. The temperature had dropped. She walked back into the bedroom and put on the sweater she liked to wear. Her lighter was hard and cold against her fingers as she shoved her hands into her pockets.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She felt a little light-headed. Nothing unpleasant, nothing like what had happened the last time she’d been in Howard’s room. This was the buzz of alcohol, although she’d not had any wine, except for that one sip during the ceremony.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">In the corner of the room she noticed that same stain on the wallpaper that had scared her. It wasn’t moving now, but there were tiny golden points dancing at the edge of it. When she closed her eyes, however, it became obvious that the golden points were in her eyes, as if she’d stared at alight bulb.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She sat down on the bed, eyes still closed, and wondered where Francis was right now and what they were saying to him, and whether he also felt pinpricks running down his spine.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She had a vague impression of a different wedding, a differ- ent bride with a garland of pearls. On the morning of her wed- ding she’d received a silver wedding casket and inside there had been colored ribbons and jewels and a coral necklace. Howard’s hand on her own, the amber ring, and she did not wish for this but she must...Was this...was she Agnes or Alice? Noemi was un- sure. Alice, probably, because the girl thought of her sister.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Sister.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">This made Noemi remember Catalina, and she opened her eyes, staring up at the ceiling. She wished they might have had a word. A single word, to soothe both of their nerves.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi rubbed a hand against her mouth. It was consider- ably warmer in the room, where before it had felt like morning frost. She turned her head and saw Virgil standing next to the bed.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">For a second she thought she was mistaken, that it was Francis and she was seeing wrong or else it was the gloom, confusing her once more. After all, why would Virgil be in her room? But then Virgil grinned, and Francis would never smile at her like that. He was leering at her.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She jumped to her feet, intending to flee, but she stumbled, and he caught her in two quick movements, grabbing hold of her arm.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Noemi, here we are again,” he said.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_227" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 227 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_183446_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">His grip was firm, and she knew she couldn't fight him using physical force alone. She took a breath. “Where’s Fran- cis?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Busy being reprimanded. Did you think we wouldn’t find out?” Virgil asked, reaching into his pocket and showing her the glass vial with the tincture. “It wouldn’t have worked, any- way. How do you feel?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Drunk. Did you poison us?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He tucked the vial back in his pocket. “No. It was a little wedding gift, a little aphrodisiac. It’s a pity Francis won't be able to enjoy it.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She had a razor, she recalled. Hidden under the mattress. It would count for something. If she could get to it. But his hand was still on her arm with an iron grip, and when she tried to brush it away he wouldn’t allow it.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“T’m married to Francis.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“He’s not here.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“But your father—”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“He’s not here either. How funny, they’re all busy right now.” He tilted his head. “Francis is a little green boy of no ex- perience, but I know what I’m doing. I know what you want.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You don’t know anything,” she whispered.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You dream of me, you come looking for me as you dream,” he said. “Life bores you, Noemi. You like a hint of danger, but back home they wrap you in gauze, to keep you from breaking. But you'd like to break, wouldn’t you? You play with people and you wish someone would have the guts to play with you.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">It was not a real question, he awaited no answer, and his mouth covered hers. She bit him, but it was not in an attempt to deter his actions, and he knew it. He was right that she liked to play, that she enjoyed flirting and teasing and dancing, that they were so careful around her because she was a Taboada, and once in a while a coil of darkness wrapped itself around her heart and she wished to strike, like a cat.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">But even as she was admitting this, even as Noemi knew this was a part of her, she also knew it was not her.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She must have said that out loud without realizing, be- cause he chuckled.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Of course it’s you. I can nudge you, but it’s you.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“No.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“It’s me you want, me you fantasize about. We have an understanding, don’t we? We know each other, really know each other. Underneath the layers of decorum all you do is want.”</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_228" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 228 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_183451_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She slapped him. It accomplished nothing. There was the briefest pause, and he caught her face between his hands and turned her head, running his thumb along her neck. Lust, thick and heady, made her gasp in ruinous delight.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The mold in the corner of the room was shifting and blur- ring, and his fingers were clenching hard into her flesh, pull- ing her tighter against him. The mold was streaked with veins of gold, and he was trying to gather up her skirts, shoving her against the bed, touching her between her thighs. The motion made her panic.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Wait!” she said, as he pressed down on her, undaunted, impatient.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“No waiting, you tease.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“The dress!” He frowned, annoyed, but Noemi spoke again, hoping to buy time. “You'd better help me take off the dress.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">This seemed to improve his mood, and he gave her a ra- diant smile. She managed to stand up, and he peeled off her sweater, tossing it on the bed, and pushed her hair away from her nape as she furiously tried to think of a way out of—</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">In the corner of her eye the mold, with its streaks of gold, had spread across the wall and was dripping onto the floor. It refracted and changed, a pattern of triangles turning to dia- monds and then whorls. She nodded, feeling as if a great hand were pressed against her face, quietly smothering her.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She was never getting out of this house. To have considered it had been a folly. To have wanted to leave had been a mistake. And she wished to be a part of this, wanted to be one with the strange machinery and the veins and the muscles and the marrow of High Place. She wanted to be one with Virgil.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Want.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He had undone the top buttons on the back of the dress. She could have left long ago. Should have left in the beginning, when the first tingle of disquiet assailed her, but there had been a thrill to it, hadn’t there? A curse, maybe a haunting. She had even been excited to tell Francis about it. A haunting, a mystery to solve.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">All along, the sickly pull of this. And why not? Why not.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Why not. Want.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Her body, which had been cold, now felt too hot, and the mold dripped down, forming a black puddle in the corner. It reminded her of the black bile that Howard had spit down her throat, and that memory awoke a wave of disgust, her mouth tasting sour, and she thought of Catalina and Ruth and Agnes and the terrible things they’d done to them, which they’d now do to her.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_229" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 229 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_183456_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She turned around, away from the shimmering, changing mold, and shoved Virgil away with all her might. Virgil stum- bled against the chest at the foot of her bed and fell down. She immediately knelt by the bed and stretched an arm under the mattress, clumsy fingers grabbing the razor she’d hidden there.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi clutched the razor and looked at Virgil, who was sprawled on the floor. He’d hit his head, and his eyes were closed. Here was luck at last. Noemi breathed in slowly and leaned down next to his body, reaching into his pocket for the tincture. She found it, uncapped it, and drank a little from it, wiping her mouth with the back of her hand.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The effect was immediate and noticeable. She felt a wave of nausea, her hands trembled, and the flask slipped from her fingers and shattered on the floor. She held on to one of the bedposts and breathed quickly. My God. She thought she’d faint. She bit her hand hard to jolt herself into wakefulness. It worked.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The black puddles of mold that had accumulated on the floor were receding, and the fog in her mind was evaporating. Noemi put on her sweater, tucked the razor in one pocket and the lighter in another.</p>
