The young man turned his head and looked at Noemi with half- lidded eyes and managed to nod and point to the left. They went in that direction, him leaning on Noemi and Catalina, stumbling often. The gravestones rose like broken teeth from the earth, and he grunted, pointed another way. Noemi had no idea where they were headed. It could be they were walking in circles. And wouldn't that be ironic? Circles.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The mist gave them no quarter until, at last, she saw the iron gates of the cemetery rising in front of them, the serpent eating its tail greeting the trio. Catalina pushed the door open and they were on the path that led back to the house.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“The house is burning,” Francis said as they stood by the gates, catching their breath.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi realized this was the case. There was a distant glow, visible even through the mist. She couldn’t see High Place, but she could picture it. The ancient books in the library quickly catching fire, paper and leather burning fast, mahogany fur- niture and heavy curtains with tassels smoldering, glass cases filled with precious silver objects crackling, the nymph and her newel post shrouded in flames as bits of the ceiling fell at her feet. The fire, flowing up the staircase like a relentless river, making floorboards snap while the Doyles’ servants still stood on the steps, frozen.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Old paintings bubbling, faded photographs curling into nothingness, doorways arched with fire. Howard Doyle’s por- traits of his wives were consumed by flames and his bed now a bed of fire, and his decayed and heaving body choked by smoke, while on the floor his physician lay immobile and the fire began to lick at the bedcovers, began to eat Howard Doyle inch by inch, and the old man screamed, but there was no one who would assist him.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Invisible, beneath the paintings and the linens and plates and glass, she imagined masses of fine threads, delicate myce- lium, also burning and snapping, fueling the conflagration.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_254" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 254 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_183652_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The house blazed in the distance. Let it burn until it was all reduced to ashes.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Let’s go,” Noemi muttered. 4 mins left in book 89%</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_255" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 255 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_183657_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">e was asleep, the covers pulled up to his chin. It</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">was a small room with scarcely space for a chair</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">and a dresser, and she occupied that chair, right</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">by the bed. Atop the dresser sat a little figurine of San Judas Tadeo, and Noemi had found herself praying to it more than once, placing a cigarette before its feet as an offer- ing. She was staring at the figurine, her lips moving slowly, when the door opened and Catalina walked in. She wore a cot- ton nightgown that belonged to one of Dr. Camarillo’s friends and a thick brown shawl.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I came to see if you needed anything before I go to bed.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I’m fine.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You should go to bed too,” her cousin said, setting a hand on her shoulder. “You've hardly had any rest.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi patted her hand. “I don’t want him to wake up alone.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“It’s been two days.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I know,” Noemi said. “I wish it were like in the fairy tales you read to us. It was very easy in them: all you had to do was kiss the princess.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">They both looked at Francis, his face as pale as the pillow- case on which his head rested. Dr. Camarillo had tended to all of them. He’d seen to their wounds, given them a chance to clean themselves and change their clothes, prepared rooms for them to stay, called for Marta to bring her tincture when Noemi quietly explained they needed it. After imbibing it, they had all experienced headaches and nausea, which quickly eased. Except for Francis. Francis had drifted into a deep slum- ber from which he couldn’t be roused.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Tiring yourself won't help him,” Catalina said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi crossed her arms. “I know, I know.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Do you want me to keep you company?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I’m fine. I swear, I’ll go to bed soon. I also don’t really want to. I’m not tired.”</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_256" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 256 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_183701_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Catalina nodded. They were both quiet. Francis’s chest rose and fell steadily. If he was dreaming, the dreams were not un- pleasant. She almost felt sorry for wishing him awake.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The truth was she was afraid of going to bed, of what nightmares might uncoil in the dark. What did people do after witnessing the horrors they had seen? Was it possible to slip back into normality, to play pretend and go on? She wanted to think this was exactly the case, but she was afraid sleep would prove her wrong.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“The doctor says two police officers and a magistrate are arriving tomorrow from Pachuca and your father will be here too.” Catalina adjusted her shawl. “What will we say to them? I don’t think they’ll believe us.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Upon stumbling onto a pair of farmers with their donkeys behind them, the bloodied, bruised, and tired trio had not really agreed what tale they would tell, and the farmers were too shocked by the sight of them to ask much. Instead they quietly guided them to El Triunfo. Later, as they were ushered into Dr. Camarillo’s house, it had been necessary to fabricate a story, and Noemi had simplified their tale, saying that Virgil had gone mad and attempted to repeat his sister’s murderous actions, killing all the inhabitants of High Place, this time by setting the house on fire.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">This, however, did not explain why Noemi had been wear- ing an old wedding dress and Francis was in a matching wed- ding suit, nor why both women’s clothes were stained with so much blood.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi was pretty sure Camarillo didn’t believe their ver- sion of events, but he pretended to. In his weary eyes Noemi had read a tacit understanding.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“My father will help smooth things out.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I hope so,” Catalina said. “What if they should charge us? You know.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi doubted anyone could hold them in place; there wasn’t even a jail in El Triunfo. If anything, they’d be sent to Pachuca, but she didn’t think they’d do such a thing. State- ments would be taken, a cursory report would be typed up, but they couldn't really prove much.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Tomorrow we'll go home,” Noemi said firmly.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Catalina smiled, and Noemi, though tired, was glad to see that smile. It was the smile of the sweet young woman she’d grown up with. It was her Catalina.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Well, then, get some sleep,” Catalina said, leaning down to kiss her cheek. “They’1l be here early in the morning.”</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_257" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 257 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_183706_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The women hugged, one long, tight embrace, Noemi un- willing to cry. Not now. Then Catalina gently brushed the hair from her face and smiled again.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“T’m down the hall if you need me,” she said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Catalina took one last look at the young man and closed the door behind her.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Noemi placed her hand in the pocket of her sweater and felt the lighter there. Her lucky talisman. Finally she took out a crumpled pack of cigarettes that Camarillo had given her the previous day.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She lit the cigarette, tapped her foot, and let the ashes fall into an empty bowl. Her back ached. She had been sitting in that uncomfortable chair for a long time but refused to go away even though first Camarillo and then Catalina had come to poke at her. When she had taken but a few puffs of the cig- arette Francis stirred, and she dropped the cigarette onto the bowl and placed the bowl on the dresser, waiting.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He had moved like this before, a faint tilting of the head, but this time she thought it was different. She touched his hand.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Open your eyes,” she whispered. Ruth had said the same words to her many times, in fear and terror, but Noemi’s voice was warm.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She was rewarded by his eyes fluttering a little, then more, until they focused on her.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Hello there,” she said.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Hi.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Let me get you some water.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">There was a carafe on the dresser. She filled a glass and helped him drink.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You hungry?” she asked.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“God, no. Maybe later. I feel terrible.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You look terrible,” she replied.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">His lips formed a fragile smile, and he let out a chuckle. “Yes, I suppose so.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You slept two whole days. I thought I’d have to dislodge an apple from your throat, like a poor imitation of Sleeping Beauty.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Snow White.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Well. You look pale enough.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He smiled anew and attempted to position himself better against the headboard, his smile waning. “Is it all gone?” he asked, his voice a worried, anxious whisper.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“A couple of townspeople went up the mountain to see what was left of the house. They told us it was a bunch of</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_258" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 258 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_183710_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">smoldering ruins. High Place is gone, and the fungus must be gone with it.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Yes, I think it is. Although...mycelia can be pretty resist- ant to fire. And I’ve heard that certain mushrooms...like...like morels, they’ll sprout more easily after a forest fire.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Tt wasn’t a morel and it wasn’t a forest fire,” she said. “If there’s anything left we could find it and burn it.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I suppose we could.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The thought seemed to relax him; he had been clutching the covers quite fiercely and now he released them and sighed, his eyes settling on her.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“What happens tomorrow, then, when your father ar- rives?” he asked.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You sneaky man. Were you listening to us the whole time?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He seemed abashed and shook his head. “No. I suppose you woke me up or I was half awake already. Anyway, I heard your cousin say your father is arriving in the morning.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“That’s right. He’ll be here soon. I think you'll like him. And you're going to love Mexico City.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“T’m going with you?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“We can’t leave you here. Besides, I dragged you down a mountain. I think in cases such as this I’m supposed to guard you. There must be a law about that,” she said with that genial tone of hers. It had been a while since she had employed it. It felt stale and difficult to sound so carefree, it almost hurt her tongue, but she managed a smile, and he looked pleased.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She must practice, she thought. It was all practice. She’d learn to live without worry, without fears, without any dark- ness chasing after her.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Mexico City, then,” he said. “It’s very large.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You'll get the hang of it,” she replied, stifling a yawn with her injured hand.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">His eyes fixed on Noemi’s splinted fingers. “Does it hurt very badly?” he asked quietly.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“It hurts a little. No sonatas for me for a while. Maybe we can play a duet and you help me with the left hand.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Seriously, Noemi.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Seriously? Everything hurts. It’1l mend.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Maybe it wouldn’t, and maybe she’d never be able to coax notes out of a piano the same way she used to, maybe she would never be able to vanquish this experience, but she didn’t want to say that. There was no point in saying that.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I did hear your cousin telling you to sleep. It sounded like a good idea.”</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_259" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 259 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_183715_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Bah. Sleep is boring,” she proclaimed and fidgeted with her pack of cigarettes.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Do you have nightmares?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She shrugged and did not reply, tapping her index finger against the box.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I didn’t have nightmares about my mother. Maybe I will dream of her later,” Francis said. “But I did dream the house had stitched itself together and I was inside of it, and this time there was no way out. I was alone in the house, and all the doors were sealed.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She crushed the box. “It’s all gone. I told you, it’s all gone.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“It was grander than before. It was the house before it had fallen into disrepair; the colors were vivid and there were flowers growing in the greenhouse, but flowers also grew in- side, and there were forests of mushrooms up the staircase and in the rooms,” he said, his voice infinitely calm. “And when I walked, mushrooms sprouted from my footsteps.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Please, be quiet,” she said, and she wished he had dreamed of murder, of blood and viscera. This dream was much more disquieting.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She dropped her box of cigarettes. They both looked down at the ground, where it had landed between her chair and his bed.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“What if it’s never gone? What if it’s in me?” he asked, and there was a hitch in his voice.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I don’t know,” she said. They’d done everything they could. Burned all the mushrooms, destroyed the gloom, in- gested Marta’s tincture. It ought to be gone. Yet, in the blood.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He shook his head with a heavy exhalation. “If it’s in me, then I should put an end to it, and you shouldn't be so close to me, it’s not—”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“It was a dream.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Noemi—”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You're not listening.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“No! It was a dream. Dreams can’t hurt you.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Then why won’t you goto sleep?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I don’t want to, and it has nothing to do with this. Night- mares mean nothing.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He intended to protest, she pulled herself closer to him, settled on the bed and finally under the covers, bidding him to hush as she embraced him. She felt his hand ghosting against her hair, heard his heart stuttering and smoothing into a steady beat.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She looked up at him. Francis’s eyes were shiny with un- shed tears.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_260" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 260 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_183719_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I don’t want to be like him,” he whispered. “Maybe I'll die soon. Maybe you can cremate me.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You won't.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“You can’t promise that.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“We'll stay together,” she said firmly. “We'll stay together and you won't be alone. I can promise you that.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“How can you make such a promise?”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">She whispered that the city was wonderful and bright, and there were areas of it where buildings were rising up, fresh and new, places that had been open fields and held no secret histories. There were other cities too, where the sun could scorch out the land and bring color to his cheeks. They could live by the sea, in a building with large windows and no cur- tains.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Spinning fairy tales,” he murmured, but he embraced her.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Catalina was the one who made stories up. Tales of black mares with jeweled riders, princesses in towers, and Kublai Khan’s messengers. But he needed a story and she needed to tell one, so she did until he didn’t care whether she was lying or speaking the truth.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">He tightened his arms around her and buried his face inthe crook of her neck.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Eventually, she slept and did not dream. When she woke up to the half-light of the early morning, Francis turned his wan face toward Noemi and locked his blue eyes on her. She wondered whether one day, if she looked carefully, she might notice a golden sheen to them. Or maybe she’d catch her own reflection staring back at her with eyes of molten gold. The world might indeed be a cursed circle; the snake swallowed its tail and there could be no end, only an eternal ruination and endless devouring.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“I thought I dreamed you,” he said, still a little sleepy.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“T’m real,” she replied, a murmur.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">They were quiet. Slowly she leaned forward, kissing him on the mouth so he’d know she was truly there, and he sighed, intertwining his fingers with hers and closing his eyes.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The future, she thought, could not be predicted, and the shape of things could not be divined. To think otherwise was absurd. But they were young that morning, and they could cling to hope. Hope that the world could be remade, kinder and sweeter. So she kissed him a second time, for luck. When he looked at her again his face was filled with such an extraor- dinary gladness, and the third time she kissed him it was for love.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_261" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 261 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_183734_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">ACKNOWLEDGMENTS Thanks to my agent, Eddie Schneider; my editor, Tricia Narwani; and the Del Rey team. Thanks also to my mother for letting me watch scary movies and read scary books as a kid. And as always, thanks to my husband, who reads every single word I write.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_262" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 262 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_183739_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">rr * Ss ar BS ) ane eB PHOTO: © MARTIN DEE : Sitvia Moreno-Garcia is the New York Times bestselling author of the critically acclaimed speculative novels Gods of Jade and Shadow, Signal to Noise, Certain Dark Things, and The Beautiful Ones; and the crime novel Untamed Shore. She has edited sev- eral anthologies, including the World Fantasy Award-winning She Walks in Shadows (aka Cthulhu’s Daughters). She lives in Vancouver, British Columbia. silviamoreno-garcia.com Facebook.com/smorenogarcia Twitter: @silviamg</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_263" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 263 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_183744_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">MEXICAN GOTHIC SILVIA MORENO-GARCIA House Book Club A BOOK CLUB GUIDE</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_264" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 264 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_183749_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">A LETTER FROM THE AUTHOR Dear Readers,</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Mexico is peppered with mining ghost towns. The descrip- tion has a double meaning. On the one hand, it refers to an abandoned place. On the other hand, these towns carry with them the traces of colonial rule and all its excesses. They are filled with ghosts.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">When the Spaniards arrived in Mexico, they found a trove of mineral riches: gold and especially silver that could be mined in huge quantities. By the eighteenth century, Mexico was the world’s chief silver producer.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Bustling mines required workers, and cheap ones. The pre- dominant laborers in these towns were Indigenous people who were forced to work the mines through several systems— slavery, encomienda, and repartimiento were used at different points in time—and were often abused, toiling in bad condi- tions. Those who did not labor in the mines might still feel the brunt of Spanish rule, by being driven from their traditional lands.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The War of Independence of 1810 did not really ease the grasp of foreign powers on Mexico and its riches, nor did it distribute those riches more evenly. After the Spaniards, there came other powers to pry the earth for silver.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">British mining companies arrived in the early 1800s and established themselves throughout the country, including in the town of Real del Monte, high in the mountains of Hidalgo. Nicknamed “Little Cornwall,” Real del Monte boasts a unique British-inspired architecture and an English cemetery out of a horror film. It is this town, which I visited many years ago, that serves as the basis for the location of Mexican Gothic.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">It might sound a bit odd for a book titled Mexican Gothic to actually take place in a town that was modeled on a British town and exploited by British forces. Yet that is part of the ironic legacy of Latin America. I suppose I could have called the book Postcolonial Gothic, but that seems so much longer and less zesty. Mexican Gothic expresses the haunting feeling I</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_265" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 265 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_183754_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">had when we drove through Mexico looking at the remains of towns from another era.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">To know a place, you must look at the land. What the land told me in Hidalgo is that there are ghosts and then there are ghosts. The ones that wear bedsheets over their heads are much less terrifying than the ones left by the sins of our an- cestors.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Mexican Gothic is a fun romp through a trove of Gothic tropes, including a dark and gloomy house, an alluring yet dangerous man, a family with secrets, and things that go bump in the night. But it’s also a story about those other ghosts: the ones that were left like a scar on the land.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Silvia Moreno-Garcia</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_266" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 266 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_183759_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">QUESTIONS FOR DISCUSSION</p>
      <h2 style="color:#2d3748; margin:1.5rem 0 0.75rem 0; font-size:1.4rem;">1. How did you feel about the historical period and setting in which the book takes place?</h2>
      <h2 style="color:#2d3748; margin:1.5rem 0 0.75rem 0; font-size:1.4rem;">2. What did you think of the main character, Noemi?</h2>
      <h2 style="color:#2d3748; margin:1.5rem 0 0.75rem 0; font-size:1.4rem;">3. Who was your favorite character and why?</h2>
      <h2 style="color:#2d3748; margin:1.5rem 0 0.75rem 0; font-size:1.4rem;">4. How did you feel about the relationship between Noemi and Francis?</h2>
      <h2 style="color:#2d3748; margin:1.5rem 0 0.75rem 0; font-size:1.4rem;">5. What did you think about the ending? What do you wish had been different?</h2>
      <h2 style="color:#2d3748; margin:1.5rem 0 0.75rem 0; font-size:1.4rem;">6. What do you think the author’s purpose was in writing this book? How well do you think she got it across?</h2>
      <h2 style="color:#2d3748; margin:1.5rem 0 0.75rem 0; font-size:1.4rem;">7. What did you enjoy most about this book? What did you enjoy the least? What changes to the story, if any, would you make?</h2>
      <h2 style="color:#2d3748; margin:1.5rem 0 0.75rem 0; font-size:1.4rem;">8. Would you read a sequel to this story? Would you prefer it feature Noemi and Francis, or would you prefer a story in the same world with new characters?</h2>
      <h2 style="color:#2d3748; margin:1.5rem 0 0.75rem 0; font-size:1.4rem;">9. If you were interviewing the author, what questions would you have for her?</h2>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_267" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 267 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_183803_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">AN INTERVIEW WITH SILVIA MORENO-GARCIA</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Q: What is a Gothic novel?</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">A: Romantic fiction with a prevailing atmosphere of suspense or terror. “Romantic” means it derives from the Romantic artistic movement, not that it must be a love story. Its emphasis on melodrama and big feelings is what marks the Gothic. Nothing is small or muted. Gothic novels encom- pass a wide spectrum of works and have recurring tropes that make them identifiable to readers (dark castles, vir- ginal heroines, Byronic men, etc.).</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Gothic fiction has been classified as Female Gothic or Male Gothic. Female Gothic does not have supernatural elements, and the emphasis is on the heroine achieving a happy outcome and establishing a relationship with the hero. Jane Eyre is a good example of this. Male Gothic has supernatural elements, takes place in an uncaring universe, and features explicit elements such as graphic violence or rape. The Monk is a good example of this type of novel.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Another way to classify Gothic fiction is by dividing it into Gothic Horror and Gothic Romance. The New York Public Library has a good overview of both categories: https://www.nypl.org/blog/2018/10/18/brief-history- gothic-horror</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">To make things more complicated, you can divide Gothic novels by time periods, such as Early Gothic, Vic- torian Gothic, and New Gothic Romance. The last is the one most people are familiar with. During the 1960s and 1970s, anew crop of Gothic novels was marketed in paper- back. These novels carried a distinctive iconography: the young woman running away from a creepy house at night. That’s what we call New Gothic Romances. That’s quite a long history of a genre!</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_268" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 268 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_183808_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Q: What are some of the medical and scientific issues present in the book?</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">A: Eugenics is a big one. Eugenics was aterm coined by Francis Galton. It refers to the idea that humans can be selectively improved by mating specific desirable hereditary traits and preventing other traits from being passed on. Eugenics was intimately concerned with issues of race. In places such as England and the United States, there were fears of race mixing, which are evident in books such as Race Crossing in Jamaica, by Charles Davenport and Morris Steggerda. In Mexico and other parts of Latin America, eugenics advo- cates were also concerned with race, but due to its highly mixed population these concerns took a different tone. Eu- genics brings together issues of science, medicine, and the social sphere. It inspired anti-immigration legislation and forced sterilization practices, among other things.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Women’s health care has a part in the story: Hys- teria wasn’t declassified as a mental health disorder by the American Psychiatric Association until the 1950s. The number of women physicians was also very small in that time period, and male doctors might not have taken women’s concerns seriously.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Tuberculosis and the rest cure are mentioned. The rest cure was a popular cure for ailments, including tubercu- losis, for many decades, until the development of anti- biotic treatments—streptomycin was the first antibiotic that could kill the TB-causing bacterium. The first random- ized trial of streptomycin against tuberculosis was carried out in 1946 through 1948. Today, four drugs are normally used to treat tuberculosis: isoniazid (1951), pyrazinamide (1952), ethambutol (1961), and rifampin (1966).</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The rest cure would have still been in use in 1950. Pa- tients were to stay in bed and isolated, eat hearty meals, and refrain from creative or intellectual activity or stimula- tion. Visits were limited or prohibited. As a result “isolation and boredom were therefore two of the biggest challenges faced by TB patients.” (Source: https://www.heritage.nf.ca/ articles/society/tuberculosis-experience.php)</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Finally, there is a curandera in town who makes rem- edies for the people in the town. Curanderas or traditional healers are part and parcel of Mexican culture.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Q: What are some of the sources of inspiration for this novel?</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_269" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 269 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_183812_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">A: A huge, vast number of books and stories. But to nar- row down some of the most important ones: “The Yellow Wallpaper” by Charlotte Perkins Gilman and “The Feather Pillow” by Horacio Quiroga weave the idea of women strug- gling with health issues together with horror. Incidentally, Gilman was a eugenics advocate.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Another source of inspiration was the compromised ge- nealogies and families used by Poe and Lovecraft in stories such as “The Fall of the House of Usher” and “The Case of Charles Dexter Ward.” There was spiritual and body pos- session in “Ligeia,” “Morella,” and “The Thing on the Door- step,” among others.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Finally, the botanical horrors of “Rappaccini’s Daughter” by Nathaniel Hawthorne helped ferment the idea of expos- ure to toxic plants, while “The Voice in the Night” by Wil- liam Hope Hodgson contained elements of fungal horror, body horror, and disease.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Q: What is the significance of the name Howard Doyle?</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">A: Howard is named after H. P. Lovecraft—the H stands for Howard—an incredibly popular Weird fiction writer. Doyle comes from Arthur Conan Doyle, considered a seminal source for the mystery and detective genre. Doyle had a keen interest in paranormal phenomena. Lovecraft was an avid science aficionado. Several of their stories contain ra- cist elements. It seemed appropriate to name my character after them.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Keen-eyed readers might notice that Arthur Conan Doyle was Scottish and the Doyles are probably from Corn- wall (since they moved to the fictional analogue of Real del Monte), which is nowhere near Scotland. In this I took some artistic license.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Q: What is the significance of the name Taboada?</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">A: The heroine of my novel is named after Carlos Enrique Taboada, a Mexican film director who released four Gothic films beginning in the 1960s. Sometimes leaning toward the giallo, other times toward the fairy tale, Taboada’s cin- ema is distinctive and unique. It does not resemble the campy El Santo films most people associate with Mexico,</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_270" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 270 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_183817_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">nor does it contain the exotic markers people expect of the country (Day of the Dead skulls, etc.). It showed me that a Latin American creator could have the freedom to imagine what they pleased.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Q: What’s up with the mushrooms?</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">A: They’re cool! There is something called “mycorrhizal net- works” which is basically a fungi communications system in forests. There is also something called a hub tree or “mother tree.” They are the central hubs for the network. Fungi are incredibly diverse, and I love looking at them. The transformations they go through are fascinating. Shaggy inkcaps start off looking white, but eventually turn black and secrete a black liquid, dissolving themselves. They’re edible in their initial stage. The common inkcap or tippler’s bane is perfectly edible, but if you consume it with alcohol it’s poisonous. And so on and so forth.</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_271" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 271 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_183824_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">THE REAL DEL MONTE</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">BY SILVIA MORENO-GARCIA One time I was asked during an interview whether it really rained in Mexico City as much as I said it did in one of my novels. It does rain, and in fact parts of the city might flood periodically. When people think of Mexico, they tend to pic- ture tumbleweeds and deserts. My interviewer, having little grasp of the varied features of the country, was one of those folks who pictured the whole nation as one hot desert, out of a Speedy Gonzales cartoon.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">My novel Mexican Gothic takes place in a chilly, fog- shrouded town at the top of a mountain, a town with a deep British influence. For some people, such a locale would seem incompatible with their idea of Mexico. But Mexican Gothic is inspired by a real town, complete with an English cemetery: Real del Monte.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Real del Monte, also called Mineral del Monte, is located in the mountains of Hidalgo. Humboldt called this region the Mexican Andes due to its mountain chains that cross each other, forming a series of steep peaks. The territory is uneven, puckered by deep ravines, and the valleys at the foot of the mountains have fertile soils. The predominant rocks in the re- gion include basalts, vestiges of huge lava currents. The Bas- altic Prisms of Santa Maria Regla, a series of tall, polygonal columns of basalt rock with twin waterfalls streaming down the rocks, are some of the most amazing examples of such rock formations.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Obsidian is abundant in Hidalgo. The pre-Hispanic people of the region mined the Sierra de las Navajas for this volcanic glass, which was used in weapon making. It’s also a region rich in silver.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">After the Spanish conquered Mexico, they quickly began exploiting its silver deposits. When Humboldt visited Guana- juato, one single mine there, the Valenciana, was the source of one fifth of all the silver production in the world. One reason the Spanish were able to make such huge profits on silver was the fact that they had access to cheap labor in the form of</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_272" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 272 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_183829_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">the Indigenous people of the area. Through outright slavery or various forms of forced labor, they mined the rich silver de- posits of the country until the War of Independence broke out. Toward the second half of the nineteenth century, mining companies from other parts of the world began to make them- selves at home in Mexico. That’s when the British arrived in Real del Monte, earning the area the nickname “Little Corn- wall.” You can clearly see their influence in the architecture of the town: the sloping roofs are a dead giveaway. It’s also obvious in the town’s English cemetery, which, thanks to its variety of trees and the cold of the mountainside, looks like something out of a Hammer horror film if you visit it at cer- tain times of the day when the mist clings to the tombstones, which are all oriented toward England (minus one). Originally published by CultureFly on June 30, 2020 (https:// culturefly.co.uk/silvia-moreno-garcia-on-the-real-life-town- that-inspired-mexican-gothic/)</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_273" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 273 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_183834_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">THE DOYLE FAMILY SEAL QS ORC EO? ILLUSTRATION: © SARA BARDI</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">“Noemi stood up, and as she did she noticed that there was a mosaic on the floor. Stepping back and looking around the room she realized it circled the table. It was another of the snake symbols. The ouroboros slowly de- vouring itself. The infinite, above us, and below, as Virgil had said.”</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_274" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 274 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_183838_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">THE GIRL IN THE MANSION BY SILVIA MORENO-GARCIA Whatever happened to that girl? You know the one I mean: long hair, old-fashioned dress, with a dark, looming house in the distance and a look of anxiety on her face. She’s most often running from said dark house.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">The girl from the Gothic novels.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">I’m talking about the mid-twentieth-century Gothic novels, not the original crop of Gothic books, like The Castle of Otranto or The Mysteries of Udolpho. No, it’s that newer wave of Gothics—termed Gothic romances—that were released in the 1960s in paperback form that I’m referring to. This was a cat- egory dominated by authors such as Victoria Holt and Phyllis A. Whitney, and their covers fixed in the minds of a couple of generations what “Gothic” meant.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Most of these mid-century gothics tended to adhere to a simple formula that contained a young woman, a big house, and a dangerous yet exciting man. Often the women were in subservient positions, working for the lord of the manor, or- phaned, or the like. The women encountered some mystery that needed solving and eventually found love with the dan- gerous-exciting man, who turned out to be misunderstood (rarely was he a criminal). Although the mystery and threats surrounding the heroine seemed to be of supernatural origin, there was usually a rational explanation.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">As Joanna Russ explains in her essay “Somebody’s Trying to Kill Me and I Think It’s My Husband: The Modern Gothic,” the 1960s Gothic romance ultimately resembled a crossbreed between Jane Eyre and Rebecca, and publishers such as Terry Carr believed the appeal of the books was that they featured “women who marry guys and then begin to discover their hus- bands are strangers...so there’s a simultaneous attraction/re- pulsion love/fear going on.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Whatever the plot variation, Gothic novels allowed for ex- citement, romance, and sublimated sexual desire, as well as providing the heroine with a certain level of agency: after all,</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_275" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 275 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_183843_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">she had to survive and solve the mystery, even if the killer was inside the house with her.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">This game of literary Scooby-Doo was profitable. Such was the demand for Gothic books that, in true pulp fashion, some- times one title would be reissued with a different cover and a new name.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Yet, by the end of the 1970s, the Gothic novel seemed to vanish from shelves. What happened? Tastes changed. Fans who had previously turned to these books now looked for the emergent, spicier romances such as The Flame and the Flower, and readers more inclined to chills were about to discover Ste- phen King and the joys of the 1980s horror boom.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">And so the genre died. Or did it? Some writers continued to write Gothic novels, even if these were less common than be- fore—V. C. Andrews was probably the only heavy-hitter in the 1980s mining this niche with Flowers in the Attic. But I believe that rather than disappear completely, what happened was that the impulses behind the Gothic novel mutated and even- tually gave birth to what we call the Domestic Noir.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">In The Contested Castle: Gothic Novels and the Subversion of Domestic Ideology, Kate Ferguson Ellis argues that Gothic literature presents the middle-class home as a paradox, a site that should feel safe but instead turns horrific. Julia Crouch has defined Domestic Noir as a genre that “takes place pri- marily in homes and workplaces, concerns itself largely (but not exclusively) with the female experience, is based around relationships and takes as its base a broadly feminist view that the domestic sphere is a challenging and sometimes danger- ous prospect for its inhabitants.”</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Domestic Noirs emphasize the female experience with their covers and titles. Peruse the shelves and you'll find that it’s a world of girls (Gone Girl, The Girl Before), wives (The Wife Between Us, The Silent Wife, My Husband’s Wife, The Perfect Wife), and the like. The covers of Gothic romances, with their ever-present women and manor, also hit a similar beat. No, the titles were not The Wife in the Tower—though I suppose that might have been a good choice—but the illustrations, promo- tional copy, and even the choice of author’s names worked to- gether to give the impression of a female space.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">In The Gothic Romance Wave: A Critical History of the Mass Market Novels, 1960-1993 Lori A. Paige states that although Gothic romances offered upright heroines, “below the surface of every story remained an undercurrent of self-conscious repression, vice and even depravity.” The same could be said of domestic noirs such as The Girl on the Train, where a hero-</p>
    </div>
    <div class="book-page" id="gothic_page_276" style="margin-bottom:2rem; padding-bottom:1.5rem; border-bottom:1px dashed #e2e8f0;">
      <div style="font-size:0.75rem; text-transform:uppercase; letter-spacing:1px; color:#718096; margin-bottom:0.75rem;">Page 276 &bull; Screenshot_20261018_183848_Kindle.jpg</div>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">ine attempts to conceal her alcoholism, and the woman she is fascinated with—a seemingly perfect woman—is involved in a torrid affair. The Couple Next Door are obviously hiding a se- cret, the doting husband of Before I Go to Sleep is not who he seems, and The Woman in the Window spies on her neighbors, the perfect family, which is not perfect after all.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Terry Carr said women liked reading Gothic romances be- cause they featured “a magnetic suitor or husband who may or may not be a lunatic and/or murderer,” a man who might frighten them and make them anxious. It sounds a bit like riding an emotional roller coaster. In Domestic Noirs heroines might still fear their husbands, but they also seem to be fright- ened of a wider variety of people including neighbors, friends, and even employees, the roller coaster taking them through numerous peaks and valleys of anxiety.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Unlike the Gothic romances, though, Domestic Noirs tend to be firmly rooted in the present and the urban—or suburban —experience. In that sense, they are realistic, while Gothic ro- mance sought an air of unreality thanks to their great big cas- tles, misty landscapes, and old-fashioned settings.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">I don’t think it’s a perfect straight line between the Gothic romances of old and the current boom of Domestic Noir, but they both reflect that eerie feeling that the callis coming from inside the house. And perhaps the phone has been ringing for along time.</p>
      <p style="margin-bottom:0.9rem; text-indent:1.5rem;">Originally published by CrimeReads on February 20, 2020 (https://crimereads.com/gothic-romance-domestic-noir/)</p>
    </div>
